\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Note that the left diagram is a weak pullback by composition. Therefore the outer perimeter of the right diagram is a weak pullback, and the right square is a pullback, so the left square is a weak pullback by Lemma
5.8
, as desired.
∎

The class of tangent submersions is closed to retracts in the arrow category and is conditionally
T
-stable under reindexing (if the
T
-pullback of a tangent submersion exists, it is a tangent submersion). This stability property leads to the following result:

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a tangent category that allows for reindexing of the class of tangent submersions
\mathcal{R}
. Then the class of tangent submersions is a display system.

Proof.

Any class of maps that is closed to reindexing is a tangent display system, and the class of submersions is closed to retracts in the arrow category.
∎

Corollary 5.12
.

The class of submersions in the category of smooth manifolds is a proper retractive display system.

Chapter 5
Differential bundles

Our principal aim in this thesis is to provide an abstract tangent-categorical axiomatization for Lie algebroids. To accomplish this, we must provide an axiomatization for Lie algebroids which is essentially algebraic (in the sense of
Freyd and Kelly 1972
). However, in the category of smooth manifolds, Lie algebroids are defined in terms of vector bundles and these are prima facie a highly non-algebraic notion.

In addition to algebraic axioms which make it an
\mathbb{R}
-module in the slice over its base
M
, a vector bundle
q:E\to M
satisfies a crucial topological requirement: it must be locally trivial. This means that the projection
q:E\to M
must be locally isomorphic to a projection
\pi_{0}:U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to U
for some open subset
U
of
M
and natural number
n
. It is this property that permits calculations using local coordinates, an approach deeply enshrined in the culture of differential geometry.

Cockett and Cruttwell 2017
introduced the algebraic notion of a differential bundle. Evidence that differential bundles are the appropriate generalization of vector bundles was provided by showing how classical results for vector bundles could be generalized to differential bundles in any tangent category
Cockett and Cruttwell 2017
;
Cockett and Cruttwell 2018
. However, the precise relationship in the category of smooth manifolds between vector bundles and differential bundles was left open. The main result of this chapter (see
MacAdam 2021
) is that vector bundles and differential bundles coincide in the category of smooth manifolds.

The axiomatization of differential bundles focuses on another important property of vector bundles: given a vector bundle
q:E\to M
and a vector
v
in the fibre
E_{x}
above
x\in M
, the tangent space
T_{v}(E_{x})
can be naturally identified with
E_{x}
. This gives a lift map
\lambda:E\to TE
which can be axiomatized. While the lift map had long been noted in the differential geometry literature in the guise of the Euler vector field (see 6.11 of
Kolár et al. 1993
and also Section 1 of
Michor 1996
which explicitly uses the term ”lift”), it had not been adopted as the basis of an abstract axiomatization.

More recently, in the differential geometry literature,
Grabowski and Rotkiewicz 2009
and
Bursztyn et al. 2016
realized that the multiplicative
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action on the total space
E
determines the vector bundle structure of
q:E\to M
, and conversely such a multiplicative action determines a vector bundle precisely when its Euler vector field (Definition
1.8
) satisfies an additional “non-singular” property. This chapter extends these more recent observations on vector bundles to differential bundles.

The chapter begins by reviewing vector bundles, describing the Euler vector field construction that sends a vector bundle
q:E\to M
to a ”lift” map
\lambda:E\to TE
from
Grabowski and Rotkiewicz 2009
, whereas the rest of the chapter contains new results developed in collaboration with Matthew Burke. The second section establishes that these lifts are associative coalgebras for the weak comonad
(T,\ell)
, and that there is a fully faithful functor from vector bundles into the category of lifts for smooth manifolds. The third section identifies the universal property satisfied by the lift (or equivalently Euler vector field), while the fourth section shows that a non-singular lift corresponds precisely to a differential bundle. The fifth section proves the main theorem of the chapter: vector bundles are precisely differential bundles for smooth manifolds. The final section contains some remarks on extending affine connections to arbitrary differential bundles, which will be useful in Chapter
6
.

\pagenote

Each chapter has been given a new introduction.

1
Vector bundles

A vector bundle over a manifold
M
axiomatizes the notion of a smoothly varying family of vector spaces indexed by the points
m\in M
.
The driving example is that of the tangent bundle over a smooth manifold
M
, where the fibre above each point
m\in M
is the tangent space
T_{m}M
.
The manifold structure guarantees that the projection is
locally trivial
: given a chart
U\hookrightarrow M
, the next pullback splits as a product:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The local triviality of the tangent bundle is essential for various constructions and is part of the definition of a vector bundle.

Definition 1.1
.

A
vector bundle
is a tuple

(q:E\to M,\xi:M\to E,+:E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E\to E,\cdot:\mathbb{R}\times E\to E)

of morphisms in
\mathsf{SMan}
so that

(i)

the tuple
(q,\xi,+,\cdot)
defines an
\mathbb{R}
-module in
\mathsf{SMan}/M
;

(ii)

the map
q:E\to M
is locally trivial.

The fibred
\mathbb{R}
-module structure means
E
is a family of vector spaces indexed by
M
,
\{E_{m}|m\in M\}
.

It is important to note that the local triviality axiom guarantees that the projection of a vector bundle is a submersion (Definition
5.1
); thus pullback powers of
q:E\to M
exist and are preserved by the tangent functor.
\pagenote
This addresses a concern Kristine had raised about the preservation of
E_{2}
by
T
.

Example 1.2
.

Consider the cylinder, defined as the subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
spanned by
C=\{(x,y,z)|x^{2}+y^{2}=1,z\in\mathbb{R}\}
:

\mathbb{R}

Above each point
i\in S^{1}=\{(x,y)|x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
the fibre over
i
is
\mathbb{R}
. For each point
i
, we can choose a sufficiently small
\epsilon
and take the open set

U_{i}=\{(x,y)\in S^{1}|(i_{x}-x)^{2}+(i_{y}-y)^{2}\leq\epsilon\},

which may be flattened to
(-(1+\epsilon),1+\epsilon)\times\mathbb{R}
.

\pagenote

Carried forward the change in chapter 1 re:notation for
\chi(M)
, then defined the
C^{\infty}
module structure for the sections of a vector bundle projection (this is a more appropriate place to introduce this notation than the Lie algebroids section)
The sections of a vector bundle also give rise to a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module, generalizing that aspect of the tangent bundle’s fibred
\mathbb{R}
-module structure.

Lemma 1.3
.

Given a vector bundle
q:E\to M
, write the set of sections of
q
as
\Gamma(q)
; as, for example,
\Gamma(p.M)=\chi(M)
(recall the notation from Definition
2.16
). The set
\Gamma(q)
has a
C^{\infty}(M)
-module structure in much the same way as
\chi(M)
:

X+_{\Gamma(q)}Y:=+\circ(X,Y),\hskip 7.11317pt0_{\Gamma(q)}:=\xi,\hskip 7.11317pt(f\cdot_{\Gamma(q)}X)(m):=f(m)\cdot X(m)

There are also a variety of general constructions that yield vector bundles.

Example 1.4
.

(i)

The tangent bundle is a vector bundle: the construction in
Section

2
makes it clear that the projection
p:TM\to M
is a locally trivial, fibred
\mathbb{R}
-module over the base space
M
.

(ii)

A
trivial
vector bundle over
M
with fibres in
V
is the product
M\times V
. In particular, every vector space is a trivial vector bundle above the one-point space
\{*\}
.

(iii)

Each
T_{k}M
will be locally trivial; locally it looks like the
k
-fold product of the tangent space
p_{k}^{-1}(U)\cong U\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{k}
for an
n
-dimensional manifold
M
. More generally, one can take the fibrewise pullback
E_{k}=E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!\dots{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E
and discover a vector bundle over
M
.

(iv)

The
cotangent bundle
of
M
,
T^{*}M
, has the
dual
vector space of
T_{m}M
above each point
M
:
T^{*}_{m}(M)=(T_{m}M)^{*}
. This space can be appropriately topologized to be smooth, and a set of sections of
\Gamma(T^{*}M)
is isomorphic to the set of morphisms
TM\to\mathbb{R}
that are linear in each fibre. This construction may be applied to any vector bundle and is called the dual vector bundle.

(v)

Consider the space
\Lambda^{n}(E)
, the alternating tensor product of
E^{*}
. The set of sections of this vector bundle is equivalent to the alternating
n
-linear morphism
E_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
; when restricted to the tangent bundle, this is the space of differential
n
-forms.

There are two constructions on vector bundles that will be necessary to prove the main theorem of this section.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,+_{q},\cdot_{q})
be a vector bundle.

(i)

For any map
f:N\to M
, the
T
-reindexing of
q
by
f
is a vector bundle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(1)

(ii)

Any retract of
q
in the space of arrows is a vector bundle; that is, given

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

(2)

if there is a vector bundle structure on
q
, then there is a vector bundle structure on
\pi
.

The category of vector bundles has “locally linear” bundle morphisms as its maps.

Definition 1.6
.

A
morphism of vector bundles
between
q:E\to M
and
\pi:F\to N
is a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

that is
fibrewise linear
, so that above each fibre

f|_{m}:E_{m}\to F_{v(m)}

is a linear morphism of vector spaces. This may equivalently be stated as a morphism of fibred
\mathbb{R}
-modules, so that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}\times F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Example 1.7
.

(i)

For the pullback vector bundle in Diagram
1
, the pair
(\bar{f},f)
is a linear bundle morphism.

(ii)

For the section/retract vector bundle structure from Diagram
2
, the section and retract are linear morphisms.
Note that this is exactly the splitting of a linear idempotent on
q:E\to M
.

The lift on the tangent bundle was defined in Section
2
as

[\gamma]_{\sim}\mapsto[\gamma\circ\cdot_{\mathbb{R}}]_{\sim}.

Instead, consider the action of
\mathbb{R}
on a tangent vector:

([\gamma]_{\sim},r)\mapsto[\gamma\circ(r\cdot x)]_{\sim}.

Note that
T.\cdot
gives the equation

T.\cdot\circ([\omega\circ(x,y)],[(a,b)\mapsto a+b\cdot x])=(\omega\circ(a\cdot x,a\cdot b\cdot y));

so the lift map
\ell
can be rederived as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TM\times\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(TM\times\mathbb{R})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\gamma]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([\gamma],1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{([\gamma\circ\pi_{0}],[r\mapsto 1\bullet r])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\gamma\circ\bullet_{\mathbb{R}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,1^{\mathbb{R}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\times\lambda}

This general construction is known as the
Euler vector field
of a multiplicative action by
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Definition 1.8
.

Consider a multiplicative monoid action
a:\mathbb{R}^{+}\times E\to E
. The
Euler vector field
1
1

1

Somewhat confusingly, the Euler vector field is almost never a vector field.
of the action is the morphism
\lambda:E\to TE
constructed as follows:

\lambda:=E\xrightarrow{(id,1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)}E\times\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{0\times\lambda}TE\times T\mathbb{R}\cong T(E\times\mathbb{R})\xrightarrow{T.a}TE.

\pagenote

This definition has been tidied up so that is clear that
\lambda
is defined by the diagram in the definition.

Local triviality for the tangent bundle is encoded by the universality of the vertical lift condition. A similar universality condition holds for vector bundles.

Proposition 1.9
.

Let
q:E\to M
be a vector bundle with corresponding Euler vector field
\lambda
.
Then the following diagram is a
T
-pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times TM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,T.q)}

Exploiting the fact that fibred
\mathbb{R}
-modules have subtraction, the following result holds.

Corollary 1.10
.

The following two diagrams are T-equalizers:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{E}:=0\circ\pi_{0}+_{Tq}\lambda\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{E}:=T.\xi\circ\pi_{0}+_{p}\lambda\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q\circ 0\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ T.\xi\circ T.q}

(recall that
E_{2}
is the pullback of a submersion along a submersion and is therefore guaranteed to exist and be preserved by the tangent functor).

Proof.

Given
v:X\to TE
so that

T.q\circ v=T.q\circ 0\circ p\circ v

then

p\circ(v-_{T.q}0\circ p\circ v)=p\circ v-_{q}p\circ v=\xi\circ q\circ v.

So there is a unique
v^{\prime}
so that

\lambda\circ v^{\prime}=(v-_{T.q}0\circ p\circ v)

meaning that

v=0\circ p\circ v+_{T.q}\lambda\circ v^{\prime}=\mu(0\circ p\circ v,v^{\prime})

as required. The projection
q
is a submersion, so the pullback
E_{2}
is preserved by the tangent functor, as is the pullback in Proposition
1.9
, and the same calculation may be applied for each
T^{n}
. The proof for
\nu
follows by the same argument.
∎

Recall that the class of submersions forms a retractive display system in the category of smooth manifolds (Definition
5.3
), so they are stable under reindexing and closed to retracts.
\pagenote

I had originally used the fact that the pullback
E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E
was preserved without explaining why it was preserved, so I have added a reference to the section on submersions and pointed out that vector bundle projections are submersions (as they are local projections).

We may now infer the following:

Corollary 1.11
.

The projection for a vector bundle is a submersion.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\pi:TE\to E
is a submersion, so that
q\circ\pi_{0}:E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM\to M
is a submersion, so the map
q:E\to M
is a retract of the projection
p.E:TE\to E
in the arrow category.
∎

Preservation of the Euler vector field is also sufficient to guarantee that a morphism
f:E\to F
determines a vector bundle morphism.

Proposition 1.12
.

Let
q:E\to M,\pi:F\to N
be a pair of vector bundles with Euler vector fields
\lambda^{E},\lambda^{F}
.
Then a bundle morphism
(f,v):q\to\pi
is a vector bundle morphism if and only if

\lambda^{F}\circ f=T.f\circ\lambda^{E}

Proof.

Note that the
\nu^{F}
map from Corollary
1.10
is monic, and if
f
preserves the lift, it preserves
\nu
:

\nu^{F}\circ(T.v,f)=+\circ(T.\zeta\circ T.v,\lambda^{F}\circ f)=+\circ(T.f\circ T.xi,T.f\circ\lambda^{E})=T.f\circ\nu^{E}.

Next, observe that
T.v\times f
is the unique map making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TN{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Now
\nu
is a vector bundle morphism and monic, and
T.f
is a vector bundle morphism, so it follows that
T.v\times f
is a vector bundle morphism and hence
f
is also a vector bundle morphism. The reverse implication is immediate.
∎

2
Lifts for the tangent weak comonad

The lift
\ell:T\Rightarrow T^{2}
gives rise to a
weak comonad
.
Weak comonads were introduced in
Wisbauer 2013
and have a natural notion of an associative algebra
2
2

2

This is not
strictly
true. Wisbauer has a more nuanced hierarchy of almost-monads, and in his language
(T,\ell)
would be an endofunctor with an associative product.

. An associative coalgebra of the weak comonad
(T,\ell)
is called a
lift
; this chapter will demonstrate that lifts provide the essential structure necessary to formulate vector bundles.
\pagenote

Included some material on weak comonads to clear up confusion, as the map
p
does have the right type for a counit it should be noted that it is not a counit in general.

Definition 2.1
.

A weak comonad on a category
\mathbb{C}
is an endofunctor
S:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
equipped with a coassociative map
\delta:S\Rightarrow S.S
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.S.S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta.S}

An
associative algebra
of a weak comonad is an object
E
equipped with a map
\lambda:E\to SE
so that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S.S.E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.\lambda}

A morphism of these algebras is a map
f:(E,\lambda)\Rightarrow(D,\gamma)
so that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

Recall that for a full (co)monad, there is an adjunction between the base category and the category of (co)algebras. That result is weakened in this case:

Lemma 2.2
.

\mathbb{C}
, there is a free coalgebra functor

F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathsf{CoAlg}(\mathbb{C});E\mapsto(S.E,\delta:S.E\to S.S.E)

an underlying object functor

U:\mathsf{CoAlg}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C};(E,\lambda)\mapsto E

and a natural transformation

Definition 2.3
.

A
lift
in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is an associative coalgebra of
(T,\ell)
, namely, a pair
(E,\lambda:E\to TE)
so that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\lambda}

A morphism of lifts is a coalgebra morphism. The category of lifts and lift morphisms in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is written
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
.

Note that the tangent bundle is
not
, in general, a comonad: while the tangent projection has the correct type for a counit,
p:T\Rightarrow id
, it does not satisfy
p\circ\ell=id
. In fact, if
p
were a counit, this would force
id=p\circ\ell=0\circ p
so that
0
=
p^{-1}
, thus if
(T,\ell,p)
is a comonad then
T
is naturally isomorphic to the identity functor.

Example 2.4
.

(i)

For every object
M
in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, the pair
(TM,\ell:TM\to T^{2}M)
is a lift, called the
free lift
on
M
.

(ii)

Every object
M
in a tangent category has a
trivial
lift,
0:M\to TM
, where
T.0\circ 0=\ell\circ 0.

(iii)

Every differential object has a lift
\lambda
; the coherence is equivalent to axiom
[D0.3]
in Definition
4.1
.
\pagenote

I have added the map
\lambda:E\to TE
from the definition of a differential object, and the exact differential object axiom that makes it a lift.

(iv)

\pagenote

I moved the Euler vector field into the examples, fixed notation, and tried to clarify some points Kristine brought up. I make explicit use of the Kock-Lawvere axiom here, so that
T\mathbb{R}
is
R[x]/x^{2}
and
T^{2}\mathbb{R}
is
R[x]/(x^{2},y^{2})
.
The Euler vector field of a multiplicative
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action
h:R^{+}\times E\to E
in
\mathsf{SMan}
is a lift.
Recall that the Euler vector field over the scalar action
s_{M}:TM\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
induces the vertical lift on a manifold:

\ell=T.s_{M}\circ(0,\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!).

In the category of smooth manifolds,
T\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}
, where
\lambda(r)=[x\mapsto r\cdot x]
corresponds to the map
\lambda^{\prime}(r)=0+r\cdot x
. Similarly, there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})
so that for the maps
0.TandT.0
,

0.T(a+r\cdot x)\cong a+r\cdot x+0y+0xy,\hskip 14.22636ptT.0(a+r\cdot x)\cong a+0x+r\cdot y+0xy.

Since we know that
(0+x)(0+y)=(0+xy)
in
\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})
(following
4.2
), we can use these isomorphisms to see that

\ell\circ\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!=(0.T\circ\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)\cdot_{T^{2}.\mathbb{R}}(T.0\circ\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!).

Consider a monoid action
(\mathbb{R}^{+},h)
on a manifold
E
. The Euler vector field of this action,

\lambda:E\xrightarrow{(id,1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)}E\times\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{(0,\lambda^{\mathbb{R}})}TE\times T\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{T.h}TE

will define an algebra if the induced scalar action on
TE
commutes with the natural scalar action:

\displaystyle T.\lambda\circ\lambda

\displaystyle=T^{2}.h\circ(T.0\circ\lambda,T.\lambda\circ 0\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)

\displaystyle=T^{2}.h\circ(T.0\circ T.h\circ(0,\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!),0\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)

\displaystyle=T^{2}.h\circ(T^{2}.h\circ(T0\circ 0,T0\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!),0\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)

\displaystyle=T^{2}.h\circ(T.0\circ 0,(T.0\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)\cdot_{T^{2}.\mathbb{R}}(0.T\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!))

\displaystyle=T^{2}.h\circ(\ell\circ 0,\ell\circ\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)

\displaystyle=\ell\circ T.h\circ(0,\lambda\circ 1^{\mathbb{R}}\circ!)

\displaystyle=\ell\circ\lambda.

Observation 2.5
.

Recall that by Proposition
1.12
, morphisms preserve a monoid action if and only if they preserve the associated Euler vector field of the action. This means the Euler vector field construction gives a fully faithful functor from monoid actions to lifts in the category of smooth manifolds, and therefore from the category of vector bundles to the category of lifts in
\mathsf{SMan}
.

The following proposition gives a pair of constructions on lifts—closure under the tangent functor and finite
T
-limits—that will be useful in this section.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a tangent category.

(i)

The tangent functor lifts to an endofunctor on the category of lifts in
\mathbb{C}
.

(ii)

Given a diagram
\pagenote

I have clarified the statement and make the proof more concrete, I have also moved this result to a lemma and made the next result a proposition rather than corollary.

D:\mathcal{D}\to\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})

in the category of lifts of
\mathbb{C}
, if the
T
-limit of
U.D
exists in
\mathbb{C}
, then
\lim U.D
has a natural lift
\lambda^{\prime}
associated to it so that
(\lim U.D,\lambda^{\prime})
is the limit of
D
in
\mathsf{Lifts}(\mathbb{C})
. (That is,
T
-limits of lifts are computed pointwise in the base category.)

\pagenote

The proof for the

Proof.

(i)

Simply check that

\displaystyle T.(c\circ T.\lambda)\circ c\circ T.\lambda=T.c\circ T^{2}.\lambda\circ c\circ T.\lambda=T.c\circ c.T\circ T^{2}.\lambda

\displaystyle=T.c\circ c.T\circ T.\ell\circ T.\lambda=\ell.T\circ c\circ T.\lambda.

(ii)

Concretely, a tangent terminal object will have a lift:

(1,1\xrightarrow{0}T.1\cong 1).

Given
(E,\lambda)
and
(F,l)
, if the tangent product
E\times F
exists there is a lift

(E\times F,E\times F\xrightarrow{\lambda\times l}TE\times TF\cong T(E\times F)).

Given the
T
-equalizer of a fork
f,g:(E,\lambda)\to(F,l)
, the equalizer has a lift induced as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

∎

Proposition 2.7
.

The category of lifts is a tangent category.

Proof.

The tangent functor sends

T.f:(TE,c\circ T.\lambda)\to(TF,c\circ T.l)f:(E,\lambda)\to(F,l).

To see that this is still an algebra morphism, compute

c\circ T.l\circ T.f=c\circ T^{2}.f\circ T.\lambda=T^{2}.f\circ c\circ T.\lambda

The structure maps are the structure maps on the underlying object of the lift; the universality conditions follow by Proposition
2.6
.
∎

The following idempotent is key in the theory of lifts and will be used in defining non-singular lifts (Definition
3.1
), and its splitting will present the projection and zero-section of a vector bundle (Definition
4.1
).

Proposition 2.8
.

The category of lifts in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
has a natural idempotent:

e:id\Rightarrow id;e_{(E,\lambda)}:(E,\lambda)\xrightarrow{p\circ\lambda}(E,\lambda).

\pagenote

This proposition originally included some ambiguities, these have been handled by earlier changes that make the notiong of lift and lift-morphism more concrete.

Proof.

First, we see that
e=p\circ\lambda
is an idempotent:

p\circ\lambda\circ p\circ\lambda=p\circ p.T\circ T.\lambda\circ\lambda\\
=p\circ p.T\circ\ell\circ\lambda\\
=p\circ 0\circ p\circ\lambda\\
=p\circ\lambda.

Moreover, every
f:(E,\lambda)\to(F,l)
preserves the idempotent:

f\circ p\circ\lambda=p\circ T.f\circ\lambda=p\circ l\circ f.

Finally, note that the idempotent is a lift morphism.:

T.\lambda\circ\lambda=\ell\circ\lambda=c\circ\ell\circ\lambda=c\circ T.\lambda\circ\lambda

which implies that

\lambda\circ e=\lambda\circ p\circ\lambda=p\circ T.\lambda\circ\lambda=p\circ c\circ T.\lambda\circ\lambda=T.p\circ T.\lambda\circ\lambda=T.e\circ\lambda.

∎

3
Non-singular lifts

Grabowski and Rotkiewicz 2009
introduced the notion of a non-singular lift as a means to axiomatize the Euler vector field of a vector bundle’s multiplicative
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action. While it is not immediately clear that our definition is the same as Grabowski’s, the results of Section
5
will justify the use of this language as they are necessarily the same.

Definition 3.1
.

A lift
(E,\lambda)
in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is
non-singular
whenever the following diagram is a
T
-equalizer:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}

where
e.E=p\circ\ell
(the idempotent associated to the free lift on
E
) and
T.e=T.p\circ T.\lambda
(the image of the idempotent associated to
(E,\lambda)
under the tangent functor). The category of non-singular lifts is written
\mathsf{NonSing}(\mathbb{C})
.
\pagenote

I have added comments clarifying what maps
e.E
and
T.e
are.

The most prominent class of examples is given by the Euler vector field of the
\mathbb{R}
-action on a vector bundle.

Proposition 3.2
.

The Euler vector field of a vector bundle is a non-singular lift.

Proof.

\pagenote

I made it clear that the diagram being discussed is a
T
-limit, as it is not actually an equalizer diagram. I have tried to clarify the wording that “one diagram is universal if and only if the other is”.
Let
(q:E\to M,+,\xi,\cdot)
be a vector bundle with Euler vector field
\lambda
. By Proposition
1.9
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ T.\xi\circ T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q\circ 0\circ p}

is a
T
-limit. The
T
-universality of this diagram will hold if and only if the diagram is universal after each parallel pair of arrows is post-composed by a
T
-monic. A section is a
T
-monic, so the previous diagram is
T
-universal if and only if the following diagram is
T
-universal:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\circ q\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\circ q\circ p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\xi}

Now simplify this diagram using the fact that
T.(\xi\circ q)=T.e,0\circ p=e.E
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E\circ T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E\circ T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

Note that the the two pairs of parallel arrows have a common arrow, implying that they may be pulled together into a single ternary equalizer. All that remains to check, then, is that for any
x:X\to TE
,

(e.e\circ x=T.e\circ x=e.E\circ x)\iff(T.e\circ x=e.E\circ x).

The forward implication is trivial, so it remains to prove the reverse.
Suppose
T.e\circ x=e.E\circ x
; then

e.e\circ x=e.E\circ T.e\circ x=e.E\circ e.E\circ x=e.E\circ x

giving the result, namely that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}

is a
T
-equalizer.
∎

Every map
f:E\to F
gives a map of free coalgebras
T.f:(TE,\ell)\to(TF,\ell)
and the idempotent
e
is a coalgebra morphism by Proposition
2.8
, so the following is immediate:

Proposition 3.3
.

A non-singular lift is an equalizer in the category of lifts:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E,\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}

Observe that the category of non-singular lifts is closed under finite limits in the category of lifts.

Proposition 3.4
.

The category of non-singular lifts in
\mathbb{C}
is closed under
T
-limits:

(i)

The tangent functor on lifts preserves non-singular lifts, so that if
(E,\lambda)
is non-singular then
(TE,c\circ T.\lambda)
is non-singular.
\pagenote

I clarified that this was in fact referring to the tangent functor
for the category of lifts
.

(ii)

The trivial lift on an object,
0:M\to TM
, is non-singular.

(iii)

T
-products of non-singular lifts are non-singular lifts.

(iv)

T
-equalizers of non-singular lifts are non-singular lifts.

Proof.

(i)

This follows from the fact that the non-singularity condition is a
T
-limit.

(ii)

The zero map splits the idempotent
0\circ p
, so it is the equalizer of
0\circ p,p\circ 0=id
.

(iii)

This follows by stability of limits under products.

(iv)

The following diagram commutes by naturality:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}

Each horizontal diagram is a
T
-equalizer, and the two columns on the right are
T
-equalizers, so the column on the left is a
T
-equalizer.

∎

Finally, when a tangent category has certain
T
-equalizers, there is an idempotent monad on the category of lifts, whose algebras are non-singular lifts:

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a tangent category with chosen
T
-equalizers of idempotents. Then the following equalizer determines a left-exact idempotent monad on the category of lifts, whose algebras are non-singular lifts.
\pagenote

I have reworded this proposition to make it clear that the functor is defined by sending a lift to the lift defined by the equalizer in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F,l)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(T.E,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(T.E,\ell)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}

Proof.

First, take the equalizer in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
; the functor sends
(E,\lambda)
to the chosen limit
(F,l)
.The unit of the monad is the unique morphism from
(E,\lambda)
to the equalizer
(F,l)
induced by universality:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(F,l)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E,\lambda)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Note that non-singular lifts are closed under finite limits, so
(F,l)
is a non-singular lift. If
(E,\lambda)
is a nonsingular lift then
\lambda:(E,\lambda)\to(TE,\ell)
equalizes the diagram, so there is a unique isomorphism
(F,l)\cong(E,\lambda)
, making the multiplication of the monad a natural isomorphism (and thus yielding an idempotent monad).
The functor is defined as a
T
-limit and therefore preserves all
T
-limits of lifts, so it is left-exact.
∎

In the category of smooth manifolds,
\ell
is the Euler vector field of an
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action, this guarantees that every
\lambda
is the Euler vector field of a multiplicative
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action. We note the following corollary.

Corollary 3.6
.

In the category of multiplicative
\mathbb{R}^{+}
actions in
\mathsf{SMan}
, multiplication by
0
is equivalent to the natural idempotent
e
in the fully faithful functor sending an
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-action to its Euler vector field. By non-singularity, the following diagram is an equalizer, giving
E
a multiplicative action by
\mathbb{R}^{+}
whose Euler vector field is
\lambda
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E,\cdot_{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\cdot_{T})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TE,\cdot_{T})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

Moreover,
\lambda
is the Euler vector field of this lift, and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times TE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}^{+}\times TE}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times TE)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times TE)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times e.E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\mathbb{R}^{+}\times T.e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\cdot_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\cdot_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\mathbb{R}},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\mathbb{R}},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1^{\mathbb{R}},id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\times 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\times 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{\mathbb{R}}\times 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}\times e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}\times T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\lambda}

4
Differential bundles

This section introduces (pre-)differential bundles, which provided the rest of the data for a vector bundle: namely the projection, the zero section, and the addition map. The zero section and projection data arise by splitting the natural idempotent
e:id\Rightarrow id
, and non-singularity will induce the addition map. Every differential bundle satisfies a pair of universality diagrams, linking this presentation of differential bundles to the original definition in
Cockett and Cruttwell 2018
.

Definition 4.1
.

(i)

A
pre-differential bundle
is a lift
\lambda:E\to TE
equipped with a chosen splitting of the natural idempotent
e=p\circ\lambda
from Proposition
2.8
. Pre-differential bundles are formally written
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
, where
q:E\to M
is the retract,
\xi:M\to E
the section, and
\lambda:E\to TE
the lift (the types are only necessary for the projection as the rest may be inferred, and will generally be suppressed to save space).

(ii)

A
differential bundle
is a pre-differential bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
with the properties that
\lambda
is non-singular and
T
-pullback powers of
q
exist.

Morphisms of (pre-)differential bundles are exactly morphisms of their underlying lifts. The categories of (pre-)differential bundles are exactly (pre-)differential bundles and lift morphisms, and are written
\mathsf{Pre}(\mathbb{C}),\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})
respectively.
\pagenote

The definition of differential bundles has been expanded so that it is clear that
\xi,q
are and idempotent splitting of
p\circ\lambda
, and the category names
\mathsf{Pre}(\mathbb{C}),\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})
are defined explicitly to help draw attention to the definition of bundle morphisms.

Recall that as
e
is a natural idempotent in the category of lifts, any lift morphism will preserve
e
and will consequently preserve its idempotent splitting. Preserving the idempotent means that every differential bundle morphism is a bundle morphism, where the base map is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m:=\pi\circ f\circ\xi}

We now look at the limits of (pre-)differential bundles.

Observation 4.2
.

(i)

The limit for a diagram of pre-differential bundles is the limit of the underlying lifts equipped with a chosen splitting of
p\circ\lambda
due to basic properties about idempotent splittings.

(ii)

The limit for a diagram of differential bundles is the limit in the category of pre-differential bundles (the lift will be universal by Proposition
3.4
), so long as
T
-pullback powers of the resulting projection exist.

Because there is a projection associated to a (pre-)differential bundle, the
T
-reindexing operation described in Proposition
5.2
can now be applied. This gives a pullback differential bundle in a similar way to Proposition
1.5
.

Lemma 4.3

(
Cockett and Cruttwell 2018
)
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
be a (pre-) differential bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, and consider a
T
-pullback in
\mathbb{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then the induced triple maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.u^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u^{*}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\bar{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.u^{*}q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\circ u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}\xi}

induce a (pre-)differential bundle
(u^{*}q:u^{*}E\to N,u^{*}\xi,u^{*}\lambda)
. If
\lambda
is non-singular and
T
-pullback powers of
u^{*}q
exist, then
(u^{*}q,u^{*}\xi,u^{*}\lambda)
is a differential bundle.

Proof.

Note that a pre-differential bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
in
\mathbb{C}
may also be regarded as a pre-differential bundle
(q:(E,\lambda)\to(M,0),\xi,\lambda)
in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
. Take the following pullback in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(u^{*}E,u^{*}\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E,\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(N,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M,0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

It follows by construction that
\iota\circ u^{*}\lambda=\lambda\circ\iota
. Thus the result holds.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
be a non-singular pre-differential bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, so that
T
-pullback powers of
q
exist.
Then there is an additive bundle structure
(q,\xi,+_{q})
so that the differential bundle morphisms are additive:

(\lambda,\xi):(q,\xi,+_{q})\to(p,0,+)\hskip 14.22636pt(\lambda,0):(q,\xi,+_{q})\to(T.q,T.\xi,T.+_{q}).

Furthermore, every differential bundle morphism preserves addition.

Proof.

Non-singularity forces the existence of an addition map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!+_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\times\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e\times e).E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}.e}

Note that this diagram commutes because
T_{2}E
is a pre-differential bundle whose lift is
\ell\times\ell
, and also
+
is a linear morphism, so it commutes with the addition map
e\circ a+e\circ b=e\circ(a\times b)
. Post-composition with
\lambda
ensures that
\xi
is the unit and that associativity holds. A differential bundle morphism will induce a morphism of the equalizer diagrams that induce each addition map to preserve addition.
∎

Recall that by Proposition
3.2
, the Euler vector field for every vector bundle is a non-singular lift.
\pagenote

The reference to Proposition
3.2
has been put into the middle of the sentence to help. Also, rather than “addition induced by singularity”, we give an explicit reference to the above proposition that constructs the additive bundle structure.
If
TM{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E
exists, the map

\nu^{E}:TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!E\xrightarrow{T.\xi\times\lambda}T_{2}E\xrightarrow{+.E}TE

may be formed. Similarly, using the additive bundle structure from Proposition
4.4
, the
\mu
map may be formed:

\mu^{E}:E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E\xrightarrow{0\times\lambda}T(E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{q}\!E)\xrightarrow{T.+_{q}}TE

Note that any differential bundle morphism will preserve
\mu
and
\nu
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Differential bundle maps preserve
\mu(x,y):=0\circ x+_{T.q}\lambda\circ y
and
\nu(v,y):=T.\xi\circ v+_{p}\lambda\circ y
.
\pagenote

The word linear had crept into this sentence in the original draft, it has been correct to “differential bundle morphism”.

Proof.

The following diagram demonstrates that lift maps preserve
\nu
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.e}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times T.n}

Similarly, this diagram demonstrates that lift maps preserve

∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Consider the full subcategory of differential bundles in
\mathbb{C}
whose objects are differential bundles
(E,\lambda)
so that the forks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{E}:=0\circ\pi_{0}+_{Tq}\lambda\circ\pi_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ T.\xi\circ T.q}

(3)

are
T
-equalizers. This subcategory is closed under

-equalizers.
\pagenote

The original wording for this lemma did not properly quantify the objects over which the theorem was stated for.

Proof.

-equalizer of lifts:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}

, the equalizer

Theorem 4.7
.

For every differential bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
in a tangent category, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.q\circ 0\circ p}

is a
T
-equalizer, and if
TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!E
exists, then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{E}:=\lambda\circ\pi_{0}+_{p}T.\xi\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ T.\xi\circ T.q}

is a
T
-equalizer.

Proof.

The tangent bundle satisfies both universality conditions, and by Lemma
4.6
every differential bundle will satisfy these conditions by the non-singularity of the lift.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

In a complete tangent category, the category of differential bundles is precisely the category of algebras of the monad in Theorem
3.5
on pre-differential bundles.

Remark 4.9
.

The universality conditions in Theorem
4.7
demonstrate that this definition of differential bundle agrees with that of
Cockett and Cruttwell 2018
.
\pagenote
I have removed a reference to the published version of this chapter, it was unnecessary.

5
The isomorphism of categories

It is now straightforward to show that the main theorem of this chapter holds. First, observe that for every differential bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
in the category of smooth manifolds, the
T
-pullback
E{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!TM
exists
\pagenote
I have switched the order of the pullback for consistency
, as
p
is a submersion. Note that the universality of the two diagrams is equivalent:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{E}:=\lambda\circ\pi_{0}+_{p}T.\xi\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ T.\xi\circ T.q}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\circ\pi_{0}+_{p}T.\xi\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Thus the following holds.

Theorem 5.1
.

There is an isomorphism of categories between vector bundles and differential bundles in smooth manifolds.

Proof.

Note that Proposition
3.2
gives a fully faithful functor from the category of vector bundles to differential bundles of smooth manifolds, as the lift associated to the vector bundle is non-singular and the projection and zero section give the rest of the structure of a differential bundle: as remarked after Definition
1.1
, local triviality guarantees that the projection is a submersion, so pullback powers of the projection exist, yielding a differential bundle.

To see there is an isomorphism on objects, recall that every differential bundle is a fibred
\mathbb{R}
-module by Corollary
3.6
, and that this identification is a bijective mapping (it recovers the original
\mathbb{R}
-action from the Euler vector field of the
\mathbb{R}
-action, and vice versa). Last, note that the universality condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\circ\pi_{0}+_{p}T.\xi\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

along with Proposition
1.5
ensures the local triviality of
q
, so that the unique fibred
\mathbb{R}
-module structure associated to a differential bundle is indeed a vector bundle.

\pagenote
I have added details to this proof to clarify that it does give a bijection on the classes of objects.
∎

6
Connections on a differential bundle

The connections discussed in this section generalize the notion of an affine connection to a differential bundle, giving a “local coordinates” presentation for
TE
similar to the presentation of
T^{2}M
as
T_{3}M
induced by an affine connection. Chapter
6
makes extensive use of connections on vector bundles, so it is useful to set out the basic definitions before embarking on algebroid theory.

Definition 6.1

(
Cockett and Cruttwell 2017
)
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
be a differential bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.

•

A vertical connection is a map
\kappa:TE\to E
so that

(i)

\kappa
is a vertical descent, and hence a retract of the lift; thus,
\kappa\circ\lambda=id
;

(ii)

\kappa
is compatible with both differential bundle structures on
TE
, so that the maps

\displaystyle\kappa:(TE,\ell)\to(E,\lambda)

\displaystyle\kappa:(TE,c\circ T.\lambda)\to(E,\lambda)

are lift morphisms.

•

A horizontal connection is a map
\nabla:E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM\to TE
so that

(i)

\nabla
is a horizontal lift, and so is a retract of
(p.E,T.q):TE\to E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM
3
3

3

That
(p.E,T.q)
land in the pullback
E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM
is a consequence of naturality, as
p\circ T.q=q\circ p
.

; thus,
(p,T.q)\circ\nabla=id
;

(ii)

\nabla
is compatible with each pair of lifts, so that the maps

\displaystyle\nabla:(E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM,\lambda\times\xi)\to(TE,c\circ T.\lambda)

\displaystyle\nabla:(E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM,0\times\ell)\to(TE,\ell)

are lift morphisms.

•

A full connection is a pair
(\kappa,\nabla)
that satisfies the following compatibility relations:
\pagenote

I have extended the exposition on the compatibility relation between
\kappa,\nabla
to make it clear that it is about constructing an isomorphism
TE\cong E_{2}\times TM
.

(i)

\kappa\circ\nabla=\xi\circ q\circ\pi_{0}
,

(ii)

\nabla(p,T.q)+_{T.q}\mu(p,\kappa)=id
, so that there is an isomorphism
E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!E\cong TE
.
\pagenote

A notion that will be useful when dealing with classical differential geometry is that of a
covariant derivative
, whose definition is equivalent to that of a vertical connection in the category of smooth manifolds.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda,\kappa)
be a vertical connection.
4
4

4

The definition of a covariant derivative only uses a vertical connection.

The
covariant derivative
associated to
(\kappa,\nabla)
is the map

\nabla_{(-)}[=]:\Gamma(\pi)\times\Gamma(p)\to\Gamma(\pi);(A,X)\mapsto(\kappa\circ TA\circ X).

Lucyshyn-Wright 2018
drastically simplified the notion of a full connection by showing that it is exactly a vertical connection satisfying a universal property.

Proposition 6.3

(
Lucyshyn-Wright 2018
)
.

A connection on a differential bundle is equivalently specified by a vertical connection

\kappa:TE\to E

so that the following diagram exhibits
TE
as a biproduct in the category of differential bundles over

:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
