There is also a notion of flatness for connections that extends to vertical connections on a differential bundle.

Definition 6.4
.

A connection
\kappa
on a differential bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
is
flat
whenever

\kappa\circ T.\kappa\circ c=\kappa\circ T.\kappa.

We can see that connections are closed under similar constructions to differential bundles, in particular idempotent splittings and the reindexing construction from Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
be a differential bundle equipped with a vertical connection.
\pagenote

I have reworded the second part of this lemma so that it no longer refers to effective vertical connections. The proof has also been streamlined.

(i)

Any linear retract of
(q,\xi,\lambda)
will have a vertical connection.

(ii)

The pullback differential bundle induced by pulling back
q
along
f:N\to M
will have a vertical connection.

If the vertical connection is flat or is part of a full connection (that is, it satisfies the universality condition in Proposition
6.3
), then the induced connection will be as well.

Proof.

The universal property induces the vertical connection in each case. The construction will preserve flatness as it is an equational condition, and it preserves effectiveness by the commutativity of limits.
∎

There is no reason for every differential bundle in a tangent category to have a connection (for example, the tangent bundle in the free tangent category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
in Chapter
7
is a differential bundle that does not have a connection). However, if the total space of a differential bundle has an affine connection, this induces a compatible connection on the differential bundle and its base space.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
be a differential bundle in a tangent category in
\mathbb{C}
, where
E
has a (flat) vertical connection. Then the total space
M
and the differential bundle
(q,\xi,\lambda)
each have a (flat) vertical connection. If the connection is full (so there is a compatible horizontal connection), then the induced connections are likewise full.

Proof.

By the strong universality condition for differential bundles, the differential bundle
(q\circ\pi_{0}:E{{}_{q}}\!\times_{p}\!TM\to M,(\xi,0),\lambda\times\ell)
is the pullback differential bundle of
p:TE\to E
along
\xi:M\to E
.
This gives
(q\circ\pi_{0},(\xi,0),(\lambda,\ell))
, a (flat, effective) vertical connection by Lemma
6.5
, and so it yields
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda)
and
(p:TM\to M,0,\ell)
as (flat, effective) vertical connections by the idempotent splitting property.
∎

Every smooth manifold has an affine connection, thus inducing a connection on any vector bundle.

Corollary 6.7
.

Every vector bundle has a connection.

\pagenote

Kristine mentioned it’s a bit weird to end a chapter on a piece of notation, which I makes sense once it has been pointed out to me.

Chapter 6
Involution algebroids

This chapter accomplishes the first major goal of this thesis by providing a tangent-categorical axiomatization of Lie algebroids, namely involution algebroids. Much like vector bundles, Lie algebroids are a highly non-algebraic notion (in the sense of
Freyd and Kelly 1972
), being vector bundles equipped with a Lie algebra structure on the set of sections of the projection. Furthermore, the bracket on sections must satisfy a product rule with respect to
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions on the base space (the
Leibniz law
), introducing another piece of non-algebraic structure to the definition. The tangent-categorical definition of Lie algebroids will treat the tangent bundle as the “prototypical Lie algebroid” in which the vertical lift
\ell:T\Rightarrow T^{2}
identifies the vector bundle structure and the canonical flip
c:T^{2}\Rightarrow T^{2}
plays the role of the Lie bracket.

Lie algebroids are the natural many-object analogue to Lie algebras, in the same way that Lie groupoids are the many-object analogue of Lie groups. In the single-object case, a Lie group is classically thought of as a space of symmetries for some smooth manifold (one often identifies a group action
G\times M\to M
), and a Lie algebra may similarly be thought of as a space of
derivations
(often identified as a sub-Lie-algebra of
\chi(M)
for a manifold
M
). The extension of groups to groupoids is natural; in fact, Brandt’s introduction of groupoids in
Brandt 1927
predates MacLane and Eilenberg’s invention of category theory in
Eilenberg and MacLane 1945
by nearly two decades. The translation of Lie algebras to the many-object case is not as straightforward. The first step is to replace the vector space underlying a Lie algebra with a vector bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda)
. The idea is to axiomatize this vector bundle so that each section in
\Gamma(\pi)
corresponds to a derivation on
C^{\infty}(M)
. The
anti-commutator
operation on derivations from Proposition
2.18
suggests there should be a Lie bracket
[-,-]:\Gamma(\pi)\otimes\Gamma(\pi)\to\Gamma(\pi)
(similar to the partially-defined multiplication for a groupoid), while the correspondence with derivations on
C^{\infty}(M)
suggests there be a vector bundle morphism
\varrho:A\to TM
satisfying the
Leibniz law
:

[X,f\cdot Y]=f\cdot[X,Y]+[X,f]\cdot Y;\hskip 14.22636pt[X,f]:=\hat{p}\circ T.f\circ\varrho\circ X

(1)

(the full definition of Lie algebroids may be found in
1.1
). This “operational” definition of Lie algebroids makes it difficult to describe their morphisms, and furthermore it essentially fails to be an algebraic structure in the classical sense, as it axiomatizes structure on the
set of sections
of a map rather than a morphism in the category itself.

Involution algebroids were introduced to provide a tangent-categorical presentation of Lie algebroids, similar to the relationship between differential bundles and vector bundles. Chapter
5
focused on the Euler vector field construction on a vector bundle, showing that this induced a fully-faithful functor from vector bundles to associative coalgebras (lifts) of the weak comonad
(T,\ell)
, and identified vector bundles with a subcategory of
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathsf{SMan})
satisfying a universal property. The corresponding construction for Lie algebroids, then, is the
canonical involution
, which was identified by Eduardo Martinez and his collaborators (a clearly written exposition may be found in Section 4 of
de León et al. 2005
). Given a Lie algebroid
(\pi:A\to M,\varrho:A\to TM,[-,-]:\Gamma(\pi)\otimes\Gamma(\pi)\to\Gamma(\pi))
, its canonical involution is a map

\sigma:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA.

Using this
\sigma
map, there is a straightforward characterization of Lie algebroid morphisms: a Lie algebroid morphism is precisely a vector bundle morphism
(f,m):A\to B
that preserves the anchor and involution maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B{{}_{\varrho^{B}}}\!\times_{T.\pi^{B}}\!TB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B{{}_{\varrho^{B}}}\!\times_{T.\pi^{B}}\!TB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times T.f}

Furthermore, it is implicit in Martinez’s work (
Martínez 2001
) that
\sigma
satisfies axioms corresponding to the Lie algebroid axioms. Thus, involutivity corresponds to antisymmetry of the Lie bracket

\sigma\circ\sigma=id\iff[X,Y]+[Y,X]=0,

while the Leibniz law holds if and only if the
\varrho
map sends the algebroid involution to the canonical flip on
M
,

T.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma=c\circ T.\pi\circ\varrho\iff\forall f\in C^{\infty}(M),[X,f\cdot Y]=f\cdot[X,Y]+[X,f]\cdot Y

(using the same definition as before for
[X,f]
).

The idea of an involution algebroid, then, is to axiomatize the canonical involution directly, just as differential bundles axiomatize the Euler vector field of a differential bundle. An involution algebroid is a differential bundle equipped with a pair of structure maps

\varrho:A\to TM,\hskip 4.26773pt\sigma:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA

satisfying a collection of axioms. Some of them are straightforward translations of the structure equations for Lie algebroids given in
Martínez 2001
, for instance

T.\varrho\circ\lambda=\ell\circ\varrho,\hskip 4.26773pt\sigma\circ\sigma=id,\hskip 4.26773ptT.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma=c\circ T.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}.

However, this requires a new coherence between the Euler vector field of the underlying vector bundle and the involution map:

\sigma\circ(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda)=(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda).

The most striking new fact about this coherence is that the Jacobi identity on the bracket
[-,-]
corresponds to the
Yang–Baxter
equation on
\sigma
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times T.\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times T.\sigma}

This is both surprising (it is a new characterization of a central object of study in differential geometry and mathematical physics) and yet in a way expected (the work in
Cockett and Cruttwell 2015
,
Mackenzie 2013
indicates that the Lie algebra structure on the set of vector fields over a manifold follows from the Yang–Baxter equation on
c
). This vector-field-free presentation of the Jacobi identity allows for a structural approach to Lie algebroids that drives the work presented in Chapters
7
and
8
.

As with
Chapter

5
, the first section is expository, and is concerned with introducing the category of Lie algebroids.
The second section introduces anchored bundles, together with the space of prolongations of an anchored bundle. The relationship between anchored bundles and involution algebroids is equivalent to that between reflexive graphs and groupoids (the subject of Chapter
8
), the space of prolongations of an anchored bundle being equivalent to the set of composable arrows for a groupoid. This section is mostly a translation of Martinez’s prolongation construction to a general tangent category. The rest of the chapter contains new results, developed in collaboration with Matthew Burke and Richard Garner.

Section 3 introduces involution algebroids, which are anchored bundles equipped with an involution map on their space of prolongations. Section 4 considers an anchored bundle in a tangent category with negatives that is equipped with a connection. The connection gives an involution algebroid a “local coordinates” presentation (in the sense of Section
4
) that is equivalent to the local characterization of Lie algebroids from Section 1. The final section of this chapter establishes the main result: the category of Lie algebroids is isomorphic to that of involution algebroids in smooth manifolds.
\pagenote

The introduction has been substantially expanded to better explain what is happening in this chapter and why we are doing it.

1
Lie algebroids

This section reviews the basic theory of Lie algebroids: their definition and that of their morphisms, along with some introductory examples. The classical definition will not appear elsewhere in this chapter, however, as we quickly introduce Martinez’s
structure equations
for a Lie algebroid (
Martínez 2001
), then translate them into tangent-categorical terms using a connection.
\pagenote

The substantial revisions to the introduction made the original preamble to this section redundant. There have also been substantial revisions to the thesis up until this point, and those changes have percolated into this section. In particular, the notation for the
C^{\infty}(M)
-module of sections
\Gamma(\pi)
was set in the previous chapter, and we stick to those conventions.

Definition 1.1
.

A
Lie algebroid
is a vector bundle
\pi:A\rightarrow M
equipped with an anchor
\varrho:A\to TM
and a bracket
[-,-]:\Gamma(\pi)\otimes\Gamma(\pi)\rightarrow\Gamma(\pi)
satisfying the following axioms:

•

bilinear:
[aX_{1}+bX_{2},Y]=a[X_{1},Y]+b[X_{2},Y]
and
[X,aY_{1}+bY_{2}]=a[X,Y_{1}]+b[X,Y_{2}]

•

anti-symmetric:
[X,Y]+[Y,X]=0

•

Jacobi:
[X,[Y,Z]]=[[X,Y],Z]+[Y,[X,Z]]

•

Leibniz:
[X,f\cdot Y]=f\cdot[X,Y]+[X,f]\cdot Y
\pagenote
Changed the notation of the Lie derivative to this bracket version.

(where
[X,f]
is defined as in Equation
1
).

Example 1.2
.

(i)

The canonical example of a Lie algebroid is, of course, the tangent bundle using the operational tangent bundle from Definition
2.16
.

(ii)

A
Lie algebra
is a Lie algebroid over the terminal object: for a group
G
, the bundle of source-constant tangent vectors is the usual Lie functor from Lie groups to Lie algebras, because a groupoid is a one-object group.

(iii)

The bundle of
source-constant
tangent vectors
s,t:G\to M
of a Lie groupoid forms a Lie algebroid. This bundle is defined by the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TM\times G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T.s,p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the projection is
\pi
and the target is given by
\varrho
in the diagram.
There is an injective
\mathbb{R}
-module morphism from sections of
\pi
,
\Gamma(\pi)
to vector fields on
G
,
\chi(G)
, and the Lie bracket on
G
is closed over the image of this lift, putting a Lie bracket on
\Gamma(\pi)
. In particular, we can see that
TM
is the bundle of source-constant tangent vectors for the pair groupoid on a manifold
M
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(M\times M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(M\times M)\times TM}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,T.\pi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,0\circ p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Given
(u,v):X\to T(M\times M)
above
(m,m):X\to TM
with
u=0\circ m
, it follows that
u=0\circ p\circ u
, so
v
is the unique map induced into
TM
.

(iv)

Every group is a groupoid over a single object. The Lie algebroid associated with a group
G
, then, is the usual Lie algebra.

(v)

From the Hamiltonian formalism of mechanics, every
Poisson manifold
has an associated Lie algebroid.
A Poisson manifold is a manifold
M
equipped with a
Poisson algebra
structure on
C^{\infty}(M)
, namely a Lie algebra that is also a derivation:

[f\cdot g,h]=f\cdot[g,h]+[f,h]\cdot g

where
\cdot
is the multiplication in the algebra
C^{\infty}(M)
as in Lemma
1.3
. The cotangent bundle over a Poisson manifold
M
is canonically a Lie algebroid, called a Poisson Lie algebroid
Courant 1994
.

(vi)

Any Lie algebra bundle—that is, a vector bundle equipped with a Lie bracket on its space of sections—is a Lie algebroid, with anchor map
\xi\circ\pi
.

Morphisms of Lie algebroids are notoriously difficult to work with, and have an involved definition.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
A,B
be a pair of anchored bundles over
M,N
, and
\Phi:A\to B
an anchored bundle morphism over a map
\phi:M\to N
. A
\Phi
-decomposition of
X\in\Gamma(\pi_{A})
is a set of
X_{i}\in\Gamma(\pi_{B})
and
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)
so that

\Phi\circ X=\sum_{i}f_{i}\cdot X_{i}\circ\phi.

An anchor-preserving vector bundle morphism
\Phi
is a Lie algebroid morphism if and only if for any
X,Y\in\Gamma(\pi_{A})
and
\Phi
-decompositions
\{X_{i},f_{i}\},\{Y_{j},g_{j}\}
of
X,Y
, the following equation holds:

\Phi\circ[X,Y]=\sum f_{i}\cdot g_{j}\cdot([X_{i},Y_{j}]\circ\phi)+\sum[X,f_{i}]\cdot(X_{i}\circ\phi)-\sum[Y,g_{j}](Y_{j}\circ\phi).

The equation defining a Lie algebroid morphism holds independently of the choice of
\Phi
-decomposition (see
Higgins and Mackenzie 1990
for a proof).
\pagenote
Here I have tidied up the definition by explaining what a
\Phi
-decomposition is (in particular, quantifying over the
X_{i},f_{i}
, and also clarified some wording.

Example 1.4
.

(i)

If
A,B
are Lie algebroids over a base manifold
M
,
\Phi:A\to B
is a Lie algebroid morphism if and only if it preserves the anchor and Lie bracket.
\pagenote
I removed the remark about tangent Lie algebroids, as it refers to De Rham cohomologies which have disappeared from this thesis.

(ii)

Given two Lie algebroid morphisms
f:A\to B,g:B\to C
, their composition
g\circ f
is also a Lie algebroid morphism.

(iii)

A
Poisson Sigma model
(see
Bojowald et al. 2005
) is a morphism of Lie algebroids

\phi:T\Sigma\to T^{*}M

for which
\Sigma
is a 2-dimensional manifold and
T\Sigma
denotes its tangent Lie algebroid, while
M
is a Poisson manifold and
T^{*}M
denotes the Lie algebroid structure on its cotangent bundle .

Lie algebroids are a natural generalization of Lie algebras to the “multi-object” setting, but they are ill-suited for a functorial presentation of the theory. A step in this direction is to consider the coordinate-based presentation of the Lie bracket and its coherences due to
Martínez 2001
.
Let
A
be an anchored bundle over
M
equipped with a bilinear bracket on its space of sections, and choose a pair of bases for
\Gamma(\pi)
and
\chi(M)
: for
\Gamma(\pi)
write
\{e_{\alpha}\}
, and for
\chi(M)
write
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\}
. The anchor and bracket then have a presentation in local coordinates:

\varrho(e_{\alpha})=\sum_{i}\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\hskip 14.22636pt[e_{\alpha},e_{\beta}]=\sum_{\gamma}C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}e_{\gamma}

(from here on out, we use Einstein summation notation to simplify our calculations, so instead write

\varrho(e_{\alpha})=\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\hskip 14.22636pt[e_{\alpha},e_{\beta}]=C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}e_{\gamma}

with
\sum
suppressed).
The following characterization of the Lie algebroid axioms uses Martinez’s
structure equations
.
\pagenote
This is better called the ”structure equations” for a Lie algebroid.

Proposition 1.5
.

[
Martínez 2001
]
An anchored bundle
A
over
M
equipped with a bracket is a Lie algebroid if and only if
\varrho
and
[-,-]
satisfy the following structure equations:

(i)

Alternating:

C^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}+C^{\nu}_{\beta\alpha}=0

(ii)

Leibniz:

\varrho^{j}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial\varrho^{i}_{\beta}}{\partial x^{j}}=\varrho^{i}_{\gamma}C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\varrho^{j}_{\beta}\frac{\partial\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}}{\partial x^{j}}

(iii)

Bianchi:

0=\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial x^{i}}+\varrho^{i}_{\beta}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\gamma\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}+\varrho^{i}_{\gamma}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{i}}+C^{\mu}_{\beta\gamma}C^{\nu}_{\alpha\mu}+C^{\mu}_{\gamma\alpha}C^{\nu}_{\beta\mu}+C^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}C^{\nu}_{\gamma\mu}

This proposition is a straightforward translation of the Lie algebroid axioms into local coordinates using a covariant derivative.
First, recall that by the smooth Serre–Swan theorem (11.33
Nestruev 2003
), the bilinearity of the bracket
\pagenote
Clarified where this new bracket comes from/what category it lives in.

[-,-]:\Gamma(\pi)\times\Gamma(\pi)\to\Gamma(\pi)

as a morphism of
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules guarantees that it corresponds to a bilinear morphism
A_{2}\to A
of vector bundles, meaning that there exists a globally defined bilinear map
A_{2}\to A
that is equal to the Lie bracket when applied to sections of the projection. We record this as a lemma:
\pagenote
Clarified the point that this theorem gives a global definition of the Lie bracket, extending from from
\Gamma(\pi)
to general elements in the category

Lemma 1.6
.

For every Lie algebroid
\mathcal{A}
, there is a bilinear morphism

\langle-,-\rangle:A_{2}\to A

so that for any sections
X,Y\in\Gamma(\pi)
,
\langle X,Y\rangle=[X,Y]
.

There are two structure maps derived from
\langle-,-\rangle
that encode the coherences of a Lie algebroid. The first map measures the extent to which the anchor maps fail to preserve the chosen connections on the vector bundle
\pi:A\to M
and the tangent bundle
p:TM\to M
.
\pagenote
Added some explanation of what the curly bracket map does.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
A
be a Lie algebroid, and for a chosen horizontal connection
\nabla
on
A
and vertical connection
\kappa^{\prime}
on
TM
, set

\{v,x\}_{\kappa^{\prime},\nabla}:=TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!A\xrightarrow{\nabla}TA\xrightarrow{T.\varrho}T^{2}M\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\prime}}TM.

When the choice of connection is evident by context, we will suppress the subscript.

Observation 1.8
.

The above parentheses bracket corresponds to the symbol

\{-,-\}=\varrho^{j}_{\beta}\frac{\partial\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}}{\partial x^{j}}.

The Leibniz coherence may be rewritten as follows:

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
A
be an anchored bundle with a bilinear bracket (inducing an involution
\sigma
).
Choose connections
(\kappa,\nabla),(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
on
A
and
TM
respectively.
The bracket and anchor map satisfy the Leibniz law if and only if

\varrho\circ\langle x,y\rangle_{(\kappa,\nabla)}+\{\varrho x,y\}_{(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla)}=\{\varrho y,x\}_{(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla)}.

Proof.

The condition is equivalent to the identity

\varrho^{j}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial\varrho^{i}_{\beta}}{\partial x^{j}}=\varrho^{i}_{\gamma}C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\varrho^{j}_{\beta}\frac{\partial\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}}{\partial x^{j}}.

∎

The Bianchi axiom measures the failure of the Jacobi identity in local coordinates, and states that it must be corrected for by the curvature of the brackets.
We see that

C^{\nu}_{\alpha\mu}C^{\mu}_{\beta\gamma}=[e_{\alpha},[e_{\beta},e_{\gamma}]]

while

\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial x^{i}}e_{\nu}=\kappa\circ T(\langle-,-\rangle_{(\kappa,\nabla)})\circ\nabla^{A_{2}}\circ(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},e_{\beta},e_{\gamma})

determines a trilinear map

\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},e_{\beta},e_{\gamma}\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\kappa\circ T(\langle-,-\rangle_{(\kappa,\nabla)})\circ\nabla^{A_{2}}\circ(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},e_{\beta},e_{\gamma}).

This is the second derived map used in the structure equations for a Lie algebroid.

Definition 1.10
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle equipped with a bilinear map

\langle-,-\rangle:A_{2}\to A.

The derived ternary bracket
\{-,-,-\}:TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A\to A
is defined as

\{v,x,y\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A\xrightarrow{\nabla[A2]}TA_{2}\xrightarrow{T.\langle-,-\rangle}TA\xrightarrow{\kappa}A

where
\nabla[A2]
is the pairing
(\nabla(\pi_{0},\pi_{1}),\nabla(\pi_{0},\pi_{2}))
.

Observation 1.11
.

The ternary bracket corresponds to the following symbol:

\{-,-,-\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial x^{i}}.

Lemma 1.12
.

Let
A
be an anchored bundle over
M
with a bilinear bracket and denote the induced involution by
\sigma
.
Choose a pair of connections and write the derived maps
\langle-,-\rangle,\{-,-,-\}
.
Then

(i)

the bracket is antisymmetric if and only if its globalization is; that is,
\langle e_{\alpha},e_{\beta}\rangle+\langle e_{\beta},e_{\alpha}\rangle=0
;

(ii)

the bracket satisfies the Jacobi identity if and only if it is alternating in the last two arguments and

0=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\langle x_{\gamma_{0}},\langle x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}\rangle\rangle+\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\{\varrho x_{\gamma_{0}},x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}\}.

Proof.

(i)

This is equivalent to
C^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}+C^{\nu}_{\beta\alpha}=0
.

(ii)

This is equivalent to

0=C^{\mu}_{\beta\gamma}C^{\nu}_{\alpha\mu}+C^{\mu}_{\gamma\alpha}C^{\nu}_{\beta\mu}+C^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}C^{\nu}_{\gamma\mu}+\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial x^{i}}+\varrho^{i}_{\beta}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\gamma\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}+\varrho^{i}_{\gamma}\frac{\partial C^{\nu}_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial x^{i}}.

∎

Proposition 1.13
.

A Lie algebroid is exactly an anchored vector bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
with a bilinear, alternating map

\langle-,-\rangle:A_{2}\to A;\hskip 14.22636pt\langle x,y\rangle+\langle y,x\rangle=0

so that for any connection
(\nabla,\kappa)
on
A
, the maps

\{v,x\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\kappa^{\prime}\circ T.\varrho\circ\nabla(v,x),\hskip 14.22636pt\{v,x,y\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\kappa\circ T(\langle-,-\rangle_{(\kappa,\nabla)})\circ\nabla^{A_{2}}\circ(v,x,y)

(2)

satisfy the equations

(i)

\varrho\circ\langle x,y\rangle+\{\varrho x,y\}_{(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla)}=\{\varrho y,x\}_{(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla)}
,

(ii)

\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\langle x_{\gamma_{0}},\langle x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}\rangle\rangle+\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\{\varrho x_{\gamma_{0}},x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}=0
.

There is also a local coordinates presentation of morphisms as in Section 2 of
Martínez 2018
. An anchored bundle morphism
A\to B
is a Lie algebroid morphism whenever

f^{\beta}_{\gamma}A^{\gamma}_{\alpha\delta}+\varrho^{i}_{\delta}\frac{\partial f^{\beta}_{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}=B^{\beta}_{\theta\sigma}f^{\theta}_{\alpha}f^{\sigma}_{\delta}+\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial f^{\beta}_{\delta}}{\partial x^{i}}.

The
A
and
B
arguments are understood as the brackets, so this condition can be rewritten as

f^{\beta}_{\gamma}A^{\gamma}_{\alpha\delta}=f\circ\langle\alpha,\delta\rangle,\hskip 7.11317ptB^{\beta}_{\theta\sigma}f^{\theta}_{\alpha}f^{\sigma}_{\delta}=\langle f\circ\alpha,f\circ\delta\rangle.

Set the following notation for maps between vector bundles with connection:
\pagenote
Reintroduce the
\nabla
notation here after removing it.

\nabla[f]:A_{2}\to B:=\kappa^{B}\circ T.f\circ\nabla^{A}\lx@proof@logical@and f:A\to B(\kappa^{A},\nabla^{A},A,\lambda^{A})(\kappa^{B},\nabla^{B},B,\lambda^{B})

(3)

The
\varrho
terms are understood to be the torsion, so that

\displaystyle\varrho^{i}_{\delta}\frac{\partial f^{\beta}_{\alpha}}{\partial x^{i}}=\kappa\circ T.f\circ\nabla(\varrho e_{\delta},e_{\alpha})=\nabla[f](\varrho e_{\delta},e_{\alpha}),

\displaystyle\varrho^{i}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial f^{\beta}_{\delta}}{\partial x^{i}}=\kappa\circ T.f\circ\nabla(\varrho\alpha,\delta)=\nabla[f](\varrho e_{\alpha},e_{\delta}),

using the notation set up in Equation
3
.
The notion of a Lie algebroid morphism, then, has the following presentation:

Proposition 1.14

(
Martínez 2018
)
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\varrho^{A},\langle-,-\rangle^{A}),(q:B\to N,\varrho^{B},\langle-,-\rangle^{B})
be a pair of Lie algebroids with chosen connections
(\kappa^{-},\nabla^{-})
.
An anchor-preserving vector bundle morphism
f:A\to B
is a Lie algebroid morphism if and only if

f\circ\langle e_{\alpha},e_{\delta}\rangle+\nabla[f]\circ(\varrho e_{\delta},e_{\alpha})=\langle f\circ\alpha,f\circ\delta\rangle+\nabla[f]\circ(\varrho e_{\alpha},e_{\delta}).

2
Anchored bundles

Anchored bundles are to Lie algebroids what reflexive graphs are to groupoids. Each theory has (mostly) the same structure, but while a reflexive graph is missing a groupoid’s composition operation, an anchored bundle lacks a Lie algebroid’s bracket operation. This section reviews the basic theory of anchored bundles and their prolongations (see
Mackenzie 2005
for more details).

Definition 2.1
.

An anchored bundle in a tangent category is a differential bundle
(A\xrightarrow{\pi}M,\xi,\lambda)
equipped with a linear morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

A morphism of anchored bundles is a linear bundle morphism
(f,v)
that preserves the anchors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.v}

The category of anchored bundles and anchored bundle morphisms in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is written
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
, and a generic anchored bundle is written
(A\xrightarrow{\pi}M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
.

There are two pullbacks that are associated with every anchored bundle.
These play the role of the spaces of composable arrows
G_{2}:=G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!G,G_{3}:=G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!G
for a reflexive graph
s,t:G\to M
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(A\xrightarrow{\pi}M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle. Its
first and second prolongations
are given by the limits

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(The notation for the fibre product is slightly non-standard, as it is not technically a pullback.)
Throughout this chapter, it will always be assumed that the first and second prolongations of an anchored bundle exist (although no choice of prolongation is explicitly made).
\pagenote
I have explicitly defined the category of anchored bundles, and split the definition of prolongations from the definition of an anchored bundle. Also clarified that we are simply assuming these first two
T
-limits always exist, but we do not make a specific choice of prolongation.

Remark 2.3
.

It is not strictly necessary that the prolongations of an anchored bundle exist; this condition is primarily a matter of convenience when discussing involution algebroids. Every result in this section, that does not explicitly mention prolongations, holds for an anchored bundle independently of their existence.

Example 2.4
.

\pagenote

This set of examples has been substantially revised to fix some clumsy wording in the original version. It made sense to included the full result about
\mathbb{C}
be a reflective subcategory of
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
and the coreflection from the category of differential bundles to anchored bundles rather than having them as separate results, as they each describe classes of anchored bundles.

(i)

For any object
M
,
id:TM\to TM
is an anchor for the tangent differential bundle, and every
f:M\to N
yields a morphism of anchored bundles. Moreover, for any anchored bundle over
M
the anchor is itself a morphism of anchored bundles
(A,\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\to(TM,p,0,\ell,id)
. This induces a fully faithful functor:

\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C}).

This inclusion has a left adjoint, which sends an anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
to its base space
M
(the unit is the anchor map
\varrho
), so that
\mathbb{C}
is a reflective subcategory of
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

For any differential bundle
(A,\pi,\xi,\lambda)
, the map
0\circ\pi:A\to TM
is an anchor and every morphism
f:A\to B
of differential bundles again yields a morphism of anchored bundles. The naturality of
0
ensures that every differential bundle morphism will preserve this trivial anchor map, giving a fully faithful functor

\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C}).

This functor has a right adjoint that replaces the anchor map with the trivial anchor map

(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\mapsto(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,0\circ\pi)

where the counit is given by the natural idempotent
e=\xi\circ\pi:(A,\lambda)\to(A,\lambda)
. It is trivial to check that the anchor map is preserved by the bundle morphism
(e,id)
:

\varrho\circ\xi\circ\pi=0\circ T.id\circ\pi

and so the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\circ\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}

This means that differential bundles are a

C^{\partial}\hookrightarrow T.C\xrightarrow{p.e^{s}}M.

The section is induced by

while post-composition with
T.t
gives the anchor map:

C^{\partial}\hookrightarrow T.C\xrightarrow{T.t}T.C.

The diagram is a pullback by composition, and the outer perimeter defines the pullback
\mathcal{L}(A)
. Note that any reflexive graph morphism will give rise to an anchored bundle morphism by naturality, making the construction of an anchored bundle from a reflexive graph functorial.

(iv)

For any object
M
in a tangent category,
c_{M}:T^{2}M\to T^{2}M
is an anchor on
(T.p,T.0,c\circ T.\ell)
, and every map

gives a morphism of anchored bundles.

(v)

For any anchored bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
, the differential bundle
(T.q,T.\xi,\newline
c\circ T.\lambda)
has an anchor

\varrho_{T}:TE\xrightarrow{T.\varrho}T^{2}M\xrightarrow{c}T^{2}M.

The first prolongation of an anchored bundle
\mathcal{L}(A)
behaves similarly to the second tangent bundle, except that it does not have a canonical flip. In the definition of an affine connection, the tangent bundle played a similar role to the “arities” of a theory. There is a lift map that makes this connection stronger:

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.
We define a
generalized lift
:

\hat{\lambda}:A\to A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA:=(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda).

This generalized lift satisfies the same coherences as the lift on the second tangent bundle:

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.
It follows that

(i)

(Coassociativity of
\hat{\lambda}
)
(\hat{\lambda}\times\ell)\circ\hat{\lambda}=(id\times T.\hat{\lambda})\circ\hat{\lambda}

(ii)

(Universality) the following diagram is a
T

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}

The top triangle of the diagram commutes by definition

Example 2.7
.

(i)

For

In a tangent category with a tangent display system, if

automatically exist.
In particular, for every anchored bundle in the category of smooth manifolds, all prolongations exist because the projection is a submersion (see

(iv)

A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\hookrightarrow T^{2}(G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!G).

\pagenote

It seemed cleaner to include this proposition in the above examples, since this is really just a remark about

The category of anchored bundles is, in a sense, “tangent monadic” over the category of differential bundles: the forgetful functor from anchored bundles to differential bundles “creates”
T
-limits and the tangent structure (this is all made precise in
Chapter

8
using an enriched perspective on tangent categories).

Observation 2.8
.

A limit of anchored bundles is the limit of the underlying differential bundles. Because the anchor is preserved by every map in the diagram, this induces a natural transformation for any
D:\mathcal{D}\to\mathsf{Anc}(C)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Anc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}

Thus,
\lim U.S.D
computes the limit of the underlying objects, while
\lim U.D
computes the limit of the underlying differential bundles in the diagram. The anchor/unit map, then, induces a differential bundle map:

\lim\varrho_{i}:(\lim A_{i},\lim\lambda_{i})\to(T.(\lim_{i}M_{i}),\ell.(\lim_{i}M_{i})).

This is the limit in the category of anchored bundles (so long as pullback powers of the limit projection and the two prolongations of the limit anchor bundle exist).

The tangent structure on lifts defined in Proposition
2.7
lifts to a tangent structure on anchored bundles.

Lemma 2.9
.

The category of anchored bundles in a tangent category has a tangent structure that maps objects as follows:

(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\mapsto(T.\pi:TA\to TM,T.\xi,c\circ T.\lambda,c\circ T.\varrho)

where the structure maps are all defined using the pointwise structure maps in
\mathbb{C}
.

\pagenote
There exists multiplie tangent-like structures on anchored bundles and involution algebroids, so I have added more detail in the statements of those results.

Proof.

(q:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)

(Tq,T\xi,c\circ T\lambda)
given by

; the diagram commutes by naturality and the coherences on

.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{3}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tp}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tc}

The tangent structure maps and universality properties all follow from the forgetful property of the functor from anchored bundles to differential bundles as a consequence of Observation

.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}

This gives the differential bundle structure

(A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}A,A\xrightarrow{(id,T.\xi\circ\varrho)}A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA,A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\xrightarrow{(0,c\circ T.\lambda)}T(A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA)).

Taking the fibre product in the category of anchored bundles as in Observation
2.8
yields the second differential bundle structure:

(A,\pi,\xi,\lambda)\xrightarrow{(\varrho,id)}(TM,p,0,\ell)\xleftarrow{(T\pi,\pi)}(TA,p,0,\ell).

The two lifts behave similarly to the pair
(T.\ell,\ell.T)
on the second tangent bundle, and
(\ell,\lambda_{T})
on the tangent bundle of a pre-differential bundle.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a tangent category and
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
the category of anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
. If
T
-pullback powers of
p\circ\pi_{0},\pi_{1}:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to A
exist, then there are two differential bundles on
\mathcal{L}(A)
, with lifts induced as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times(c\circ T.\lambda))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TA,c\circ T.\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,\lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TM,\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(TM,0)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

with structure maps

1.

(\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{p\circ\pi_{1}}A,A\xrightarrow{(\xi\pi,0)}\mathcal{L}(A),\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\lambda\times\ell}T\circ\mathcal{L}(A))
,

2.

(\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}A,A\xrightarrow{(id,T.\xi\circ\varrho)}\mathcal{L}(A),\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{0\times c\circ T.\lambda}T.\mathcal{L}(A))
.

Furthermore, the two lifts
\lambda_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\lambda_{\varrho}
commute:

c\circ T.(\lambda\times\ell)\circ(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)=T.(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\circ(\lambda\times\ell).

Proof.

The two differential bundles exist as a consequence of Observation
4.2
and Lemma
4.3
, respectively. The commutativity of limits follows by postcomposition, as both
c\circ T.\lambda,\ell
and
\lambda,0
commute by the differential bundle axioms.
∎

The above lemma determines a functor, which we denote
\mathcal{L}
, that sends an anchored bundle in
\mathbb{C}
to an anchored bundle in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
(equipped with the tangent structure from Proposition
2.7
).

Proposition 2.11
.

There is a functor
\mathcal{L}
from anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
to anchored bundles in the category of lifts
\pagenote
added more context for what
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
is
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
, that sends an anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
to the tuple in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathcal{L}}:=(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\xrightarrow{p\circ\pi_{1}}(A,0),

\displaystyle\xi^{\mathcal{L}}:=(A,0)\xrightarrow{(\xi\circ\pi,0)}(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)

\displaystyle\lambda^{\mathcal{L}}:=(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\xrightarrow{(\lambda\times\ell)}(T(\mathcal{L}(A)),c\circ T(0\times c\circ T.\lambda),

\displaystyle\varrho^{\mathcal{L}}:=(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}(TA,c\circ T.\lambda)

(note that this functor lands in anchored bundles of
non-singular
lifts; see Definition
3.1
).
Morphisms of anchored bundles

(f,m):(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\to(q:E\to N,\zeta,l,\delta)

are sent to

\mathcal{L}(f,m):=f{{}_{Tm}}\!\times_{Tm}\!Tf:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to E{{}_{\delta}}\!\times_{Tq}\!TE

Proof.

First, note that the tuple

(\pi^{\mathcal{L}}:\mathcal{L}(A)\to A,\xi^{\mathcal{L}},\lambda^{\mathcal{L}},\varrho^{\mathcal{L}})

is an anchored bundle, and that each morphism is a lift morphism:

•

\pi^{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\to(A,0)
follows because

\displaystyle T.p\circ T.\pi_{1}\circ(\lambda\times\ell)=T.p\circ\ell\circ\pi_{1}=0\circ p\circ\pi_{1}

•

\xi^{\mathcal{L}}:(A,0)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)
follows since

(T.\xi\circ T.\pi,T.0)\circ 0=(0\circ\xi\circ\pi,T.0\circ 0)=(0\circ\xi\circ\pi,c\circ T.\lambda\circ 0)=(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\circ(\xi\circ\pi,0)

•

\lambda^{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\to(T.\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.(c\circ T.\lambda))

follows by the commutativity of
0\times c\circ T.\lambda)
and
\lambda\times\ell
, and

•

\varrho^{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\to(TA,c\circ T.\lambda)
is a lift by definition of
\varrho^{\mathcal{L}}=\pi_{1}
.

This gives an anchored bundle in the category of non-singular lifts in
\mathbb{C}
.

Next, check that the mapping is functorial. To see that
\mathcal{L}(f,m)
preserves the lifts, note that
(f,m)
gives a morphism of diagrams for each pullback in
\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C})
defining the two lifts, so the induced map
\mathcal{L}(f,m)
\pagenote
There was some ambiguity in map names here, so
(f,v)
was switched to
(f,m)
. preserves each lift. To see that
\mathcal{L}(f,m)
preserves the anchor, check that

\varrho^{\mathcal{L}B}\circ\mathcal{L}(f,m)=\pi_{1}\circ(f\times T.f)=T.f\circ\pi_{1}=T.f\circ\varrho^{\mathcal{L}A}.

∎

Corollary 2.12
.

In a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
where pullbacks along differential bundle projections exist, such as a tangent category equipped with a proper retractive display system (Definition
5.3
) like the category of smooth manifolds, there is an endofunctor on the category of anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
,

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})=U^{\mathsf{Lift}}.\mathcal{L}.

Observation 2.13
.

T
-Limits in
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathsf{Lift}(\mathbb{C}))
are computed as pointwise limits in
\mathbb{C}
by Observations
2.8
and
4.2
, and
\mathcal{L}
is constructed as a limit, so it follows that
\mathcal{L}
preserves
T
-limits.

Proposition 2.14
.

The prolongation endofunctor
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
has natural transformations

•

p^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow id

•

0^{\prime}:id\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}

•

+^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}{{}_{p^{\prime}}}\!\times_{p^{\prime}}\!\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}

•

\ell^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}

satisfying all of the axioms of a tangent category that do not incvolve the canonical flip (Definition
3.2
).

(Sketch).

Note that the full argument is given in
Section

3
, but it is not difficult to sketch it out here. For the projection, zero map, and addition, use the differential bundle structure induced by Corollary
2.11
. To see the lift axiom, note that up to a choice of pullback, we have:

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)=\begin{cases}\quad\quad\quad\quad(p\circ\pi_{1},p\circ\pi_{2}):&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)\\
(\xi\circ\pi\circ\pi_{0},0\circ\pi_{1},0\circ\pi_{2}):&\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(\lambda\times\ell\times\ell):&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\to T.\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(\pi_{1},\pi_{2}):&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\to T.\mathcal{L}(A)\end{cases}

The “lift map” is then

\ell^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime};\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{A\times T.\hat{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)

(where we recall that
\hat{\lambda}=(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda):A\to\mathcal{L}(A)
).
∎

Remark 2.15
.

The structure described in Proposition
2.11
leads to the theory of
double vector bundles
, developed by MacKenzie and his collaborators
F. and Mackenzie 2019
;
Mackenzie 1992
. A double vector bundle is a commuting square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{D}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{D}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{B}}

where each projection is a vector bundle projection, and “vertical” and “horizontal” orientations of the square are each vector bundle homomorphisms. It was observed by
Grabowski and Rotkiewicz 2009
that this is equivalent to a pair of commuting
\mathbb{R}^{+}
-actions on the total space
E
; following the development in
Chapter

5
, this is a
commuting
pair of non-singular lifts on
E
,

\lambda^{A},\lambda^{B}:E\to TE,\hskip 4.26773ptT.\lambda^{B}\circ\lambda^{A}=c\circ T.\lambda^{A}\circ\lambda^{B}.

A proper exposition of the so-called “Ehresmann doubles” (
Mackenzie 2011
) for the structures in Lie theory would substantially expand the scope of this thesis, and so it has been relegated to the margins.

Finally, observe that a connection (see Section
6
) on an anchored bundle’s underlying differential bundle behaves similarly to an affine connection.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle with
\mathcal{L}(A)
existing in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.
\pagenote
The original draft had a typo - it was meant to be “anchored bundle equipped with a connection”.
If
(\pi,\xi,\lambda)
has a connection, then define

\hat{\kappa}:=\kappa\pi_{1},\quad\hat{\nabla}:=\nabla(\pi_{0},\varrho\pi_{1})

and note that

(i)

\hat{\kappa}:\mathcal{L}(A)\to A
is a retract of
(\xi\pi,\lambda)
, and
\hat{\kappa}:(\mathcal{L}(A),l)\to(A,\lambda)
is linear for both
l=(\lambda\times\ell),(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)
;

(ii)

\hat{\nabla}:A_{2}\to\mathcal{L}(A)
is a section of
(\pi_{0},p\circ\pi_{1})
and is bilinear;

(iii)

there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}(A)\cong A_{3}
:

added the explicit isomorphism

\nabla(p\circ\pi_{1},\varrho\circ\pi_{0})+_{(\pi,T.\pi)}\hat{\mu}(p\circ\pi_{1},\hat{\kappa})=id.

Example 2.17
.

Every vector bundle in the category of smooth manifolds has a connection; it follows that Lemma
2.16
holds for every anchored bundle in the category of smooth manifolds.

Remark 2.18
.

The category of anchored bundles in a tangent category is almost a tangent category, except that it lacks a symmetry map. The “differential objects” in such a category will act like a cartesian differential category, except that the symmetry of mixed partial derivatives fails. There has been some interest in settings for differentiable programming where the symmetry of mixed partial derivatives need not hold (see Definition 3.4 along with the discussion at the end of Section 6 in
Cruttwell et al. 2021
);
\pagenote
I have included an actual reference to quantify some interest. the category of anchored bundles in a tangent category appears to be a source of examples.

3
Involution algebroids

Involution algebroids are a tangent-categorical axiomatization of Lie algebroids. Recall that a Lie algebroid has almost all of the structure of the operational tangent bundle, in particular a Lie bracket on sections that satisfies the Leibniz law so that it gives a directional derivative. In Proposition
2.14
, it was demonstrated that the category of anchored bundles have almost all of the same maps as the tangent bundle, the only structure map missing being the canonical flip

c:T^{2}M\Rightarrow T^{2}M

which, we may recall from the chapter introduction (and
Cockett and Cruttwell 2015
,
Mackenzie 2013
), is used in constructing the Lie bracket of vector fields in a tangent category with negatives. Thus, a natural next step is to add an involution map to an anchored bundle and require that it satisfies the same coherences as
c
from the tangent bundle.

Definition 3.1
.

An involution algebroid is an anchored bundle
(A,\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
equipped with a map
\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)
satisfying the following axioms:

(i)

Involution:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

(ii)

Double linearity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\times c\circ T.\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda\times\ell}

(iii)

Symmetry of lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\lambda}=(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\lambda}}

(iv)

Target:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

(v)

Yang–Baxter:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times T.\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times T.\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times T.\sigma}

A
is an
almost
-involution algebroid if the involution does not satisfy the Yang–Baxter equation.
A morphism of involution algebroids is a morphism of anchored bundles, so that
\mathcal{L}(f)\circ\sigma_{A}=\sigma_{B}\circ\mathcal{L}(f)
.
(Note that because
\sigma
is an isomorphism and
\sigma=\sigma^{-1}
,

\sigma:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)

is linear as well.) Write the category of involution algebroids and involution algebroid morphisms in
\mathbb{C}
is written
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
.
\pagenote

The category
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
has been explicitly defined - this addresses a few later remarks in this section.

Observation 3.2
.

It is not immediately clear that the Yang–Baxter equation is well-typed. This follows from the target axiom (iv) and the double linearity axiom (ii). Starting with
(u,v,w):A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A
, we see that
\sigma\times c
is well-typed if and only if

T.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma(u,v)=T^{2}.\pi\circ c\circ w=c\circ T^{2}.\pi\circ w=c\circ T.\varrho\circ v.

Similarly,
1\times T.\sigma
is well-typed if
T.\varrho\circ u=T.\pi\circ\pi_{0}\circ T.\sigma(v,w)
; then use the double linearity axiom to compute

\displaystyle T.\pi\circ\pi_{0}\circ T.\sigma(v,w)=T.\pi\circ T.p\circ T.\pi_{1}(v,w)=T.\pi\circ T.p\circ w

\displaystyle=T.p\circ T^{2}.\pi\circ w=T.p\circ T.\varrho\circ v=T.\pi\circ v.

This perspective on Lie algebroids has already appearad in the work of Martinez and his collaborators in
de León et al. 2005
, where a “canonical involution” was derived on space of prolongations of a Lie algebroid using the formula

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A);\sigma(x,y,z)=\sigma(y,x,z+\langle x,y\rangle).

The structure of this map has been largely unexplored; helpfully, involution algebroids succeed in reverse-engineering axioms for an involution map that will induce a Lie bracket on the sections of the projection map.
\pagenote
I removed the remark about the Yang-Baxter equation, as that is discussed more thoroughly in the introduction of this chapter. Identifying axioms on the involution to identify Lie brackets is original, I think I may have muddied the waters with my original remark.
The bracket from the original Lie algebroid is induced using the same formula as for the bracket of vector fields on the tangent bundle:

\lambda\circ[X,Y]^{*}=\left((\pi_{1}\circ\sigma\circ(id,T.X\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{p}T.Y\circ X\circ\varrho)-_{T.\pi}0Y\right).

Furthermore, a morphism of anchored bundles is a Lie algebroid morphism if and only if it preserves the derived involution map.

Example 3.3
.

\pagenote

I have updated this example following to clarify the relationships between
\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C}),\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
.

(i)

For any
M
in
\mathbb{C}
,
(TM,p,0,id,c)
is an involution algebroid. Furthermore, for any involution algebroid anchored on
M
,
(\varrho,id)
is a morphism of involution algebroids (by the target axiom). This defines a fully faithful functor
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
. The same construction as the anchored bundle case in Example
2.4
(i) exhibits
\mathbb{C}
as a reflective subcategory of
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

For any differential bundle, the trivial anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,0\circ\pi)
has an involution using the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}(A)\cong A_{3}
, given by
\sigma:=(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2})
(proving this map satisfies the involution algebroid axioms is just an exercise in combinatorics). It follows that every differential bundle morphism gives rise to a morphism of these
trivial
involution algebroids. The same construction from the anchored bundle case (Example
2.4
(ii)) exhibits
\mathsf{Diff}(\mathbb{C})
as a
coreflective
subcategory of involution algebroids.

(iii)

Consider a groupoid

s,t:G\to M,\hskip 4.26773pte:M\to G,\hskip 4.26773pt(-)^{-1}:G\to G,\hskip 4.26773ptm:G_{2}\to G.

The underlying reflexive graph has an associated anchored bundle, constructed in Example
2.4
, and the space of prolongations of this anchored bundle includes into
(T^{2}.G_{2})
. Note that there is a well-formed involution map:

\sigma(u,v)=c\circ((0\circ p\circ v)^{-1};(T.0\circ u);v)

The direct proof of this involves a more conceptual construction, which is the focus of Section
5
.

An involution algebroid resembles a generalized tangent bundle, and so the lift
(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda):A\to\mathcal{L}(A)
satisfies the same universality conditions as
\ell:T\Rightarrow T^{2}
. The double linearity condition is equivalent to the naturality condition for
c,\ell
in Definition
3.2
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T.\hat{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T.\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\lambda}\times\ell}

(4)

which, using string diagrams for monoidal categories (
Selinger 2010
), is the equation

=

where the circle denotes the lift and
c
is the crossing of two lines.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle, and suppose that

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)

satisfies the involution axiom (i) and the symmetry of lift axiom (iii), and furthermore that the involution “exchanges” the idempotents associated to the two lifts
(\lambda\times\ell)
and
(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)
on
\mathcal{L}(A)
(Proposition
2.8
)

\sigma\circ((p\circ\lambda)\times(p\circ\ell))=(p\circ 0)\times(p\circ c\circ T.\lambda)=id\times(T.p\circ T.\lambda)

Then the double linearity axiom is equivalent to the left-hand commuting diagram in Diagram
4
:

(\hat{\lambda}\times\ell)\circ\sigma=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\times c)\circ(id\times T.\hat{\lambda}).

Proof.

Starting with the left-hand side of the equation,

\displaystyle\quad(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\times c)\circ(id\times T.\hat{\lambda})\circ(u,v)

\displaystyle=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\times c)\circ(u,T.\xi\circ T.\pi\circ v,T.\lambda v)

\displaystyle=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\circ(u,T.\xi\circ\varrho\circ u),T.\lambda v)

\displaystyle=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\xi\circ\pi\circ u,0\circ\varrho\circ u,T.\lambda v)

\displaystyle=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\xi\circ\pi\circ u,0\circ T.\pi\circ v,T.\lambda v)

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ\pi\circ u,T.\sigma\circ\hat{\lambda}\circ v).

For the right-hand side:

\displaystyle\quad(\hat{\lambda},\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v)

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ\pi\circ\pi_{0}\circ\sigma\circ(u,v),(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v))

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ\pi\circ p\circ v,(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v))

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ p\circ T.\pi\circ v,(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v))

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ p\circ\varrho\circ u,(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v))

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ\pi\circ u,(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma\circ(u,v))

\displaystyle=(\xi\circ\pi\circ u,T.\sigma\circ(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\circ(u,v)).

\pagenote

Added a step to clarify the calculation.
So the naturality equation
4
is equivalent to

T.\sigma\circ(0\times c\circ T.\lambda)=(\lambda\times\ell)\circ\sigma.

∎

The category of involution algebroids is “tangent monadic” over the category of anchored bundles, in the same sense that anchored bundles are tangent monadic over the category of differential bundles, or internal categories over reflexive graphs in a category. The “tangent monadicity” leads to a similar observation about
T
-limits of involution algebroids as in Observation
2.8
.

Observation 3.5
.

The forgetful functor from involution algebroids to anchored bundles creates limits; that is to say, the
T
-limits of the underlying anchored bundles give the limits of involution algebroids. Recall that by Observation
2.13
, the limit
\mathcal{L}(\lim A_{i})=\lim\mathcal{L}(A_{i})
in the category of anchored bundles, and this induces a map between objects in
\mathbb{C}

\lim\sigma_{i}:\lim\mathcal{L}(A_{i})\to\lim\mathcal{L}(A_{i}).

The axioms for an involution algebroid are induced by universality.

Note that a point-wise tangent structure may be defined following the anchored bundles example from Lemma
2.9
:
\pagenote
Added some preamble to this result to help differentiate it from the prolongation tangent structure.

Proposition 3.6
.

For a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, the category of involution algebroids has a “point-wise” tangent structure that maps objects as follows:

(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho,\sigma)\mapsto(T.\pi,T.\xi,c\circ T.\lambda,T.\varrho,\sigma_{T})

where
\sigma_{T}
is defined as

\sigma_{T}:=(1\times_{c}c)\circ T.\sigma\circ(1\times_{c}c):\mathcal{L}(A_{T})\to\mathcal{L}(A_{T}).

(5)

Proof.

The tangent structure on involution algebroids is inherited from the functor
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})
. Thus, it suffices to give the involution map for the tangent involution algebroid.

Note that given
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho,\sigma)
, the space of prolongations on
(T.\pi,T.\xi,\newline
c\circ T.\lambda,c\circ T.\varrho)
is
TA{{}_{c\circ T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}\pi}\!T^{2}A
. We can construct an isomorphism between the objects
\mathcal{L}(TA)
and
T(\mathcal{L}(A))
in
\mathbb{C}
using the cospan isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c\circ T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{2}.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

thus inducing a map

TA{{}_{c\circ T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}\pi}\!T^{2}A\xrightarrow{id\times c}T(A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA)\xrightarrow{T.\sigma}T(A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA)\xrightarrow{id\times c}TA{{}_{c\circ T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}\pi}\!T^{2}A

which we call
\sigma_{T}
. The linearity and involution axioms follow by construction.

Now check the rest of the axioms. For the unit:

\displaystyle(1\times_{c}c)\circ T.\sigma\circ(1\times_{c}c)\circ(T.(\xi\circ\pi),c\circ T.\lambda)

\displaystyle=(1\times_{c}c)\circ T.\sigma\circ(T(\xi\pi),T\lambda)=(1\times_{c}c)\circ(T.(\xi\circ\pi),T.\lambda)=(T.(\xi\circ\pi),c\circ T.\lambda).

For the anchor:

\displaystyle T.\varrho_{T}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma_{T}=T(c\circ T\varrho)\circ\pi_{1}\circ(1\times_{c}c)\circ T.\sigma\circ(1\times_{c}c)

\displaystyle=T.c\circ c\circ T^{2}.\varrho\circ T.\pi_{1}\circ T.\sigma\circ(1\times_{c}c)=T.c\circ c\circ T.c\circ T^{2}.\varrho\circ T.\pi_{1}\circ(1\times_{c}c)

\displaystyle=c\circ T.c\circ c\circ T^{2}\varrho\circ c\circ\pi_{1}=c\circ Tc\circ T^{2}.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}=c\circ T.\varrho_{T}\circ\pi_{1}.

The Yang–Baxter equation is straightforward to check.
∎

The tangent bundle is a canonical involution algebroid on every object in a tangent category, and the anchor induces a morphism from an involution algebroid to the tangent involution algebroid on its base space.
The anchor acts as a reflector from involution algebroids in
\mathbb{C}
to
\mathbb{C}
itself.

Proposition 3.7
.

Any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is a reflective subcategory of the category of involution algebroids in
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

First, observe that the inclusion of
\mathbb{C}
into
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
(Definition
3.1
)
\pagenote
The notation
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
had not yet been introduced at this point, it has been added to the definition of involution algebroids and a reference has been made to that definition. is fully faithful because the anchor on the tangent involution algebroid is
id:TM\to TM
. This means that the only involution algebroid morphisms
TA\to TB
are pairs
(Tf,f)
,
f:A\to B
.
Now consider the functor
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}
that sends
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho,\sigma)
to
M
: this gives an endofunctor
S:\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
along with a natural transformation
\varrho:id\Rightarrow S
, so that
S.\varrho=\varrho.S=id
, given by the anchor map.
Thus, the category
\mathbb{C}
is the category of algebras for an idempotent monad on
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Let

\widehat{A}=(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho,\sigma)

be an involution algebroid in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.

(i)

The morphism

(T.\pi,\pi):TA\to TM

is an involution algebroid morphism from the tangent involution algebroid on
A
to the tangent involution algebroid on
M
.

(ii)

The morphism

(\varrho,id):\widehat{A}\to TM

is an involution algebroid morphism.

If pullback powers of
p\circ\pi_{1}:\mathcal{L}(A)\to A
exist, then

(p\circ\pi_{1}:\mathcal{L}(A)\to A,(\xi\circ\pi,0),(\lambda\times\ell))

is a differential bundle
\pagenote
A “differentiable bundle” slipped in.; note that
\pi_{0}
acts as an anchor. If the prolongations exist, then

(p\circ\pi_{1}:\mathcal{L}(A)\to A,(\xi\circ\pi,0),(\lambda\times\ell),\pi_{0},\sigma^{\prime})

is an involution algebroid; this follows from computing the pullback in the category of involution algebroids (
\sigma^{\prime}
is induced as in Observation
2.8
).

The above corollary puts an involution algebroid structure on the differential bundle
(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)
. Note that the map
\sigma
gives an isomorphism of differential bundles

(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda).

Martinez observed that the canonical involution
\sigma
puts a unique Lie algebroid structure on
(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)
, and
\sigma
is a uniquely determined isomorphism of involution algebroids (see
de León et al. 2005
):

Corollary 3.9
.

For an involution algebroid
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho,\sigma)
, the isomorphism of differential bundles

\sigma:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T.\lambda)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)

induces a second involution algebroid structure on
\mathcal{L}(A)
.

Recall that Proposition
2.14
sketched out a proof that the category of anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
has an endofunctor
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
and natural transformations
p^{\prime},0^{\prime},+^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}
satisfying the axioms of a tangent structure; this endofunctor and the natural transformations all lift to
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
. The involution map
\sigma
is the missing piece that gives a tangent structure on
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
. The construction may be spelled out here at a big-picture level, but the actual proof brings up tricky coherence issues that make up the bulk of Chapter
7
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The category of involution algebroids in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
has a second tangent structure, where the tangent functor is given by
\pagenote
Changed wording to help differentiate this structure against the pointwise tangent structure defined beforehand.

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})

and the tangent natural transformations are given as in Proposition
2.14
, with the canonical flip

\sigma^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{1\times T.\sigma}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A).

Starting with an involution algebroid,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)
are given by

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)\begin{cases}\pi^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{p\circ\pi_{1}}A\\
\xi^{\prime}:&A\xrightarrow{(\xi\circ\pi,0)}\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\lambda^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\lambda\times\ell}T.\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\varrho^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}TA\\
\sigma^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\sigma\times c}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\end{cases}\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)\begin{cases}\pi^{\prime\prime}&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(p\circ\pi_{1},p\circ\pi_{2}):}\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\xi^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{(\xi\circ\pi\circ\pi_{0},0\circ\pi_{1},0\circ\pi_{2})}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\lambda^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(\lambda\times\ell\times\ell)}T.\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\varrho^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}T.\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\sigma^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{3}(A)\xrightarrow{(\sigma\times c\times c)}\mathcal{L}^{3}(A)\end{cases}

(where
\mathcal{L}^{3}(A)=A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T.(A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA)
).
The tangent natural transformations are given by

•

p:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow id;\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}A

•

0:id\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime};\hskip 4.26773ptA\xrightarrow{(id,T.\xi\circ T.\pi)}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)

•

+:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{2}\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime};\hskip 4.26773ptA{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T.\pi\circ\pi_{i}}\!T_{2}A\xrightarrow{id\times+}\mathcal{L}(A)

•

\ell:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime};\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{1\times T.(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda)}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)

•

\varrho^{\prime}:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\Rightarrow T

•

c:\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime};\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{1\times T.\sigma}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)
.

Proof.

Deferred to
