.
∎

4
Connections on an involution algebroid

In this section we take an involution algebroid with a chosen linear connection on its underlying anchored bundle (Definition
3.1
,
6.1
), and rederive Martinez’s structure equations for a Lie algebroid (Proposition
1.5
).

In a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
with negatives, there is a natural transformation
\pagenote
Clarifying notation for this section as there is substantially more fibered linear algebra, including the use of substraction, than is present in other sections/chapters.

n:T\Rightarrow T

making each fibred commutative monoid
(p:TM\Rightarrow M,0,+,n)
a fibred abelian group. Because the additive bundle structure on differential bundles is induced via universality (Proposition
4.4
), in a tangent category with negatives the additive bundle structure for differential bundles will likewise have negatives.
We adopt the following notation for the “fibred linear algebra” used in this section, as there are a significant number of computations done on bundles with multiple choices of additive bundle structure (e.g. the second tangent bundle of a differential bundle has three additive bundles structures).

Notation 4.1
.

Let
E
be an object with multiple differential bundle structures
(\pi^{i}:E\to M^{i},\xi^{i},\lambda^{i})
in a tangent category with negatives. Addition over a specific differential bundle is written using infix notation, where a subscript is added to the symbol denoting the projection of the differential bundle. Letting
x,y:X\to E
denote a pair of generalized elements for which the
\pi^{i}
-addition operation is well-defined; we set

x+_{\pi[i]}y=X\xrightarrow{(x,y)}E{{}_{\pi[i]}}\!\times_{\pi[i]}\!E\xrightarrow{+^{i}}E.

Similar notation is used for subtraction:

x-_{\pi[i]}y=X\xrightarrow{(x,y)}E{{}_{\pi[i]}}\!\times_{\pi[i]}\!E\xrightarrow{id\times n[i]}E{{}_{\pi[i]}}\!\times_{\pi[i]}\!E\xrightarrow{+^{i}}E.

Throughout this section, we work in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
with negatives
\pagenote
I had originally forgotten to add the “with negatives” caveat and a chosen anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
, equipped with a connection
(\kappa,\nabla)
, whose base object has a torsion-free connection
(\kappa^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime})
and a morphism

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)

that exchanges the two projection maps
p\circ\pi_{1},\pi_{0}
, meaning that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}

The following notation will be useful when working with local coordinates.

Notation 4.2
.

First, recall the
\nabla
-notation for morphisms of differential bundle where each morphism has a choice of connection from Equation
3
:

\nabla[f]:=\kappa^{B}\circ T.f\circ\nabla^{A}:A_{2}\to B\lx@proof@logical@and f:A\to B(\kappa^{A},\nabla^{A},A,\lambda^{A})(\kappa^{B},\nabla^{B},B,\lambda^{B})

Let

(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho),(q:B\to N,\zeta,l,\rho)

be a pair of anchored bundles equipped with connections.
“Hatting” a map
f:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(B)
refers to the map:

\widehat{f}:A_{3}\xrightarrow{\hat{\nu}(\pi_{0},\pi_{2})+\hat{\nabla}(\pi_{0},\pi_{1})}\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{L}(B)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{0},p\circ\pi_{1},\kappa\circ\pi_{1})}B_{3}.

Similarly, for
f:TA\to TB
,

\widehat{f}:TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A\xrightarrow{\nu(\pi_{0},\pi_{2})+\nabla(\pi_{0},\pi_{1})}TA\xrightarrow{f}TB\xrightarrow{(\pi_{0},p\circ\pi_{1},\kappa\circ\pi_{1})}TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{q}\!B{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{q}\!B

Clearly,
\widehat{f\circ g}=\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{g}
. Similarly, a map
A_{3}\to B_{3}
may be “barred” to form a map
\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(B)
:

\overline{g}:\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{0},p\circ\pi_{1},\kappa\circ\pi_{1})}A_{3}\xrightarrow{g}B_{3}\xrightarrow{\hat{\nu}(\pi_{0},\pi_{2})+\hat{\nabla}(\pi_{0},\pi_{1})}\mathcal{L}(B).

It is straightforward to see that
\overline{\widehat{f}}=f,\widehat{\overline{g}}=g
.

Lemma 4.3
.

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)
induces a bracket on
\Gamma(\pi)
:

\lambda\circ[X,Y]=(\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{T\pi}(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X)-0\circ Y.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in\Gamma(\pi)
and compute:

\displaystyle\quad p\circ\pi_{1}\circ(\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{\pi_{0}}(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X)

\displaystyle=p\circ(\pi_{1}\circ\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{T\pi}TY\circ\varrho\circ X)

\displaystyle=p\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{\pi}p\circ TY\circ\varrho\circ X

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-Y\circ p\circ\varrho\circ X

\displaystyle=Y-Y=\xi,

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\circ(\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{\pi_{0}}(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{\pi}\pi_{0}\circ(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X

\displaystyle=p\circ\pi_{1}\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{\pi}X

\displaystyle=p\circ TX\circ\varrho\circ Y-_{\pi}X=X-_{\pi}X=\xi.

The universality of the lift induces a new section
[X,Y]
so that

\lambda\circ[X,Y]=(\sigma\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{T\pi}(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X)-0Y.

∎

Definition 4.4
.

The map
\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A)
is
linear
whenever the two bundle morphisms

\sigma:(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T\lambda)\hskip 7.11317pt\sigma:(\mathcal{L}(A),0\times c\circ T\lambda)\to(\mathcal{L}(A),\lambda\times\ell)

are linear, and
cosymmetric
if

\sigma\circ\widehat{\lambda}=\widehat{\lambda}=(\xi\pi,\lambda).

Note that whenever
\sigma
is linear and cosymmetric,
\sigma\circ\hat{\mu}(u,v)=\hat{\nu}(u,v)
.

Linearity and unit axioms, along with the connection on the differential bundle, force the existence of a bilinear bracket
\langle-,-\rangle
as in the definition of a Lie algebroid in Proposition
1.13
.

Proposition 4.5
.

For an anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
with connection
(\kappa,\nabla)
, a cosymmetric and bilinear
\sigma
is equivalent to a bilinear
\langle-,-\rangle:A_{2}\to A
, with the correspondence given by

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow[\overline{(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2}+\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle)}]{}\mathcal{L}(A)\langle-,-\rangle:\kappa\circ\sigma\circ\nabla-_{\pi}\kappa\circ\nabla

Proof.

Derive

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}\circ(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=(y,x,\kappa\circ\sigma\circ(\nabla(\varrho x,y)+_{p\circ\pi_{1}}\mu(y,z)))

\displaystyle=(y,x,\kappa\circ\sigma\circ\nabla(\varrho x,y)+_{\pi}\kappa\circ\sigma\circ\mu(y,z))

\displaystyle=(y,x,\kappa\circ\sigma\circ\nabla(\varrho x,y)+_{\pi}\kappa\circ\nu\circ(y,z))

\displaystyle=(y,x,\kappa\circ\sigma\circ\nabla(\varrho x,y)+_{\pi}z)

\displaystyle=:(y,x,\langle x,y\rangle+_{\pi}z)

where
\langle-,-\rangle
is certainly bilinear. The converse is immediate: take

\widehat{\sigma}(u,v,w):=(v,u,w+\langle u,v\rangle).

It is easy to see that
\sigma
is linear and cosymmetric.
∎

The linear bracket must be involutive for the bilinear bracket to be alternating.

Proposition 4.6
.

If
\sigma
is cosymmetric and linear, then the bilinear bracket
\langle-,-\rangle
is alternating if and only if
\sigma\circ\sigma=id
.

Proof.

First, note that
\sigma\circ\sigma=id
if and only if
\widehat{\sigma}\circ\widehat{\sigma}=id
.
Then check that

\widehat{\sigma}\circ\widehat{\sigma}(u,v,w)=\widehat{\sigma}(v,u,w+\langle u,v\rangle)=(u,v,w+\langle u,v\rangle+\langle v,u\rangle).

By the cancellativity of addition on
A
,

w=w+\langle u,v\rangle+\langle v,u\rangle\iff 0=\langle u,v\rangle+\langle v,u\rangle.

∎

Observation 4.7
.

A bilinear
\langle-,-\rangle
on an anchored bundle with a connection induces the maps

\{v,x\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\kappa^{\prime}\circ T.\varrho\circ\nabla(v,x),\quad\{v,x,y\}_{(\kappa,\nabla)}:=\kappa\circ T(\langle-,-\rangle_{(\kappa,\nabla)})\circ\nabla^{A_{2}}\circ(v,x,y)

from
Equation

2
in Proposition
1.13
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\sigma
be cosymmetric and bilinear, with the associated bracket
\langle-,-\rangle
. Then

T\varrho\circ\pi_{1}\circ\sigma=c\circ T\varrho\circ\pi_{1}

if and only if the Leibniz equation is satisfied:

\varrho\circ\langle u,v\rangle+\{\varrho v,u\}=\{\varrho u,v.\}

(6)

Proof.

Following the given notation and using the hypothesis that the connection is torsion-free on
M
,

\widehat{T\varrho\circ\pi_{1}}(u,v,w)=(\varrho u,\varrho v,w+\{u,v\})\hskip 14.22636pt\widehat{c}(x,y,z)=(y,x,z).

Computing each side,

\displaystyle\widehat{T\varrho}\circ\widehat{\pi_{1}}\circ\widehat{\sigma}\circ(u,v,w)

\displaystyle=\widehat{T\varrho}\circ\widehat{\pi_{1}}\circ(v,u,w+\langle u,v\rangle)

\displaystyle=\widehat{T\varrho}\circ(\varrho v,u,w+\langle u,v\rangle)

\displaystyle=(\varrho v,\varrho u,\varrho w+\varrho\langle u,v\rangle+D[\varrho](\varrho v,u))

\displaystyle=(\varrho v,\varrho u,\varrho w+\varrho\langle u,v\rangle+\{v,u\}),

\displaystyle\widehat{c}\circ\widehat{T\varrho}\circ\widehat{\pi_{1}}(u,v,w)

\displaystyle=\widehat{c}(\varrho u,\varrho v,\varrho w+\{u,v\})

\displaystyle=(\varrho v,\varrho u,\varrho w+\{u,v\}),

so it follows that the two terms are equal if and only if the desired equality holds.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\sigma
be linear and cosymmetric.
Then

(i)

T(\langle-,-\rangle):TA_{2}\to TA
satisfies

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{T(\langle-,-\rangle)}(a_{x},u_{y},u_{z},u_{xy},u_{xz})

\displaystyle=(a_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle);

(ii)

T.\sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{(id\times T(\widehat{\sigma}))}(u_{x},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{yz},u_{xyz})

\displaystyle=(u_{x},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,

\displaystyle\quad u_{xyz}+\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle).

Proof.

(i)

By the universality of the vertical lift and bilinearity of
\langle-,-\rangle
, the outer squares below are pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{A_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle,\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{3}\rangle+\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\langle,\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi\circ T\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

so that

T(\langle,\rangle)(0,u_{y},u_{z},u_{xy},u_{xz})=\mu^{A}(\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle).

Now we compute

\displaystyle\quad T(\langle,\rangle)(\mu^{A_{2}}((u_{y},y_{z}),(u_{xy},u_{xz}))+_{p}\nabla^{A_{2}}(a_{x},(u_{y},u_{z})))

\displaystyle=T(\langle,\rangle)\mu^{A_{2}}((u_{y},u_{z}),(u_{xy},u_{xz}))+_{p}T(\langle,\rangle)\circ\nabla(a_{x},(u_{y},u_{z}))

\displaystyle=\mu^{A}(\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle)+_{p}T(\langle,\rangle)\circ\nabla(a_{x},(u_{y},u_{z}))

and then postcompose this with
(T\pi,p,\kappa)
to obtain

\displaystyle\quad(0,\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\ \langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle)+_{p}(a_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\})

\displaystyle=(a_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle).

(ii)

Consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A_{3}{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A_{3}{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(A_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\circ\mathcal{L}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\circ\mathcal{L}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{T\widehat{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{A^{3}}+_{p}\mu^{A^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2}+\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\nabla^{\prime}+_{\pi_{0}}\mu^{\prime}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T(\pi^{3}),p,\kappa^{A_{3}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\pi_{0},p\circ\pi_{1},\kappa\circ\pi_{1})}

We want to find
\widehat{T\widehat{\sigma}}=\widehat{T(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2}+\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle)}
.
Note that

T(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2}+_{\pi}\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle)=T(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2})+_{T\pi}T(\xi\pi\pi_{i},\pi_{0},\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle).

The left term is straightforward:

\displaystyle\widehat{T(\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}(a_{x},(u_{y},u_{xy}),(u_{z},u_{xz}),(u_{yz},u_{xyz}))

\displaystyle=(a_{x},(u_{z},u_{xz}),(u_{y},u_{xy}),(u_{yz},u_{xyz}))

and for the right term, use part (i) of this lemma:

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{T\langle-,-\rangle}\circ\widehat{T(\pi_{0},\pi_{1}))}(a_{x},(u_{y},u_{xy}),(u_{z},u_{xz}),(u_{yz},u_{xyz}))

\displaystyle=\widehat{T\langle-,-\rangle}(a_{x},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{z},u_{xyz})

\displaystyle=(a_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle)

Then compute

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{T\widehat{\sigma}}(a_{x},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{yz},u_{xyz})

\displaystyle=(a_{x},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,u_{xyz}+q)

where
q=\{a_{x},u_{y},y_{z}\}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle
,
giving the desired equation.

∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\sigma
be cosymmetric, doubly linear, and involutive, and satisfy the target axiom.
Then
\sigma
satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation if and only if
\langle-,-\rangle
and
\{-,-,-\}
satisfy the Bianchi identity:

0=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\langle x_{\gamma_{0}},\langle x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}\rangle\rangle+\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Cy}(3)}\{\varrho x_{\gamma_{0}},x_{\gamma_{1}},x_{\gamma_{2}}.\}

(7)

Proof.

We expand
\widehat{id\times T\sigma}
and
\widehat{\sigma\times c}
.
Start with
T(id\times\sigma)
, which was derived in Lemma
4.9
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\widehat{(id\times T(\widehat{\sigma}))}(u_{x},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{yz},u_{xyz})

\displaystyle=(u_{x},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,

\displaystyle\quad u_{xyz}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}).

Using the fact that
\kappa^{\prime}
is torsion free, so

\hat{c}(u_{xz},u_{yz},u_{xzy})=(u_{yz},u_{xz},u_{xyz})
, it follows that

\sigma_{2}(u_{x},u_{y},u_{xy},u_{z},u_{xz},u_{yz},u_{xyz})=(u_{y},u_{x},u_{xy}+\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,u_{z},u_{yz},u_{xz},u_{xyz}).

Then compute

\displaystyle\quad\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\big(u_{z},u_{y},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,u_{x},u_{xz}+\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{xy}+\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,z_{1}\big),

\displaystyle\quad\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=\big(u_{z},u_{y},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle,u_{x},u_{xz}+\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{xy}+\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,z_{2}\big).

Note that the first five terms are equal, so it suffices to check
z_{1}=z_{2}
for
z_{1}=\pi_{6}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}(u),z_{2}=\pi_{6}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}(u)
.

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}+\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle u_{xz}+\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{y},u_{xz}\rangle+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle u_{xz},u_{y}\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle,

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{x},u_{z}\}+\langle u_{xy}+\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,u_{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{x},u_{z}\}+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,u_{z}\rangle.

So
z_{1}=z_{2}
is equivalent to requiring

\displaystyle\quad u_{xyz}+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}

\displaystyle\quad+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{xyz}+\langle u_{x},u_{yz}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{x},u_{z}\}+\langle u_{xy},u_{z}\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,u_{z}\rangle;

cancelling alike terms, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\quad\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}

\displaystyle=\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{x},u_{z}\}+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle,u_{z}\rangle.

Using the fact that
\langle-,-\rangle
and
\{-,-,-\}
are alternating in the last two arguments, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle\langle u_{x},u_{z}\rangle,u_{y}\rangle+\langle u_{z},\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{z},u_{x}\}

\displaystyle=\langle u_{x},\langle u_{y},u_{z}\rangle\rangle+\langle u_{y},\langle u_{z},u_{x}\rangle\rangle+\langle u_{z},\langle u_{x},u_{y}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\{\varrho\circ u_{x},u_{y},u_{z}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{z},u_{x},u_{y}\}+\{\varrho\circ u_{y},u_{z},u_{x}\}

giving the desired identity.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle in a tangent category with negatives, with anchored connection
(\nabla,\kappa)
on
A
and torsion-free affine connection
(\nabla^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
on
M
.
An involution algebroid structure on
A
is equivalent to a bilinear map

\langle-,-\rangle:A_{2}\to A

with derived maps

\displaystyle\{-,-\}:A{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{p}\!TM\to TM:=\kappa^{\prime}\circ T\varrho\circ(\pi_{0},\pi_{1}),

\displaystyle\{-,-,-\}:TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{\pi\circ\pi_{i}}\!A_{2}\to A;\{a,u_{1},u_{2}\}:=\kappa\circ T(\langle-,-\rangle)\circ(\nabla(a,u_{1}),\nabla(a,u_{2}))

satisfying

(i)

\langle-,-\rangle
is linear and cosymmetric,

(ii)

\langle-,-\rangle
is alternating,

(iii)

\langle-,-\rangle
and
\{-,-\}
satisfy the Leibniz equation, Equation
6
.

(iv)

\langle-,-\rangle,\{-,-\},
and
\{-,-,-\}
satisfy the Bianchi identity,
Equation

7
.

Morphisms of involution algebroids may also be characterized by preservation of the tensor.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
A,B
be a pair of involution algebroids with chosen connections in a tangent category with negatives.
Then an anchored bundle morphism
f:A\to B
is an involution algebroid morphism if and only if (recalling the notation from Equation
3
)

\nabla[f](x,y)+\langle f\circ x,f\circ y\rangle=\nabla[f](y,x)+f\circ\langle x,y\rangle.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ\mathcal{L}(f)=\mathcal{L}(f)\circ\sigma)

\displaystyle\iff(\pi_{1},\pi_{0},\pi_{2}+\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1}\rangle)\circ(f\circ x,f\circ y,f\circ z+\nabla[f](x,y))

\displaystyle=(f,f,f\circ\pi_{2}+\nabla[f](\pi_{0},\pi_{1}))\circ(y,x,z+\langle x,y\rangle)

while the second condition reduces to

\nabla[f](x,y)+\langle f\circ x,f\circ y\rangle=\nabla[f](y,x)+f\circ\langle x,y\rangle.

∎

5
The isomorphism of Lie and involution algebroid categories

Sections
1
and
4
have made the relationship between involution algebroids and Lie algebroids clear. It is important to note that while the proofs used connections as a tool to identify the local coherences satisfied by involution and Lie algebroids, the construction of a Lie algebroid from an involution algebroid (or vice versa) is independent of the choice of connection.

Theorem 5.1
.

There is an isomorphism of categories between Lie algebroids and involution algebroids in smooth manifolds.

Proof.

For the equivalence of categories, note that by Propositions
1.13
and
1.14
, Corollary
4.11
, and Proposition
4.12
there is an isomorphism of categories between involution algebroids with a choice of connection and Lie algebroids with a choice of connection (morphisms are
not
restricted to connection preserving morphisms). This allows us to chain together isomorphisms

\mathsf{Inv}(\mathsf{SMan})\cong\mathsf{Inv}(\mathsf{SMan})_{\mathsf{ChosenConn}}\cong\mathsf{LieAlgd}_{\mathsf{ChosenConn}}\cong\mathsf{LieAlgd}.

\pagenote

One thing that was unclear in the original proof of this statement is that while connections are helpful for the nuts and bolts of this proof, the actual constructions do not rely on connections.

To complete the proof, we must show that the assignment that sends an involution algebroid to a Lie algebroid whose bracket is given by

\lambda\circ[X,Y]^{*}=\left((\pi_{1}\circ\sigma\circ(id,T.X\circ\varrho)\circ Y-_{p}T.Y\circ\varrho\circ)-_{T.\pi}0\circ Y\right),

(8)

is a bijection on objects, which brings up some subtleties. First, while an involution map

\sigma:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA

is defined with respect to a particular choice of pullback
A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA
, the category of involution algebroids
does not
distinguish between different choices of this pullback (and therefore different representations of the map
\sigma
), and it is not part of the data of an involution algebroid. It is immediate by universality that the left-hand-side of Equation
8
is independent of the choice of pullback
A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA
.

Now, recall that the canonical involution of a Lie algebroid is uniquely by Theorem 4.7 of
de León et al. 2005
(this was also mentioned in Corollary
3.9
). Once we make a choice of prolongation
A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA
, we have made a choice of pullback
\mathcal{L}(A)
in
\mathsf{LieAlgd}
, which uniquely determines the canonical involution

\sigma:A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA\to A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA.

While the exact map
\sigma
depends on the choice of pullback
A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA
, they all determine the same involution algebroid, thus proving the bijection correspondence of objects.
∎

Chapter 7
The Weil nerve of an algebroid

\pagenote

This chapter has been given a new introduction to help with its exposition.
Various typos have been fixed, but the most substantial changes are in Section
3
, where the proof has been restructured to address some concerns brought up in Michael’s comments.

The first three chapters of this thesis demonstrated that tangent categories allow for an essentially algebraic description of Lie algebroids by axiomatizing the behaviour of the tangent bundle, and showing that a Lie algebroid over a manifold
M
is a “generalized tangent bundle”, namely an
involution algebroid
. This chapter will make precise the sense in which an involution algebroid is a generalized tangent bundle, by showing that the category of involution algebroids in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is equivalent to a certain tangent-functor category from the free tangent category over a single object to
\mathbb{C}
, or more generally that there is a fully faithful functor

\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Tang_{Lax}}(\mathsf{FreeTangCat}(*),\mathbb{C}).

This functor, the
Weil nerve
of an involution algebroid, builds a functor from the free tangent category over a single object to
\mathbb{C}
using a span construction. This chapter primarily builds on two pieces of work: Leung’s construction of the free tangent category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
(
Leung 2017
) , and Grothendieck’s original nerve construction (first published in
Segal 1974
).

To understand Leung’s construction of the free tangent category, and more generally his actegory-theoretic presentation of tangent categories (Section
2
), we first look at Weil’s original insight relating the kinematic and operational descriptions of the tangent bundle in
\mathsf{SMan}
. The definition of a tangent vector on a manifold
M
as an equivalence class of curves (Definition
2.8
) puts a bijective correspondence between tangent vectors and
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphisms from the ring of smooth functions
C^{\infty}(M)
to the ring of dual numbers:

C^{\infty}(M)\to\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}.

The hom-set
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{Alg}(C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2})
is precisely the set of
derivations
on
C^{\infty}(M)
, which defines the operational tangent bundle discussed in Definition
2.16
: there is a natural smooth manifold structure on this set. The Weil functor formalism, most notably developed in
Kolár et al. 1993
, extends this observation to a general class of endofunctors on
\mathsf{SMan}
. For example, the fibre product
T_{2}M
corresponds to
\mathbb{R}
-algebra morphisms,

C^{\infty}(M)\to R[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2},xy),

while the second tangent bundle corresponds to
\mathbb{R}
-algebra morphisms into the tensor product,

C^{\infty}(M)\to R[x]/(x^{2})\otimes R[y]/(y^{2})=R[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2}).

By applying Milnor’s exercise (Problem 1-C
Milnor and Stasheff 1974
), which states that the
C^{\infty}
functor

\mathsf{SMan}\to\mathbb{R}\mathsf{Alg^{op}};\hskip 4.26773ptM\mapsto C^{\infty}(M)=\mathsf{SMan}(M,\mathbb{R})

is fully faithful, the structure maps occur as natural transformations. For example, the tangent projection is induced by the morphism

{p}:\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}\xrightarrow{a+bx\mapsto a}\mathbb{R},

so that

TM\xrightarrow{p}M=[C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}]\xrightarrow{(p)_{*}}[C^{\infty}(M),\mathbb{R}].

The zero map and addition are similarly induced by

{0}:\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{a\mapsto a+0x}R[x]/x^{2}\text{ and }+:R[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2},xy)\xrightarrow[\mapsto a+(b+c)x]{a+bx+cy}R[x]/x^{2},

respectively, while the lift and flip are induced by the morphisms

{\ell}:\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}\xrightarrow[\mapsto a+bxy]{a+bx}\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})

and

{c}:\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})\xrightarrow[\mapsto a+cx+by+dxy]{a+bx+cy+dxy}\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2}).

More generally, there is a monoidal category of
Weil algebras
(Definition
1.1
) which has a monoidal action on the category of smooth manifolds. The Weil functor formalism, then, studies differential geometric structures from the perspective of the endofunctors and natural transformations induced by this action. Leung’s insight is that there is an analogous category of commutative rigs
1
1

1

A rig is a ri
n
g without
n
egatives, i.e. a commutative monoid equipped with a bilinear multiplication.
built by replacing
\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}
with
\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}
, called
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
(Definition
1.3
); a tangent structure is precisely a monoidal action by
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
satisfying some universal properties. In particular, this category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is precisely the free tangent category over a point,
\mathsf{FreeTang}(*)
, so that every object
A
in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
determines a strict tangent functor

T^{(-)}A:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C};V\mapsto T^{V}A

and morphisms
f:A\to B
are in bijective correspondence with tangent-natural transformations
T^{(-)}A\Rightarrow T^{(-)}(B)
.

The axioms of an involution algebroid in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
correspond bijectively with those of the tangent bundle - this suggests that an involution algebroid should determine a tangent functor from
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
to
\mathbb{C}
. A first guess would lead one to think that
p:\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
is sent to
\pi:A\to M
,
0
to
\xi
, and
+
to
+_{A}
. As the space of prolongations
\mathcal{L}(A)=A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA
plays the role of the second tangent bundle, we can see that

\ell:N[x]/x^{2}\to N[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})\mapsto(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda):A\to\mathcal{L}(A),

and

c:N[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})\to N[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})\mapsto\sigma\mathcal{L}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(A).

This pattern may be neatly summed up using span composition - we will construct a functor that sends
\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}
to the span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

and the tensor product
\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}\otimes\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}
to the span composition (e.g. the pullback)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

which is the space of prolongations. This leads to the first major result of this chapter, the
Weil Nerve
(Theorem
3.9
), which states there is a fully faithful embedding

N_{\mathsf{Weil}}:\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}].

This bears a strong similarity to Grothendieck’s original nerve theorem, which takes an internal category
s,t:C\to M
and constructs a functor
\Delta^{op}\to\mathbb{C}
(where
\Delta^{op}
is the monoidal theory of an internal monoid) by sending tensor to span composition, and the composition and unit maps given span morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

while the unit and associativity axioms for a category are exactly the unit and associativity laws for a monoid in this setting. The
Segal conditions
identify exactly the functors
C:\Delta^{op}\to\mathbb{C}
that lie in the image of the nerve functor
N
as those whose
[n]^{th}
object is sent to the wide pullback
C([n])=C[2]{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C[2]\dots{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C[2]
. The corresponding result for involution algebroids is found in Theorem
4.8
, which states that a tangent functor
(A,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is the nerve of an involution algebroid if and only if
A
preserves tangent limits and
\alpha
is a
T
-cartesian natural transformation (this forces
A.(W^{\otimes n})=A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T.\pi}\!TA\dots{{}_{T^{n-1}\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{n}\pi}\!T^{n}A
). The similarity between the Weil nerve and Grothendieck/Segal’s nerve runs deep, and in Chapter
8
we demonstrate that the
enriched
perspective on tangent categories puts these both into the same formal framework.

Sections
1
and
2
give a detailed introduction to the Weil functor formalism (
Kolár et al. 1993
,
Bertram and Souvay 2014
) and Leung’s unification of Weil functors with tangent categories
Leung 2017
. The rest of the chapter contains contains new results developed by the author. Section
3
proves the embedding part of the Weil nerve theorem, that the category of involution algebroids embeds into the category of tangent functors and tangent natural transformations
[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]
. Section
4
identifies exactly those tangent functors
(A,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
that are the nerve of an involution algebroid, completing the proof of the Weil nerve theorem. Section
5
uses the Weil nerve to develop a novel tangent structure on the category of involution algebroids in a tangent category (in particular, the category of Lie algebroids will have this novel tangent structure).

1
Weil algebras and tangent structure

This section gives a more thorough introduction to the Weil functor formalism of
Kolár et al. 1993
, and in particular how the structure maps of a tangent category may be teased out of it. We begin by introducing Weil algebras, and the
prolongation
of a smooth manifold by a Weil algebra. (The relationship with prolongations of involution algebroids from Definition
2.1
will be made clear in Section
3
.)

Definition 1.1
.

An
\mathbb{R}
-Weil algebra
2
2

2

Not to be confused with the normal usage of “Weil algebra” in Lie theory, e.g.
Meinrenken and Pike 2021
.
is a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
V
so that
V=\mathbb{R}\oplus\dot{V}
as
\mathbb{R}
-modules and
\dot{V}
is a nilpotent ideal. The category
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{Weil}
is the full subcategory of
\mathbb{R}\mathsf{Alg}
spanned by the
\mathbb{R}
-Weil algebras. The
prolongation
of a manifold by a Weil algebra
V
is given by the manifold

T^{V}M:=\mathbb{R}\mathsf{Alg}(C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}),V).

Eck 1986
showed that every product-preserving endofunctor on the category of smooth manifolds is constructed as the Weil prolongation by some Weil algebra. Consequently,
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphisms induce natural transformations between these product-preserving endofunctors on the category of smooth manifolds.

Example 1.2
.

Consider the following
\mathbb{R}
-Weil algebras and their associated prolongation functors.

(i)

Prolongation by
\mathbb{R}
induces the identity functor, and the tangent bundle is given by
\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}
. The tangent projection, then, is equivalent to the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra morphism

p:\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}\to\mathbb{R};\hskip 5.69046ptp(a+bx)=a

while the 0-map induces the zero vector field:

0:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2};\hskip 5.69046pt0(a)=a+0x.

(ii)

The algebra
\mathbb{R}[x_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}/(x_{i}x_{j})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}=(\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2})^{n}
is the wide pullback
T_{n}M=TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{p}\!TM\dots{{}_{p}}\!\times_{p}\!TM
.
In particular, prolongation by
\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2},xy)
gives the bundle
T_{2}M=TM{{}_{p}}\!\times_{p}\!TM
. The
\mathbb{R}
-algebra morphism

+:\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2},xy)\to R[x]/x^{2};\hskip 5.69046pt+(a_{0}+a_{1}x+a_{2}y)=a_{0}+(a_{1}+a_{2})x

corresponds to the addition of tangent vectors.

(iii)

The algebra
\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2})=(\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2})\otimes(\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2})
is the second tangent bundle
T^{2}M=TTM
. The vertical lift
T\to T^{2}
is induced by the morphism

\ell:\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}\to\mathbb{R}[x,y]/(x^{2},y^{2});\hskip 5.69046pt\ell(a+bx)=a+bxy.

(iv)

The monoidal symmetry map induces
c:T^{2}\Rightarrow T^{2}
, as follows:

\displaystyle c:(\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2})\otimes(\mathbb{R}[y]/y^{2})\to(\mathbb{R}[y]/y^{2})\otimes(\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2});

\displaystyle c(a+b_{1}x+b_{2}y+b_{3}xy)=a+b_{2}x+b_{1}y+b_{3}xy.

(v)

For
n\geq 2
, the algebra
\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{n}
gives the
n
-jet bundle.
Note that this is the equalizer of
\otimes^{n}\mathbb{R}[x]/x^{2}
by the symmetry actions of
S_{n}
.

Further examples may be found in the monograph
Kolár et al. 1993
. Tangent categories bridge the gap between the Weil functor approach to studying the differential geometry of smooth manifolds and the synthetic differential geometry approach of axiomatizing a tangent bundle using nilpotent infinitesimals. The main structure axiomatized here is that of
monoidal action
of a symmetric monoidal category on a category
M\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
, or equivalently, a lift from a category to the category of complexes
\mathbb{C}\to[M,\mathbb{C}]
, which involves translating a bit of classical category theory to the 2-categorical setting.

Example
1.2
leads to the classical theorem that the category of smooth manifolds has an action by the category of
\mathbb{R}
-Weil algebras that preserves all connected limits that exist. These “natural” universal properties (in the sense of
Kolár et al. 1993
) is foundational to synthetic differential geometry; see, for example, Chapter Two of
Lavendhomme 1996
. Unfortunately, Weil algebras are not an ideal syntactic presentation: they are not a finitely presentable category, and it is not immediately clear when a diagram is a connected limit.
3
3

3

It should be noted that
Nishimura and Osoekawa 2007
made progress applying techniques from computer algebra to latter problem.
Moving from
\mathbb{R}
-algebras to commutative rigs and restricting to an appropriate subcategory solves this problem.
\pagenote
Clarified a point raised by Michael

Definition 1.3

(Definition 3.1
Leung 2017
)
.

The category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is defined to be the full subcategory of commutative rigs,
\mathsf{CRig}
, generated by the rig of dual numbers
W:=\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}
, constructed as follows:

1.

Start with finite product powers of
W
in
\mathsf{CRig}
, and make a strict choice of presentation:

W_{0}=\mathbb{N},\hskip 4.26773ptW_{n}:=\mathbb{N}[x_{i}]/(x_{i}x_{j})_{i\leq j},{0\leq i<n}.

2.

Then take the closure of
W_{n}
under coproduct of commutative rigs, written
\otimes
. Again, make a strict choice of presentation:

W_{n(0)}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{n(m-1)}:=\mathbb{N}[x_{i,j}]/(x_{ij}x_{ik})_{j\leq k},0<i<m,0<j<n(i).

Note that we will often suppress the tensor product
\otimes
and simply write
\pagenote
explained notation used throughout this chapter

The coproduct of Weil algebras is the tensor product of the underlying commutative monoids, which are finite-dimensional and free, so these limits are closed under
\otimes
.

Proposition 1.6

(

.

The category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is a tangent category, where the tangent functor is

T:=W\otimes(\_):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}

and the natural transformations are given by

\displaystyle p:W\otimes(\_)\xrightarrow{p\otimes(\_)}(\_),\hskip 7.11317pt0:(\_)\xrightarrow{0\otimes(\_)}W\otimes(\_),\hskip 7.11317pt+:W_{2}\otimes(\_)\xrightarrow{+\otimes(\_)}W\otimes(\_),

\displaystyle\ell:W\otimes(\_)\xrightarrow{\ell\otimes(\_)}W\otimes W\otimes(\_),\hskip 7.11317ptc:W\otimes W\otimes(\_)\xrightarrow{p\otimes(\_)}W\otimes W\otimes(\_)).

The category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is, in some sense, a finitely presented theory. It is precisely the free tangent category on a single object:

.

The category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}

so that there is an isomorphism of categories between

with tangent natural transformations as morphisms.

Notation 1.9
.

Throughout this section, the notation
T^{V}C
will refer to the action of the Weil algebra
V
on an object
C
in a tangent category.
In particular, we will make use of the isomorphism
T^{U}.T^{V}C=T^{UV}C
.

2
Tangent structures as monoidal actions

The presentation of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
as the free tangent category situates the formal theory of tangent categories as an instance of more general categorical machinery, namely monoidal actions. Recall that in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{C},\otimes,I)
, an internal monoid
(C,\bullet,i)
determines a monad:

(C\otimes\_:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C},\mu:\mathbb{C}\otimes(\mathbb{C}\otimes\_)\xrightarrow{\bullet\otimes\_}\mathbb{C}\otimes\_,\eta:\_\xrightarrow{\rho}I\otimes\_\xrightarrow{e\otimes\_}C\otimes\_).

The category of algebras for this monad is exactly the category of
C
-modules, objects with an associative and unital action by
C
. A morphism will be a map on the base object that preserves the action:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes M\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M\otimes D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes\propto}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet\otimes M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u,C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes f}

Strict actegories are the 2-categorical generalization of modules over a monoid. The coherences for a 2-monad follow from the coherences from a strict monoidal category in the 2-category of categories. The following proposition relies on a few facts from enriched category theory (treating the cartesian closed category
\mathsf{Cat}
as a
\mathsf{Cat}
-enriched category, per
Kelly 2005
) but a more general treatment of non-strict actegories may be found in
Janelidze and Kelly 2001
:
\pagenote
Since I’m not referencing any sort of weakness, this is just enriched category theory so I think Kelly is a fair reference. However, I have included the reference to Janelidze and Kelly’s work on actegories.

•

A 2-functor and 2-natural transformations are exactly a functor and natural transformations that satisfy extra coherences. These coherences follow for free by constructing the monad and comonad
\mathcal{M}\times\_,[\mathcal{M},\_]
.

•

An algebra of the underlying 1-monad is exactly an algebra of the 2-monad (the same result holds for comonads).

When working with algebras of a 2-monad, four different notions of morphisms can come into play (
Lack and Power 2009
). These arise through using the 2-categorical data to weaken the notion of a morphism:

(i)

Strict: this is exactly a morphism of the underlying algebras. Write the 2-category of strict
\mathcal{M}
-actegories.

(ii)

Strong: the morphisms preserve the action to an isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

(iii)

Lax: the 2-cell is no longer an isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

(iv)

Oplax: the 2-cell travels in the opposite direction (these will not figure into this account).

2-cells between actegory morphisms must satisfy a coherence between the natural transformation parts of the actegory morphisms.

Definition 2.1
.

In the case of strict, strong, and lax tangent functors, the same notion of a 2-cell applies: a natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow G
satisfying the following coherences with the natural transformations
\alpha
and
\beta
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto^{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto^{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto^{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\propto^{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}\times\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

We call these
actegory natural transformations
.

Note that for strict actegory morphisms, this condition holds for any natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow G
. Now consider the following three 2-categories.
\pagenote
Added the actual definition of the 2-categories referenced later on. I also added the coherence for actegory-natural transformation

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\otimes,I)
be a strict monoidal category. Define the following three 2-categories.

1.

\mathcal{M}\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{strict}}
: the 2-category of strict
\mathcal{M}
-actegories, strict actegory morphisms, and natural transformations.

2.

\mathcal{M}\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{strong}}
: the 2-category of strict
\mathcal{M}
-actegories, strong actegory morphisms, and actegory natural transformations.

3.

\mathcal{M}\mathsf{Act}_{\mathsf{lax}}
: the 2-category of strict
\mathcal{M}
-actegories, lax actegory morphisms, and actegory natural transformations.

Note that the inclusions of these 2-categories are
locally
fully faithful, so only the 1-cells differ.

The case where the action preserves certain limits in the monoidal category is of particular interest. A small category equipped with a class of chosen limits is known as a
sketch
. The previous correspondence restricts to the class of limit-preserving actions in this case.

Definition 2.3
.

A
sketch
is a small category with a class of chosen limits, and a sketch morphism is a functor sending chosen limits to the chosen limits in the domain (up to isomorphism). The category of models of a sketch
\mathcal{C}
in a category
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{C})
, is the full subcategory
[\mathcal{C},\mathbb{C}]
whose functors preserve the chosen limits.
A
monoidal sketch
, then, is a sketch
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{L})
equipped with a symmetric monoidal category structure on
\mathcal{C}
so that
\_\otimes\_
preserves limits in each argument.

Now use the fact that the category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is a monoidal sketch, since it is a small, strict monoidal category equipped with a class of limits stable under the tensor product.

Theorem 2.4

(Theorem 14.1,
Leung 2017
)
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a category. The following are equivalent:

(i)

A tangent structure on
\mathbb{C}
,

(ii)

A sketch action
\propto:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
.

Observation 2.5
.

There is a
coalgebraic
perspective on tangent categories, coming from the equivalence between algebras of the 2-monad
(\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\times(\_),\otimes,I:1\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1})
and the 2-comonad
([\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\_],[\otimes,\_],[I,\_])
.
For any category
\mathbb{C}
, there is a free tangent category given by

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\times\mathbb{C}

and this agrees with the free
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
-actegory. However, for the
cofree
tangent category, take

\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}),

the category of transverse-limit-preserving functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
.

We can use Leung’s theorem to induce a monoidal functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
when a tangent structure is induced by a single object.

Corollary 2.6
.

Let
(\mathbb{C},\otimes,I)
be a strict monoidal category.
\pagenote
I’ve added this intermediary result to make the Weil nerve a bit more explicit.
If an additive bundle
(p:A\to I,+:A_{2}\to A,0:I\to A)
equipped with morphisms

A\otimes A\xrightarrow{c}A\otimes A\hskip 14.22636ptA\xrightarrow{\ell}A\otimes A

determines a tangent structure on
\mathbb{C}
using the endofunctor
A\otimes(-)
, then
A
determines a strict, transverse-limit-preserving, monoidal functor

A(-):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C};W_{n[1]}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{n[k]}\mapsto A_{n[1]}\otimes\dots\otimes A_{n[k]}=T_{n[1]}\dots T_{n[k]}.I

Note that this allows for a more conceptual description of representable tangent structure.

Proposition 2.7
.

In a symmetric monoidal closed category, an infinitesimal object is exactly a strict symmetric monoidal functor
D:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
.
\pagenote
The other statement was probably more general than necessary - this statement is more to the point.

This presentation of an infinitesimal object makes it tautological that
\mathbb{C}^{op}
has a tangent structure.

Corollary 2.8
.

Given a strict symmetric monoidal functor

D:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{op}

there is a strict action of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{op}
given by

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\times\mathbb{C}^{op}\xrightarrow{D^{op}\otimes\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}^{op}\times\mathbb{C}^{op}\xrightarrow{\otimes}\mathbb{C}^{op}.

There is a clear correspondence between the notions of a (strict, strong, lax) tangent functor and a (strict, strong, lax) actegory morphism. This proposition extends to the following equivalence of 2-categories.

Corollary 2.9

(Theorem 14.1
Leung 2017
)
.

The following pairs of 2-categories are equivalent.

(i)

The 2-category of tangent categories and strict tangent functors is the full sub-2-category of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\mathsf{Act_{Strict}}
spanned by sketch actions.

(ii)

The 2-category of tangent categories and strong tangent functors is the full sub-2-category of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\mathsf{Act_{strong}}
spanned by sketch actions.

(iii)

The 2-category of tangent categories and lax tangent functors is the full sub-2-category of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\mathsf{Act_{Lax}}
spanned by sketch actions.

3
The Weil nerve of an involution algebroid

The construction in this section is analagous to the nerve of an internal category—hence the “Weil nerve” construction—and deals with similar technical issues. In particular, the construction in this section will mimic the nerve construction for internal categories by replacing the tensor product of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
with span composition in the domain category. Recall that every anchored bundle or internal category has a canonical span associated with it:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

In any category
\mathbb{C}
, there is a category of spans in
\mathbb{C}
as well as span composition.

Definition 3.1
.

A
span from
A
to
B
in a category
\mathbb{C}
is a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

There is a notion of
span composition
, so given a span
X:A\to B
and
Y:B\to C
, then the composition of
X
and
Y
is the pullback (if it exists):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime\prime}}

A morphism of spans is a commuting diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}

Note that if
f
and
g
are span morphisms with
f_{r}=g_{l}
, then the horizontal composition may be formed if each respective span composition exists:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}=g_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

When discussing span composition in a tangent category, it is assumed that the pullback is a
T
-pullback.

Observation 3.2
.

Note that the category of spans in
\mathbb{C}
is a functor category, so that limits are computed pointwise in
\mathbb{C}
. This also means that the horizontal composition operation, when it exists, preserves limits in either argument.

These span constructions can be helpful in constructing functors from a monoidal category into a non-monoidal category
\mathbb{C}
, by forming a monoidal category from
\mathbb{C}
using spans. In the case of an internal category over
M
, one takes the slice category
\mathbb{C}/(M\times M)
where the tensor product is span composition. An internal category
s,t:C\to M
is a monoid in this category of spans over
M
, so that it determines a monoidal functor

C:\Delta^{op}\to\mathbb{C}/(M\times M),

remembering that
\Delta^{op}
is the monoidal theory for monoid (every monoid in a monoidal category
\mathbb{C}
determines a monoidal functor
\Delta\to\mathbb{C}
). The construction of the corresponding monoidal category for spans is more nuanced, as the category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is not
\mathbb{N}
-indexed. Observe that the prolongation of an anchored bundle is constructed as a span composition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The third prolongation is given by span composition as well:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\mathcal{L}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\pi\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This horizontal composition will play the same role as the tensor product in
\mathbb{C}/(M\times M)
.

Definition 3.3
.

In a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, consider a pair of spans

X:M\to T^{U}M,\hskip 4.26773ptY:M\to T^{V}M.

Define
X\boxtimes Y
to be the horizontal composition (when it exists):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{UV}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.l_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(recall that we will often suppress the
\otimes
in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
to save space).
\pagenote
clarifying notation
So the span composition is

M\xrightarrow{X}T^{U}M\xrightarrow{T^{U}.Y}T^{U}.T^{V}M

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{A}}

(1)

The horizontal composition
f\boxtimes g
is defined as
f\times_{\theta.M}\theta.g
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.T^{V}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U^{\prime}}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U^{\prime}}.T^{V^{\prime}}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U^{\prime}}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.r_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U^{\prime}}.r_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta.\psi.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta.g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.l_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U^{\prime}}.l_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

In any tangent category with a tangent display system (Definition
\pagenote
there was no reason to pull this out as an observation
5.3
), the category of spans on
M
whose maps are of the form given by Equation
1
with
l\in\mathcal{D}
is a monoidal category.
Any anchored bundle in a tangent category gives rise to a monoidal category after a strict choice of
T
-pullbacks (assuming those
T
-pullbacks exist).

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
. Write the span

\widehat{A}.W_{n}:=(M\xleftarrow{\pi\circ\pi_{i}}A_{n}\xrightarrow{\varrho^{n}}T_{n}M),\hskip 14.22636pt\widehat{A}.\mathbb{N}:=(M=M=M).

A
choice of prolongations
for
(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
is a strict choice of horizontal composition for each
V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
:

\widehat{A}.V=\widehat{A}.(W_{n[1]}\dots W_{n[k]}):=\widehat{A}.W_{n[1]}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\widehat{A}.W_{n[k]}.

We will write the span as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}.M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{V}}

(notice that the apex is not hatted).
Given a choice of prolongations for an anchored bundle
(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
, the category
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
is defined as follows:

•

Objects are
\widehat{A}.V
for
V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
.

•

Morphisms are given by pairs

(f,\phi):\widehat{A}.V\to\widehat{A}.U

where
f:A.V\to A.U
and
\phi:V\to U
determine a span morphism of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

as discussed in Definition
3.1
.

•

Tensor structure: The tensor product is defined using the horizontal composition
\boxtimes
as defined in Definition
3.1
.

The idea is to show that an involution algebroid structure on an anchored bundle induces a tangent structure on the monoidal category of prolongations, and then to apply Leung’s theorem.
The following two lemmas will simplify this proof.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle with chosen prolongations in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, and identify the monoidal category
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
.

(i)

There is a functor

U^{\varrho}:\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\to\mathbb{C}

constructed by sending a span morphism to the morphism between the objects at its apex:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(ii)

Suppose we have a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(l,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r,\beta)}

whose image under
U^{\varrho}
is a
T
-pullback in
\mathbb{C}
, and so that the square in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is a transverse
T
-pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{A}.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{A}.Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{A}.X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{A}.Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then
U^{\varrho}
reflects the limit; that is, the original square in
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
is a
T
-pullback.

(iii)

T
-pullbacks of the form described in (ii) are closed under
\boxtimes
.

Proof.

The functor in in (i) is straightforward to construct, as it simply forgets the left and right legs of the spans. For (ii), note that because the
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
part of the diagram is a transverse
T
-pullback, then given a pair of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(l,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(r,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,\omega)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(y,\gamma)}

a unique span morphism
\widehat{A}.V\to\widehat{A}.U
may be induced using the apex map from
\mathbb{C}
, and the unique map induced in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
by the universality of transverse squares (this square is also universal in
\mathbb{C}
), so the span morphism diagram will commute by universality.

For (iii),
T
-pullback squares of the form in (ii) are closed under
\boxtimes
as transverse squares in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
are closed under
\otimes
, so the result follows by the commutativity of limits and by applying part (ii) of this lemma.
∎

Observation 3.6
.

It will be useful to have a “flat” presentation of the prolongation
A.W_{n(1)}\dots W_{n(k)}
.
The higher prolongations of an anchored bundle may be concretely described as the
T
-pullback of the zig-zag below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.W_{n(1)}\dots W_{n(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.W_{n(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n[1]}.\hat{A}.W_{n(2)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n(1)\dots n(k-1)}\hat{A}.W_{n(k)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n(1)}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n(1)}.\varrho_{n(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n(1)\dots n(k)}.(\pi\circ\pi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{n(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n}[1].(\pi\circ\pi_{0})}

so that the prolongation
A.W^{n[1]}\dots W^{n[k]}
may be written concretely as

(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}):A_{n[1]}{{}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}\!\times_{T.\pi^{\prime}}\!T_{n[1]}.A_{n[2]}{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!\dots{{}_{\varrho^{\prime}}}\!\times_{T.\pi^{\prime}}\!T_{n[1]\dots n[k-1]}A_{n[k]}.

Furthermore, the choice of prolongation identifies the following limits:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.UV}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\hat{A}.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.UV}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}\hat{A}.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{A}.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\pi^{V}}

so that

A.UV=A.U\boxtimes A.V=A.U\ \boxtimes id_{M}\boxtimes A.V

where
id_{M}
is the span
M=M=M
.

Note that a
\varrho
sends the involution algebroid structure map to its corresponding tangent structure map. Each of the structure maps, then, gives a span morphism where
\boxtimes
is well defined:

Definition 3.7
.

The flip map

,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

The idea is to show that the monoidal category of chosen prolongations
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
for an involution algebroid has a tangent structure generated by the structure maps in Definition
3.7
and the endofunctor
\widehat{A}.W\boxtimes(-)
. Using the flat presentation, we can then show that
U^{\varrho}
will determine a tangent functor in
\mathbb{C}
. The following lemma about
\varrho
will be useful in constructing the natural transformation part of a tangent functor.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be an anchored bundle with chosen prolongations.
Recall that by Definition
3.4
, the right leg of
A.U
is written
\varrho
, so it induces a span map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{U}}

This map has a flat presentation as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.UV}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U{{}_{\varrho^{U}}}\!\times_{T^{U}.\pi^{V}}\!T^{U}.A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M{{}_{id}}\!\times_{T^{U}.\pi^{V}}\!T^{U}.A.V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}\boxtimes A.V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}\times T^{U}.A.V}

We write the map

\varrho^{U}.V:=\varrho^{U}\boxtimes(\widehat{A}.V)

which corresponds to the following span morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.UV}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{UV}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A.V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\varrho^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\pi^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{U}.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\pi^{V}}

Theorem 3.9

(The Weil Nerve)
.

There is a fully faithful functor

\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{Weil}}:\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\to[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]

that sends an involution algebroid to the transverse-limit-preserving tangent functor:

(\widehat{A},\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

Proof.

For the first step of this proof, we show that an involution algebroid structure on an anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
determines a tangent category structure on the monoidal category
\mathsf{Span}(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
.

We check that the endofunctor
\hat{A}\boxtimes(-)
determines a tangent structure, with the structure maps given by Definition
3.7
:

[TC.1]

Additive bundle axioms:

(i)

Use Lemma
3.5
to see that

\hat{A}.W_{2}=\hat{A}.W{{}_{p}}\!\times_{p}\!\hat{A}.WA{{}_{\pi}}\!\times_{\pi}\!A;

this is preserved by
\hat{A}.V\boxtimes(-)
.

(ii)

The triple
(\hat{A}.+,\hat{A}.p,\hat{A}.0)=(+_{q},\pi,\xi)
is an additive bundle induced by Proposition
4.4
, and
\boxtimes
preserves pullbacks (and therefore additive bundles), so the additive bundle axioms hold.

[TC.2]

Symmetry axioms:

(i)

\hat{A}.c\circ\hat{A}.c=id
follows from the involution axiom
\sigma\circ\sigma=id
.

(ii)

For Yang–Baxter, note that

(\hat{A}\boxtimes c)\circ(c\boxtimes\hat{A})\circ(\hat{A}\boxtimes c)=(c\boxtimes\hat{A})\circ(\hat{A}\boxtimes c)\circ(c\boxtimes\hat{A})

follows from the Yang–Baxter equation on an involution algebroid

(\sigma\times c)\circ(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\times c)=(id\times T.\sigma)\circ(\sigma\times c)\circ(id\times T.\sigma),

since

\sigma\times c.A=(\hat{A}.c)\boxtimes(\hat{A}.W)\text{ and }id\times T.\sigma=(\hat{A}.W)\boxtimes c.

(iii)

For the naturality conditions:

(a)

The interchanges of
+,0,p
all follow from the fact that

\sigma:(A.WW,\lambda\times\ell)\to(A.WW,id\times c\circ T.\lambda)

is linear, and so is an additive bundle morphism.

(b)

The axiom

\ell.T\circ c=T.c\circ c.T\circ T.\ell

is equivalent to the equation

(\sigma\times c)\circ(1\times T.\sigma)\circ(\hat{\lambda}\times\ell)=(1\times T\hat{\lambda})\circ\sigma

which is equivalent to the double linearity axiom on
\sigma
by Proposition
3.4
.

[TC.3]

The lift axioms:

(i)

The additive bundle equations are a consequence of
\lambda
being a lift and
+
being the addition induced by the non-singularity of
\lambda
.

(ii)

The coassociativity axiom

\ell.T\circ\ell=T.\ell\circ\ell

is equivalent to

(\hat{\lambda}\times\ell)\circ\hat{\lambda}=(id\times T.\hat{\lambda})\circ\hat{\lambda}

proved in (i) of Proposition
2.6
.

(iii)

The symmetry of comultiplication,
c\circ\ell=\ell
, is given by the unique equation for an involution algebroid, so that
\sigma\circ(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda)=(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda)
.

(iv)

The universality of the lift follows from part (ii) of Proposition
2.6
; Lemma
3.5
ensures that for any
V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
,
\widehat{A}.V\boxtimes\mu
and
\mu\boxtimes\widehat{A}.V
are universal.

This lemma puts a tangent structure on
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
. Now consider the functor sending spans to the apex map,

U^{\varrho}:\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\to\mathbb{C}.

The family of maps

\{\varrho^{U}.V:\varrho^{U}\boxtimes\widehat{A}.V|U,V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\}

gives a family of natural transformations

\varrho^{U}:A.T^{U}\Rightarrow T^{U}.A,

so that the following pair constitute a tangent functor

(U^{\varrho},\varrho):\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)\to\mathbb{C}.

Because the universality conditions on
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
followed by reflecting limits in
\mathbb{C}
using Lemma
3.5
, it follows that
(U^{\varrho},\varrho)
will preserve the tangent-natural limits in
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
corresponding to transverse limits in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
.

By Leung’s Theorem
1.7
(by way of Corollary
2.6
), the tangent structure on
\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
induces a strict, monoidal, transverse-limit-preserving functor

\bar{A}:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Span}(\pi,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)

that sends the tensor product
\otimes
to the span composition
\boxtimes
. By composing the strict tangent functor
(\bar{A},id)
and
(U^{\varrho},\varrho)
, we have a lax, transverse-limit-preserving, tangent functor:

(A,\varrho):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C};V\mapsto A.V

Now, check the bijection on morphisms. Starting with an involution algebroid morphism
(f,m):A\to B
, note that this gives a span morphism
\hat{f}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

This gives a natural definition of
\hat{f}.V
using the horizontal composition of span morphism, so that

\hat{f}.(UV)=\hat{f}.U\boxtimes\hat{f}.V\text{ and }\hat{f}.\mathbb{N}=m,

(2)

giving a family of maps
\{\hat{f}_{U}:U\in\mathsf{objects}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1})\}
. Because
f
will commute with the structure maps
\{\pi,\xi,+,(\xi\circ\pi,\lambda),\sigma\}
, it follow immediately that
\hat{f}
is a natural transformation, because the following calculation holds for each
\theta:X\to Y\in\{p,0,+,\ell,c\}
:

\displaystyle\quad\hat{f}.UYV\circ(\hat{A}.U\boxtimes\theta\boxtimes\hat{A}.V)

\displaystyle=(\hat{f}.U\boxtimes\hat{f}.Y\boxtimes\hat{f}.V)\circ(\hat{A}.U\boxtimes\theta\boxtimes\hat{A}.V)

\displaystyle=\hat{f}.U\boxtimes(\hat{f}.Y\circ\theta)\boxtimes\hat{f}.V

\displaystyle=\hat{f}.U\boxtimes(\theta\circ\hat{f}.X)\boxtimes\hat{f}.V

\displaystyle=(\hat{A}.U\boxtimes\theta\boxtimes\hat{A}.V)\circ\hat{f}.UXV

Tangent naturality will follow by the preservation of the anchor map by
f
.
The equality, for any Weil algebra
U
, of the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{f}.U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

is precisely the tangent-naturality condition from Definitions
3.12
,
2.1
.

For the inverse of this mapping, consider a tangent natural transformation (Definition
3.12
)

where
(A,\alpha)
and
(B,\beta)
are tangent functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
built out of involution algebroids with chosen prolongations. For any
U,V
, the map
\gamma.UV
decomposes as
\gamma.U\boxtimes\gamma.V
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\widehat{A}.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{B}.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\widehat{B}.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.T}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{B}.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.\widehat{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}.p.T=\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T=\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\widehat{A}.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{B}.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\widehat{B}.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{B}.p.T=\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta.T=\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\gamma.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.TT}

Applying this relationship inductively, it is clear that the base maps
\gamma.W
and
\gamma.\mathbb{N}
determine the entire morphism
\gamma.V
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{n[1]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n[1]}.M}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{n[1]}.M}}
