\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{n[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{n[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.W_{n[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n[1]}.\gamma.\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n[1]}.(\pi\circ\pi_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{n[1]}.(\pi\circ\pi_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\gamma.\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.\mathbb{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Thus, every tangent-natural transformation is constructed out of a pair

(\gamma.\mathbb{N}:M\to N,\gamma.W:A\to B)

using the
\boxtimes
construction from Equation
2
.
All that remains to show is that this pair is an involution algebroid morphism.

Tangent naturality gives the following two coherences:

\varrho^{B}\circ\gamma.W=T.\gamma.\mathbb{N}\circ\varrho^{A}\text{ and }\sigma^{B}\circ\gamma.WW=\gamma.WW\circ\sigma^{A}

since
\varrho^{B}=\beta.W,\varrho^{A}=\alpha.W,\sigma^{B}=B.c
, and
\sigma^{A}=A.c
by construction. The following diagram proves that
\gamma.W
preserves the lifts, so that
(\gamma.W,\gamma.\mathbb{N})
is an involution algebroid morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.B.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.B.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.B.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi\pi,\lambda^{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi\pi,\lambda^{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda^{B}}

Thus, a tangent natural transformation
(\widehat{A},\alpha)\to(\widehat{B},\beta)
is exactly a morphism of involution algebroids
A\to B
, proving the theorem.

∎

Now, the projection for a Lie algebroid is a submersion, as we may make a choice of prolongations for each
U\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
. These prolongations lead to a new observation about Lie algebroids: they embed into a category of functors into smooth manifolds.

Corollary 3.10
.

Using the Weil nerve construction, the category of Lie algebroids embeds into the tangent-functor category:

\mathsf{LieAlgd}\hookrightarrow[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathsf{SMan}].

4
Identifying involution algebroids

This section identifies those tangent functors

(A,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

that are involution algebroids as precisely those where
A
preserves transverse limits and
\alpha
is a
T
-cartesian
natural transformation (Definition
4.1
).
These conditions will force each
A.V
to be the
V
-prolongation of the underlying anchored pre-differential bundle:

(A.p:A.T\to A,\;\ A.0:A\to A.T,\;\ A.T\xrightarrow{A.\ell}A.T.T\xrightarrow{\alpha.T}T.A.T,\;\ \alpha:A.T\to T.A)

(these conditions also ensure that this tuple is an anchored differential bundle).

Initially, it is only clear that
\alpha
is
T
-cartesian for the projection
p
. Indeed, recall that the prolongation
A.UV
is defined to be the
T
-pullback of the cospan:

\widehat{A}.U\xrightarrow{\alpha^{U}}T^{U}.A.\mathbb{N}\xleftarrow{T^{U}.A.p^{V}}T^{U}.\widehat{A}.V

Then consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.UWV}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\widehat{A}.T.T^{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.U.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\widehat{A}.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.T^{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\widehat{A}.\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\hat{A}.p.V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}.T^{U}.p.T^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}.T^{U}.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\widehat{A}.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means that every naturality square of
\alpha
for
p
is a
T
-pullback; natural transformations satisfying this property for every map in the domain category are called
T
-cartesian.

Definition 4.1
.

A natural transformation
\gamma:F\Rightarrow G
is
cartesian
whenever each naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FD}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a pullback. A natural transformation between functors into a tangent category is
T
-cartesian
whenever each component square is a
T
-pullback (we will generally suppress the
T
when the context is clear).

Now, recall that the Weil complex determined by an involution algebroid has
A.U.V
determined by the following
T
-pullback squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.U.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\widehat{A}.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{A}.T^{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.\widehat{A}.\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{A}.T^{U}.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.\widehat{A}.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Then it is not difficult to show that the
T
-cartesian condition on a Weil complex forces it to be an involution algebroid. We first need:

Definition 4.2
.

A
T
-cartesian Weil complex in
\mathbb{C}
is a tangent functor

(A,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

for which
A
sends transverse limits to
T
-limits and
\alpha
is a
T
-cartesian natural transformation.

The first condition to check is that a
T
-cartesian Weil complex gives a natural anchored bundle
\hat{A}
whose Weil prolongations coincide with the functor assignments on objects.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
(A,\alpha)
be a
T
-cartesian Weil complex. Then we have an anchored bundle

(M:=A.\mathbb{N},\hskip 4.26773pt\hat{A}:=A.W,\hskip 4.26773pt\pi:=A.\pi,\hskip 4.26773pt\xi:=A.\xi,\hskip 4.26773pt\lambda:=\alpha.T\circ A.\ell).

Furthermore,

\mathcal{L}(\hat{A})=A.WW,\hskip 8.5359pt\mathcal{L}^{2}(\hat{A})=A.WWW.

Proof.

Suppose we have a tangent functor
(F,\alpha):\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
and a differential bundle
(\pi,\xi,\lambda)
in
\mathbb{C}
. If
F
preserves
T
-pullbacks of
\pi
, it preserves the additive bundle structure on
(\pi,\xi,+)
, so to show
(F.\pi,F.\xi,\alpha\circ F.\lambda)
is universal it suffices to show that the following diagram is a
T
-pullback in
\mathbb{D}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\alpha\circ F.\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi}

Expand this to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.\mu^{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.M}

In this case, it restricts to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Each square is a
T
-pullback by hypothesis, so the universality of the lift follows by the
T
-pullback lemma. Because the complex is
T
-cartesian, the assignment
A.V
gives a coherent choice of prolongations by the
T
-pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A.V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{U}.A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.U.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.A.p^{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{U}}

∎

There is, of course, a natural candidate for the involution map.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
be the anchored bundle induced by a
T
-cartesian Weil complex in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
. Then we have an involution map

\sigma:\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{A.c}\mathcal{L}(A).

The equations for an involution algebroid should follow immediately by functoriality; one need only ensure that the maps take the correct form.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
A
be a
T
-cartesian Weil complex in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, with
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\sigma)
its underlying anchored bundle.
Then we have:

(i)

A.c.T=\sigma\times c
,

(ii)

A.T.c=1\times T.\sigma
,

(iii)

A.\ell.T=\hat{\lambda}\times\ell.A
,

(iv)

A.T.\ell=id\times T.\hat{\lambda}
.

Proof.

(i)

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{TT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{TT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c.A.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.c.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Observe that this forces
A.c.T=A.c\times c.A.T=\sigma\times c
.

(ii)

Likewise, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.p.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.TT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.p.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.TT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.A.c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

forces
A.T.c=id\times T.A.c=id\times T.\sigma
.

(iii)

The diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.T.A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.\ell.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{TT}.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell.A.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.T.A.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell.A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{TT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

forces
A.\ell.T=A.\ell\times\ell.A=\hat{\lambda}\times\ell
.

(iv)

As
\alpha
is
T
-cartesian, the following diagram is a
T
-pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.A.T.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A.T.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.A.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Using previous results, this means that
A.\ell.T
is the unique map making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T.\pi}\!TA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T.\pi}\!TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA{{}_{T.\varrho}}\!\times_{T^{2}.\pi}\!T^{2}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\hat{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

which we can see is
id\times\hat{\lambda}
.

∎

Pulling together this lemma and the previous proposition, the following is now clear:

Proposition 4.6
.

A
T
-cartesian Weil complex determines an involution algebroid.

However, we have not yet exhibited an isomorphism of categories between the image of the Weil nerve functor and
T
-cartesian Weil complexes.
At first glance, the Weil nerve construction only gives a Weil complex that is
T
-cartesian for the tangent projection
p\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
. Being
T
-cartesian for
p
is, however, sufficient: a Weil complex that is
T
-cartesian for tangent projections will be
T
-cartesian for every map in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
(a similar result appears in the context of differentiable programming languages; see
Cruttwell et al. 2019
).

Proposition 4.7
.

A lax tranverse-limit-preserving tangent functor
(F,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
for which
F
preserves pullback powers of each
T^{U}.p
is
T
-cartesian if and only if each

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{B}}

is a
T
-pullback.

Proof.

We only check the converse since the forward implication is trivial. We make use of the
T
-pullback lemma.

(i)

c
is an isomorphism, so its naturality square is a
T
-pullback.

(ii)

For projections
T_{2}\to T
, the retract of a
T
-pullback diagram is a
T
-pullback, so the following diagram is universal:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi_{i}}

(iii)

For
0
, observe that the following two diagrams are equal:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The right diagram is a
T
-pullback, and the right square of the left diagram is a
T
-pullback by hypothesis.
By the
T
-pullback lemma, the left square of the left diagram is a
T
-pullback.

(iv)

For
\ell
, observe that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The outer perimeter of the right diagram is a
T
-pullback (left square by hypothesis, right square by (ii)), as is the right square of the left diagram (by hypothesis).
By the
T
-pullback lemma, the left square of the left diagram is a
T
-pullback.

(v)

For
+
, observe that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F.T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.+}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F.T.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.F.p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The outer diagram on the right is a
T
-pullback by composition, and the right square on the left diagram is a
T
-pullback by hypothesis, so the result follows.

To check that the naturality square is a
T
-pullback for
every
map in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
, we once again use Leung’s characterization of maps in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
from Proposition
1.7
. Inductively, the set of maps generated by
\{p,0,+,\ell,c\}
closed under
\otimes
and
\circ
follows as
T
-pullback squares are closed to composition. For maps induced by a tranverse limit in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
,
F
preserves transverse limits so this follows by the commutativity of limits.
∎

Theorem 4.8
.

For any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, the replete image of the Weil nerve functor

\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]

is precisely the category of
T
-cartesian Weil complexes.

Corollary 4.9
.

That
\alpha:A.T\Rightarrow T.A
is
T
-cartesian is equivalent to requiring that the tangent functor

(A,\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

restricts to an anchored bundle

(\pi:A.T\xrightarrow{A.p}A.\mathbb{N},\;\ \xi:A\xrightarrow{A.0}A.T,\;\ \lambda:A.T\xrightarrow{A.\ell}A.TT\xrightarrow{\alpha.T}T.A.T,\;\ \varrho:A.T\xrightarrow{\alpha}T.A)

and each
A.T^{V}
is the
V
-prolongation of this anchor bundle.

Remark 4.10
.

The condition in Corollary
4.9
is analogous to the Segal conditions identifying those simplicial complexes

\Delta\to\mathbb{C}

that are internal categories. Note that every simplicial object has an underlying reflexive graph

\mathsf{tr}_{1}(X):=(s,t:X([1])\to X([0]),i:X([0])\to X([1]))

where
X([n])
is isomorphic to the object of
n
-composable arrows for the underlying reflexive graph.

Remark 4.11
.

Notably, being
T
-cartesian for
p
is enough to force that a natural transformation is
T
-cartesian for the other tangent-structural natural transformations. This has consequences when one uses partial maps to combine
topological
notions with tangent categories.
In this context, a partial map
N\to X
with domain
M\hookrightarrow N
is a span

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

whose right leg is monic. The intuition is that the map
f
is defined on the subobject
M
of
N
, which introduces a new problem: what is the proper notion of a
subobject
in a tangent category?
Such a notion should give rise to a
stable class of monics
: one that is closed under horizontal span composition.
One answer is the notion of etale monics: a morphism is
etale
whenever the naturality square for
p
is a
T
-pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Geometrically, this means that the morphism is a local diffeomorphism; for example, an etale subobject of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the Dubuc topos is precisely an open subset in the usual sense.
An endofunctor lifts to the partial map category whenever it preserves the class of monics. A natural transformation lifts to endofunctors on the partial map category whenever it is
T
-cartesian for the class of monics, and the same proof will show that this property holds for etale monics
Cruttwell et al. 2019
.

5
The prolongation tangent structure

One of the most important consequences of the Weil Nerve Theorem
3.9
is that the category of involution algebroids (with chosen prolongations) may be equipped with two tangent structures. The first tangent structure is the pointwise tangent structure described in Proposition
3.6
.
The tangent functor sends

(A,\alpha)\mapsto(T.A:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C},c.A\circ T.\alpha:T.A.T\Rightarrow T.T.A)

(recall the composition of tangent functors given in Example
3.11
(ii)). The structure morphisms will be given by whiskering, so in this case
\theta.A,\theta\in\{p,0,+,\ell,c\}
. The restriction to tangent functors that preserve transverse limits along with the fact that the natural part
\alpha
is
T
-cartesian, however, ensures that precomposition with the tangent functor

(A,\alpha)\mapsto(A.T:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C},T.\alpha\circ A.c:A.T.T\Rightarrow T.A.T)

returns an involution algebroid. The structure maps are once again given by whiskering, with the pre-composition tangent structure
A.\theta,\theta\in\{p,0,+,\ell,c\}
. Preservation of transverse limits guarantees that this tangent structure will satisfy the necessary universality conditions.

Proposition 5.1

(Proposition
3.10
)
.

The category of involution algebroids with chosen prolongations in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
has a second tangent structure, where the action by
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is given by

(A,\alpha)\mapsto(A.T:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C},\alpha.T\circ\hat{A}.c:\hat{A}.T.T\Rightarrow T.\hat{A}.T).

Proof.

The proposition statement means that the structure morphisms for this new involution algebroid are given by

(A.T,\alpha.T\circ A.c)\cong\begin{cases}\alpha.T\circ A.c=\varrho^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}TA\\
\quad\;\;A.T.p=\pi^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{p\circ\pi_{1}}A\\
\quad\;\;\;A.T.0=\xi^{\prime}:&A\xrightarrow{(\xi\circ\pi,0)}\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\varrho^{\prime}\circ A.T.\ell=\lambda^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{\lambda\times\ell}T.\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\quad\;\ A.T.c=\sigma^{\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\sigma\times c}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\end{cases}

Similarly, we can see that

\displaystyle(A.T.T,\alpha.T.T\circ A.c.T\circ A.T.c)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha.T.T\circ A.c.T\circ A.T.c=\varrho^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})}T.\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\hskip 71.13188ptA.T.p=\pi^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(p\circ\pi_{1},p\circ\pi_{2}):}\mathcal{L}(A)\\
\hskip 73.97733ptA.T.0=\xi^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}(A)\xrightarrow{(\xi\circ\pi\circ\pi_{0},0\circ\pi_{1},0\circ\pi_{2})}\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\hskip 52.63777pt\varrho^{\prime\prime}\circ A.T.\ell=\lambda^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{(\lambda\times\ell\times\ell)}T.\mathcal{L}^{2}(A)\\
\hskip 61.17325ptA.T.T.c=\sigma^{\prime\prime}:&\mathcal{L}^{3}(A)\xrightarrow{(\sigma\times c\times c)}\mathcal{L}^{3}(A)\end{cases}

These coincide with the involution algebroids
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A),\mathcal{L}^{\prime}.\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(A)
in Proposition
3.10
: the second tangent structure follows from the fact that the natural transformations for the tangent structure there are given by

A.\phi:A.U\Rightarrow A.V,\phi:U\to V\in\{p,0,+,\ell,c\}.

The result follows as a corollary of Theorem
3.9
.
∎

The Jacobi identity for involution algebroids

Classically, the theory of Lie algebroids uses the algebra of sections
\Gamma(\pi)
. One key observation is that when using the Lie tangent structure
(\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{L})
, sections of
\pi
are in bijective correspondence with
\chi_{\mathcal{L}}(A)
.
This observation allows for different statements about Lie algebroids to be translated into formal statements about the tangent bundle in
(\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C}),\mathcal{L})
.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
A
be an involution algebroid in
\mathbb{C}
.
There is a bijection between the sections of
\pi
in
\mathbb{C}
and the vector fields on
A
in
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
:

X\in\Gamma(\pi)\mapsto((id,TX\circ\varrho),X):A\to T_{L}(A);\hskip 5.69046pt\hat{X}\in\chi_{\mathcal{L}}(A)\mapsto\hat{X}_{R}:A.R\to A.T.

Proof.

Recall the coherence for tangent natural transformations
\gamma:(H,\phi)\Rightarrow(G,\psi)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K.T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\gamma}

We specify this to a morphism
\hat{X}:(A,\alpha)\Rightarrow T_{L}(A,\alpha)=(A.T,\alpha.T)
at
\mathbb{N},W
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.X}

and so infer that, if we set
X:=X_{R}
, we have
\pi_{1}\circ X.T=T(X)\circ\varrho
.
Furthermore, the condition that
p_{L}\circ X=id
forces
id=\pi_{0}\circ X.T
; thus, we can see that every section
X
of
p_{L}
is given by a morphism of the form
((id,TX\circ\varrho),X))
on the underlying involution algebroids, where
\pi\circ X=id
.

We now show that every
X\in\Gamma(\pi)
gives rise to a section of
\pi_{L}
.
Observe that the following is a morphism of involution algebroids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A{{}_{\varrho}}\!\times_{T\pi}\!TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,TX\circ\varrho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\circ\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

Note that it is well typed, as
T.\pi\circ T.X\circ\varrho=\varrho\circ id
. Check that it is a bundle morphism:

p\circ\pi_{1}\circ(id,T.X\circ\varrho)=p\circ T.X\circ\varrho=X\circ p\circ\varrho=X\circ\pi

and that it is linear:

\displaystyle(\lambda\circ\pi_{0},\ell\circ\pi_{1})\circ(id,T.X\circ\varrho)=(\lambda,\ell\circ T.X\circ\varrho)

\displaystyle=(\lambda,T^{2}.X\circ\ell\circ\varrho)=(\lambda,T^{2}.X\circ\lambda)=T(id,TX)\circ\lambda.

Then check that it preserves the anchor:

\pi_{1}\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)=TX\circ\varrho

and the involution:

\displaystyle(\pi_{0},\pi_{1},T^{2}X\circ T\varrho\pi_{1})\circ\sigma=(\sigma,T^{2}X\circ T\varrho\circ\pi_{1}\sigma)

\displaystyle=(\sigma,T^{2}X\circ cT\varrho\circ\pi_{1})=(\sigma(\pi_{0},\pi_{1}),c\pi_{3})\circ(id,T^{2}X\circ T\varrho\circ\pi_{1}).

Thus we have that
(id,TX\circ\varrho\pi_{1})
is a morphism of involution algebroids, inducing a morphism of
T
-cartesian Weil complexes.
Lastly, we check that it is a section of
p^{L}_{A}
, but this is clear, since

\pi_{0}\circ(id,TX\circ\varrho)=id;

thus we have the desired bijection.
∎

Recall that given an involution on an anchored bundle, there is a bracket on its set of sections (see the explicit construction in Section
4
).
Given an
X,Y\in\Gamma(\pi)
, there is a bracket defined as follows:

\hat{\lambda}\circ[X,Y]_{A}+_{1}(\xi\pi,0)\circ Y=((\sigma\circ(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X-_{2}(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y)).

A direct proof of the Jacobi identity is a detailed calculation (see the original preprint on involution algebroids
Burke and MacAdam 2019
) and still relies on Cockett and Cruttwell’s result for an arbitrary tangent category with negatives. As a result of Proposition
5.2
, we can instead use Cockett and Cruttwell’s result directly:

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
A
be a complete involution algebroid in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
with negatives.
There is a Lie bracket defined on
\Gamma(\pi)
,
[-,-]
induced by

\hat{\lambda}\circ[X,Y]_{A}+_{1}(\xi\pi,0)\circ Y=((\sigma\circ(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X-_{2}(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y)).

Proof.

The bracket is induced by Rosicky’s universality diagram, as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=p\circ((\sigma\circ(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X-(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y))-0Y

\displaystyle=Tp\circ((\sigma\circ(id,TY\circ\varrho)\circ X-(id,TX\circ\varrho)\circ Y))-0Y.

We look at the Lie tangent structure for
\mathsf{Inv}^{*}(A)
; this is precisely the vector field induced by

ev_{R}([\hat{X},\hat{Y}]).

We complete the proof by using the result that for any
A
in a tangent category with negatives, the bracket on
\chi(A)
that is defined by

\ell\circ[X,Y]=(c\circ T.X\circ Y-T.Y\circ X)-0X

satisfies the Jacobi identity.
∎

Identifying categories of involution algebroids

Section
4
identified whenever a functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is an involution algebroid, whereas this section identifies tangent categories
\mathbb{C}
that embed into the category of involution algebroids in some tangent category
\mathbb{C}
. We call this structure an
abstract category
of involution algebroids. This notion involves some 2-category theory, using a modified notion of
codescent
(see
Bourke 2010
for a development of codescent).

Recall that for any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, the category of involution algebroids has
\mathbb{C}
as a reflective subcategory. Furthermore, because limits of involution algebroids are computed pointwise, this reflector is left-exact. This left-exact reflection is the main structure we axiomatize.

Definition 5.4
.

An
abstract category of involution algebroids
is a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
with a left-exact
T
-cartesian tangent localization
(Z,\varrho):\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
, where
L
satisfies a
codescent
condition:

\mathsf{TangCat_{Strict}}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{TangCat_{Lax}}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C})\xrightarrow{L_{*}}\mathsf{TangCat_{Lax}}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{D})

(where
L_{*}
denotes post-composition by
L
) is fully faithful.

Example 5.5
.

The category of involution algebroids in any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is an abstract category of involution algebroids using
(\mathsf{Inv}(C),\mathcal{L})
. The reflector is the functor sending an involution algebroid to its base space; the
T
-cartesian natural transformation is the anchor map. Any tangent subcategory of
\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{C})
that contains
\mathbb{C}
as a full subcategory will give rise to an abstract category of involution algebroids.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be an abstract category of involution algebroids.
Then there is an embedding
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The proof follows by treating objects in
\mathbb{C}
as strict tangent functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
and morphisms as tangent natural transformations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-,A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-,B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(-,f)}

The natural part of
Z
is
T
-cartesian, and the functor part preserves limits, so
Z.\mathcal{L}(-,A)=:Z[A]
determines an involution algebroid in
\mathbb{Z}
, and
f
a morphism of involution algebroids.
The embedding is guaranteed by the codescent condition so that the post-composition functor is fully faithful.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

An abstract category of involution algebroids
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is exactly a full subcategory
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Inv}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Chapter 8
The infinitesimal nerve and its realization

The main thrust of Chapters
5
,
6
, and
7
has been that the tangent categories framework allows for Lie algebroids to be regarded as tangent functors

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{SMan}

which satisfy certain universality conditions.
This chapter, which is more experimental than the previous four chapters and represents work still in progress, puts Lie algebroids into the framework of
enriched functorial semantics
. This new perspective on algebroids uses Garner’s enriched perspective on tangent categories (
Garner 2018
) and the enriched theories paradigm from
Bourke and Garner 2019
. The functorial-semantics presentation of the Lie functor will generalize the Cartan–Lie theorem (that the category of Lie algebras is a coreflective subcategory of Lie groups) into a statement within the general theory of functorial semantics.

The goal is to show that the infinitesimal approximation of a groupoid, as discussed in Example
1.2
, may be constructed as a
nerve
, just like Kan’s original simplical approximation of a topological space. The nerve of a functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
approximates objects and morphisms in
\mathbb{C}
by
\mathcal{A}
-presheaves, so it sends an object in
\mathbb{C}
to the
\mathcal{A}
-presheaf

N_{K}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C};C\mapsto\mathbb{C}(K-,C).

Thus there will be an infinitesimal object,

\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})

where
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
denotes groupoids in the category
{\mathcal{W}}
of Weil spaces (formally defined in Section
1
). The nerve of
\partial
has a left adjoint, the
Lie realization
, given by the left Kan extension (just as Kan’s geometric approximation of a simplicial set is, in
Kan 1958
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},{\mathcal{W}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lan_{\mathcal{Y}}\partial}

(1)

{restatable*}

[The Lie Realization]theoremlie

There is a tangent adjunction between the category of involution algebroids and groupoids in
{\mathcal{W}}
, where each functor preserves products and the base spaces.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inv}({\mathcal{W}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Note, however, that the left Kan extension in Equation
1
does not immediately give the desired adjunction of Theorem
8
, as there is no guarantee that the nerve functor
N_{\partial}
lands in the category of algebroids. To prove this we must revisit the work in Section
3
presenting involution algebroids as those functors
A:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
for which each
A(V)
is the prolongation of its underlying anchored bundle; this leads naturally to the formalism for enriched theories developed in
Bourke and Garner 2019
.

The presentation of algebroids as models of an enriched theory requires situating the categories of differential bundles, anchored bundles, and involution algebroids in
{\mathcal{W}}
as full subcategories of
{\mathcal{W}}
-presheaf categories on small
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories.
Section
1
reviews the work in
Garner 2018
characterizing tangent categories as categories enriched in
{\mathcal{W}}=\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathsf{Set})
(the cofree tangent category on
\mathsf{Set}
, by Observation
2.5
).

Section
2
reconfigures the content of Chapter
5
using the enriched perspective, so that lifts are
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors from the
{\mathcal{W}}
-monoid
D+1
, while differential bundles are a reflective subcategory of functors from its idempotent splitting. Similarly, the category of anchored bundles are a reflective subcategory of the category
[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1},\mathbb{C}]
, where
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
is the category of 1-truncated Weil algebras (Definition
2.8
).

Section
3
reviews the basic idea of an enriched nerve/approximation. A particularly important example is the linear approximation of a reflexive graph, the functor introduced in Example
2.7
, which is the nerve of a functor

\partial:(\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1})^{op}\to\mathsf{Gph}({\mathcal{W}}).

Section
4
applies the enriched theories framework introduced in
Bourke and Garner 2019
, where a
dense
subcategory of a locally presentable
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
forms the “arities”, and a bijective-on-objects functor
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}
is the theory. The category of models is the pullback of (enriched) categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathcal{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{T},\mathcal{V}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]}}

The first step is to freely complete the
{\mathcal{W}}
-category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
of truncated Weil algebras (Definition
2.8
) so that its base anchored bundle has all prolongations; call this
{\mathcal{W}}
-category
\mathcal{L}
. Then, in every tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, the category of anchored bundles with chosen prolongations (Definition
3.4
) in
\mathbb{C}
embeds fully and faithfully into the functor category:

\mathsf{Anc}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{L},\mathbb{C}]

(here,
\mathsf{Anc}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbb{C})
denotes the category of anchored bundles with chosen prolongations).
In particular, the tangent bundle on
\mathbb{N}
in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
determines a bijective-on-objects functor

\mathcal{L}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}

so that the category of involution algebroids in any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is the pullback in
{\mathcal{W}}
Cat:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inv}^{*}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Anc}^{*}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{\mathcal{L}}^{op},\mathbb{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

This means that the category of involution algebroids in
{\mathcal{W}}
is monadic over the category of anchored bundles in
{\mathcal{W}}
using the monad-theory correspondence from
Bourke and Garner 2019
.

The final section looks at the category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids. Essentially, the free groupoid over the linear approximation of a graph will now give an infinitesimal object
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
. The nerve of
\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
—the
infinitesimal approximation
—has a right adjoint via the
realization
of the nerve functor from Definition
3.5
. This is used to prove the culminating Theorem
8
.

The individual pieces of categorical machinery used in this chapter are not new (the enriched perspective on tangent categories, enriched nerve constructions, enriched theories). However, all of the results dealing with the application of enriched nerve constructions and enriched theories to tangent categories is original work of the author.

1
Tangent categories via enrichment

This section gives a quick introduction to Garner’s enriched perspective on tangent categories. The enriched approach to tangent categories first appeared in
Garner 2018
and builds on the category perspective on tangent categories introduced in
Leung 2017
. Garner was able to exhibit some of the major results from synthetic differential geometry as pieces of enriched category theory; for example, the Yoneda lemma implies the existence of a well-adapted model of synthetic differential geometry.

The category of Weil spaces is the site of enrichment for tangent categories and is closely related to Dubuc’s
Weil topos
from his original work on models of synthetic differential geometry
Dubuc 1981
; a deeper study of this topos may be found in
Bertram 2014
. Recall that the category
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is the free tangent category over a single object. The category of Weil spaces is the
cofree
tangent category over
\mathsf{Set}
, which is the category of transverse-limit-preserving functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
by Observation
2.5
. Call this the category of
Weil spaces
, and write it
{\mathcal{W}}
. Just as a simplicial set
S:\Delta\to\mathsf{Set}
is a gadget recording homotopical data, a Weil space records
infinitesimal
data.

Definition 1.1
.

A
Weil space
is a functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
that preserves transverse limits (Definition
1.5
): that is, the
\otimes

A morphism of Weil spaces is a natural transformation. Write the category of Weil spaces as
{\mathcal{W}}
.

Example 1.2
.

(i)

Every commutative monoid may be regarded as a Weil space canonically. Observe that for every
V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
and commutative monoid
M
, one has the free
V
-module structure on
M
given by
|V|\otimes_{\mathsf{CMon}}M
(here
|V|
is the underlying commutative monoid of
V
). The commutative monoid
|V|
is exactly
\mathbb{N}^{\mathsf{\dim V}}
, so that

|V|\otimes_{\mathsf{CMon}}M\cong\oplus^{\dim V}M.

This agrees with the usual tangent structure on a category with biproducts.

(ii)

Following (i), any tangent category
\mathbb{C}
that is
concrete
—that is, admitting a faithful functor
U:\mathbb{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
—will have a natural functor into Weil spaces (copresheaves on
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
). Every object
A
will have an underlying Weil space
V\mapsto U_{\mathsf{Set}}(T^{V}(A))
, and whenever
U
preserves connected limits (such as the forgetful functor from commutative monoids to sets), each of the underlying copresheaves will be a Weil space.

(iii)

Consider a symmetric monoidal category with an infinitesimal object, which by Proposition
2.7
is a transverse-colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functor
D:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
. Then for every object
X
, the nerve (Definition
3.1
)
N_{D}(X):\mathbb{C}(D-,X):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a Weil space.

(iv)

For any pair of objects
A,B
in a tangent category,
\mathbb{C}(A,T^{(-)}B):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a Weil space by the continuity of
\mathbb{C}(B,-):\mathbb{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
.

Unlike the category of simplicial sets, the category of Weil spaces is not a topos.
The category of Weil spaces does, however, inherit some nice properties from the topos of copresheaves on
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
by applying results from
Section

3
, as it is
locally presentable
. The basics of locally presentable categories can be found in the Appendix
Appendix: Background on locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-categories
. Roughly speaking, a cocomplete category
\mathbb{C}
has a subcategory of finitely presentable objects
\mathbb{C}_{fp}
, those
C
so that

\mathbb{C}(C,-):\mathbb{C}\to\mathsf{Set}

preserves
filtered
colimits (i.e. those colimit diagrams that commute with all finite limits in
\mathsf{Set}
).
\mathbb{C}
is locally finitely presentable whenever every object is given by the coend

C\cong\int^{X\in\mathbb{C}_{fp}}\mathbb{C}(X,C)\cdot X

where
S\cdot X
is the (possibly infinite) product
X^{|S|}
with
|S|
the cardinality of the set
S
. This means that
\mathbb{C}=\mathsf{Lex}(\mathbb{C}_{fp}^{op},\mathsf{Set})
, where
\mathsf{Lex}
means the category of finite-limit-preserving functors.

The third point of Corollary
1.4
below, that
{\mathcal{W}}
is locally finitely presentable as a cartesian monoidal category, means that the category
{\mathcal{W}}_{fp}
is closed under products. This implies that
{\mathcal{W}}
is locally presentable
as a

{\mathcal{W}}
-category (this ends up being an important technical condition whereby locally presentable
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories make sense). Whenever we discuss an arbitrary
\mathcal{V}
-category, we will assume that
\mathcal{V}
is locally presentable as a monoidal category.

Proposition 1.3

(
Garner 2018
)
.

The category of Weil spaces is a cartesian-monoidal reflective subcategory of
[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathsf{Set}]
.

Corollary 1.4
.

The category of Weil spaces is

(i)

a cartesian closed category;

(ii)

a representable tangent category, where the infinitesimal object is given by the restricted Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{Y}:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to{\mathcal{W}}
;

(iii)

Locally finitely presentable as a cartesian monoidal category.

The cofree tangent structure on
{\mathcal{W}}
is given by precomposition, that is:

T^{U}.M.(V)=M.(U\otimes V)=M.T^{U}.V.

This tangent coincides with the representable tangent structure induced by the Yoneda embedding. The proof is an application of the Yoneda lemma. Observe that

[D,M](V)\cong[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathsf{Set}](D\times\mathcal{Y}(V),M)\cong[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathsf{Set}](\mathcal{Y}(WV),M)\cong M(WV)

where
D\times\mathcal{Y}(V)=\mathcal{Y}(WV)
follows because the tensor product in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is cocartesian and the reflector is cartesian monoidal.

Notation 1.5
.

The tangent category
{\mathcal{W}}
is a representable tangent category, where
D=\mathcal{Y}W
(Definition
3.7
).
We will write the Yoneda functor
\mathcal{Y}:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to{\mathcal{W}}
as
D(-)
, so it is closer to the usual notation used in representable tangent categories or synthetic differential geometry (a single
D
may be used as shorthand for
D(W)
,
D(n)
for
D(W_{n})
, etc.).
Note that

D(V)=D(\otimes^{k}W_{n(i)})=\prod^{K}D(n_{i})

and that
D(\ell)=\otimes,D(c)=(\pi_{1},\pi_{0})
,
D(+)=\delta
, and so on.

At this point, we are ready to move to the enriched perspective on tangent categories. The basics of enriched category theory may be found in the Appendix
Appendix: Background on locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-categories
, but one definition in particular is important to include here.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-category for
\mathcal{V}
a closed symmetric monoidal category. For
J\in\mathcal{V}
, the
power
by
J
of an object
C\in\mathbb{C}
is an object
C^{J}
so that the following is an isomorphism:

\forall D:\mathcal{V}(J,\mathbb{C}(D,C))\cong\mathbb{C}(D,C^{J})

whereas the
copower
is given by

\forall D:\mathcal{V}(J,\mathbb{C}(C,D))\cong\mathbb{C}(J\bullet C,D).

Let
\mathcal{J}\hookleftarrow\mathcal{V}
be a full monoidal subcategory of
\mathcal{V}
. A
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathbb{C}
has
coherently chosen powers
by
\mathcal{J}
if there is a choice of
\mathcal{J}
-powers
(-)^{J}
so that

(C^{J})^{K}=C^{J\otimes K}.

Likewise,
coherently chosen copowers
are a choice of
\mathcal{J}
-copowers so that

K\bullet(J\bullet C)=(K\otimes J)\bullet C.

The sub-2-categories of
\mathcal{V}
-categories equipped with coherently chosen powers and copowers are
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{Cat}^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{Cat}_{\mathcal{J}}
, respectively.

Wood 1978
proved that the 2-category of actegories over a monoidal
\mathbb{C}
is equivalent to the 2-category of
[\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Set}]
-enriched categories with powers by representable functors (Definition
1.6
), using the monoidal structure on
[\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Set}]
induced by Day convolution
1
1

1

The
Day convolution
tensor product of presheaves
X,Y:\mathbb{C}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
is given by
X\widehat{\otimes}Y:=\mathsf{Lan}_{\otimes}(X\boxtimes Y)
, where
(X\times Y)(V)=X(V)\times Y(V)
(
Day 1970
).

.
Moreover,
Garner 2018
showed that a monoidal reflective subcategory
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathcal{A}}
exhibits the 2-category of
\mathcal{V}
-categories as a reflective sub-2-category of
\mathcal{A}
-categories; this proves that tangent categories are equivalent to a particular class of enriched category.

Proposition 1.7

(
Garner 2018
)
.

A tangent category is exactly a
{\mathcal{W}}
-category with powers by representables.

Proof.

For every
A,B\in\mathbb{C}_{0}
, the Weil space is defined as

\underline{\mathbb{C}}(A,B):=U\mapsto\mathbb{C}(A,T^{U}B).

The functor
\mathbb{C}(A,-)
is continuous and
T^{-}B
is an infinitesimally linear functor, so this is a Weil space. The following diagram gives the composition map :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathbb{C}}(B,C)\times\underline{\mathbb{C}}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\underline{\mathbb{C}}(A,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,T^{U}C)\times\mathbb{C}(A,T^{V}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,T^{VU}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(T^{V}B,T^{V}.T^{U}C)\times\mathbb{C}(A,T^{V}B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{V}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Note that it is natural in
U
and
V
.

By the Yoneda lemma (as the internal hom in
{\mathcal{W}}
is the internal hom of copresheaves on
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
),

\mathbb{C}(A,T^{V}B)=(U\mapsto\mathbb{C}(A,B)(V\otimes U))=[D(V),\mathbb{C}(A,B)]

so this category has coherently chosen powers by representable functors.
∎

Now the original notions of (lax, strong, strict) tangent functors can be shown to correspond to power-preservation properties of
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors between
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories with coherently chosen powers:

Theorem 1.8

(
Garner 2018
)
.

We have the following equivalences of 2-categories:

(i)

the 2-category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories with coherently chosen powers and
\mathsf{TangCat}_{\mathsf{Lax}}
;

(ii)

the 2-category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories with coherently chosen powers and power-preserving
{\mathcal{W}}
functors and
\mathsf{TangCat}_{\mathsf{Strong}}
;

(iii)

The 2-category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories with coherently chosen powers and chosen-power-preserving
{\mathcal{W}}
functors and
\mathsf{TangCat}_{\mathsf{Strict}}
.

Note that for a lax tangent functor
(F,\alpha):\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
, the map

\alpha.X:F.T.X\to T.F.X

can be seen as the unique morphism induced by universality. Conversely, for a strong tangent functor, the natural isomorphism
\alpha
is the isomorphism from
a
power
F.T.X
to the
coherently chosen
power
T.F.X
. In contrast, a strict tangent functor preserves the coherent choice of powers.

The ability to work with
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories that do not have powers by representables allows for significant flexibility. It is useful to observe that there are
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories which are not tangent categories.

Example 1.9
.

(i)

Every monoid
(M,m,e)
in
{\mathcal{W}}
gives rise to a one-object
{\mathcal{W}}
-category whose hom-object is
M
, composition is
m
, and unit is
e
.

(ii)

Given a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
, it is possible to take the full
{\mathcal{W}}
-category over some set of objects
\mathbb{D}
, even though
D
may not be closed under iterated applications of the tangent functor.

(iii)

The dual of a
{\mathcal{W}}
-category is a
{\mathcal{W}}
category, where

\mathbb{C}^{op}(A,B)=\mathbb{C}(B,A):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}.

Dually, in the case that
\mathbb{C}
is a tangent category,
\mathbb{C}^{op}
will have coherent
copowers
by representables.

(iv)

If a cartesian category
\mathbb{C}
has an infinitesimal object
D
(Definition
3.7
), it has a natural
{\mathcal{W}}
-category structure (with coherent
copowers
by representables)

\mathbb{C}(A,B):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}:=\underline{\mathbb{C}}(A\times D(-),B).

Using the dual tangent structure on
\mathbb{C}^{op}
from Proposition
3.8
, the enrichment on
\mathbb{C}
is exactly the enrichment found by regarding
\mathbb{C}
as the dual
{\mathcal{W}}
-category of the tangent category
\mathbb{C}^{op}
.

As a final remark, note that the Yoneda lemma applies to
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories, so there is an embedding

\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow[\mathbb{C}^{op},\mathsf{Set}].

The powers and copowers by representables are computed pointwise in a presheaf category, so they inherit the coherent choice. Thus the following holds:

Corollary 1.10

(
Garner 2018
)
.

Every tangent category embeds into a
{\mathcal{W}}
-cocomplete representable tangent category.

(In fact, showing that the Yoneda embedding applies to tangent categories is the main theorem of
Garner 2018
.)

2
Differential and anchored bundles as enriched structures

This section gives an enriched-categorical reinterpretation of the work in Chapter
5
regarding differential bundles and Chapters
6
and
7
regarding anchored bundles.

Differential bundles as enriched structures

As a first case study using the enriched perspective for tangent categories, consider differential bundles. Most of the work in
Chapter

5
uses the intuition that differential bundles are some sort of tangent-categorical algebraic theory; this section will make that intuition concrete. Recall that a
lift
(Definition
2.3
) is a map
\lambda:E\to TE
. Using the enriched perspective and treating
TE
as a power (recall Definition
1.6
), this gives the following correspondence:

\hat{\lambda}:D\to\mathbb{C}(E,E)\lambda:1\to\mathbb{C}(E,E^{D})

The commutativity condition for
\hat{\lambda}
, then, is translated as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(E,E)\times\mathbb{C}(E,E)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(E,E)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{E,E,E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\lambda}\times\hat{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}

That is,
\hat{\lambda}
is a semigroup morphism
D\to\mathbb{C}(E,E)
. In any cartesian closed category with coproducts, a semigroup may be freely lifted to a monoid using the ”exception monad”
(-)+1
from functional programming (see, for example,
Seal 2013
).

Definition 2.1
.

Regard the following monoid as the one-object
{\mathcal{W}}
-category
\Lambda
:

m:(D+1)\times(D+1)\to D+1D\times D+D+D+1\xrightarrow{(\iota_{L}\circ m|\iota_{L}|\iota_{L}|\iota_{R})}D+1

Thus, a lift
\lambda
is exactly a functor
\hat{\lambda}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
. Now check that morphisms are tangent natural transformations. Note that the semigroup
D
is commutative, so
\Lambda=\Lambda^{op}
; the choice of using
\Lambda^{op}
in the next lemma is to be consistent with conventions used in
Section

3
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The category of lifts in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to the category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors and
{\mathcal{W}}
-natural transformations
\Lambda^{op}\to\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Check that a
{\mathcal{W}}
-natural transformation is exactly a morphism of lifts
f:\lambda\to\lambda^{\prime}
. Start with the
{\mathcal{W}}
-naturality square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,A)\times\mathbb{C}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,B)\times\mathbb{C}(B,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda,f\circ!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\circ!,l)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{ABB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{AAB}}

Now, rewriting
D+1\to\mathbb{C}(A,B)
as a semigroup map
D\to\mathbb{C}(A,B)
, we have:

1\xrightarrow{Tf\circ\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{C}(A,TB)1\xrightarrow{(\lambda^{\prime},f)}\mathbb{C}(A,TA)\times\mathbb{C}(A,B)\xrightarrow{1\times T}\mathbb{C}(A,TA)\times\mathbb{C}(TA,TB)\xrightarrow{m_{A,TA,TB}}\mathbb{C}(A,TB)D\xrightarrow{(\lambda,f\circ!)}\mathbb{C}(A,A)\times\mathbb{C}(A,B)\xrightarrow{m_{AAB}}\mathbb{C}(A,B)

Similarly, the other path is exactly
\lambda^{\prime}\circ f
. Thus, a
{\mathcal{W}}
-natural transformation is exactly a morphism of lifts. ∎

It is a classical result in synthetic differential geometry that the object
D
has only one point. In the case of
{\mathcal{W}}
, this follows from the Yoneda lemma (regarding
{\mathcal{W}}
as a
\mathsf{Set}
-category):

{\mathcal{W}}(1,D)=\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2},\mathbb{N})=\{!:\mathbb{N}[x]/x^{2}\to\mathbb{N}\}

Note that the natural idempotent
e:id\Rightarrow id
from Proposition
2.8
, then, must be the point
0:1\to D
. Note that this idempotent is an absorbing element of the monoid
D+1
, so for any
f:X\to D+1
, it follows that
m(f,0\circ!)=m(0\circ!,f)=0\circ!
. A pre-differential bundle is exactly a lift with a chosen splitting of the natural idempotent
p\circ\lambda
.

Lemma 2.3
.

For every lift
\bar{\lambda}:\Lambda^{op}\to\mathbb{C}
, the natural idempotent
e=p\circ\lambda
is exactly

1\xrightarrow{0}D\xrightarrow{\iota_{L}}D+1\to\mathbb{C}(E,E).

Now that the natural idempotent is understood as a map in
\Lambda
, that idempotent splits to give the theory of a pre-differential object.

Definition 2.4
.

The
{\mathcal{W}}
-category
\Lambda^{+}
is given by the set of objects
\{0,1\}
with hom-Weil-spaces. Specifically,

•

The hom-spaces are
\Lambda^{+}(1,1)=D+1
, otherwise
\Lambda^{+}(i,j)=1
.

•

Composition (writing the original composition from
\Lambda
as
m
) is given by

\displaystyle m_{111}:(D+1)\times(D+1)\xrightarrow{m}(D+1)

\displaystyle m_{101}:1\times 1\xrightarrow{\iota_{R}}(D+1)

\displaystyle\text{otherwise: }m_{ijk}=!

Idempotent splittings are absolute (co)limits, and are preserved by all functors; as this is a limit completion, we have the following.

Lemma 2.5
.

The category of pre-differential bundles is exactly the category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors
\Lambda^{+}\to\mathbb{C}
(that is,
\mathbb{C}
-valued presheaves).

It is straightforward to exhibit the category of differential bundles as a reflective subcategory of pre-differential bundles in
[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]
(so long as
\mathbb{C}
has equalizers).

Proposition 2.6
.

The category of differential bundles in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
with
T
-equalizers and
T
-pullbacks is a reflective subcategory of
[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]
.

Proof.

The category of pre-differential bundles in
\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to
[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]
. By Corollary
4.8
, the category of differential bundles is the category of algebras for an idempotent monad on the category of pre-differential bundles in
\mathbb{C}
. The reflector sends a pre-differential bundle to the
T
-equalizer:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TE}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e}

This equalizer will always exist if
\mathbb{C}
has equalizers, and pullbacks of the projection will exist if
\mathbb{C}
has pullbacks, so the pullback is a differential bundle. This reflection gives a left-exact idempotent monad on
[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]
whose algebras are differential bundles.
∎

Now, in the case that
\mathbb{C}
is a
locally presentable
tangent category (such as
{\mathcal{W}}
),
[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]
is locally presentable and so is the reflective subcategory of differential bundles; thus the following holds.

Corollary 2.7
.

If
\mathbb{C}
is locally presentable, then
\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})
is a locally presentable category.

Anchored bundles

There are two ways to think about anchored bundles:

(i)

an anchored bundle is a differential bundle with an anchor
A\to TM
, or

(ii)

an anchored bundle is an involution algebroid without an involution.

These two perspectives can be unified by regarding
A
as a cylinder for the weighted limit
TM
, so that the anchor is induced by the unique map
A.T.0\to T.A.0
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Lambda^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}

That is, the syntactic category for anchored bundles is constructed as a full
{\mathcal{W}}
-category of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
that doesn’t include the map
c
. This may be found by taking the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
whose objects are constructed out of
W_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 2.8
.

A Weil algebra has
width

k\in\mathbb{N}
if it can be written

V=\otimes^{0\leq i<k}W_{n(i)},\;n(i)\in\mathbb{N}

The category of
k
-truncated Weil algebras, written
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{k}
, is the full
{\mathcal{W}}
-subcategory of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
of Weil algebras of width
k
or less.

The full subcategory whose objects are
\{\mathbb{N},W\}
will be given the special notation
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}
.

Note that for each
V
in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{k}
, the enrichment is given by

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{k}(U,V):=(X\mapsto\underline{\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}(U,X\otimes V)).

The maps in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
—that is, the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
whose objects are

\{W_{n}|n\in\mathbb{N}\}

—have the useful property that they may be written without the flip
c
. This makes
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
a natural candidate for the syntactic category of anchored bundles.

Lemma 2.9
.

Every morphism

W_{n}\to V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}

may be written without
c
; that is, it is generated by the set of maps
\{p,+,0,\ell\}
closed under tensor, composition, and maps induced by transverse limits.

Proof.

Every map
W_{n}\to V
in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
is the finite sum

v\mapsto\sum_{x}(A_{x}v)\bullet x

where
x\in\mathsf{var}(V)
, and
A_{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
so that
A_{n}v
is the ordinary dot product.
Each term can be written without
c
, and the whole term is then constructed by adding each component using the appropriate
U.+.V
-symbols.
2
2

2

This may also be regarded as a consequence of the graphical notation for maps in
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
in Table 1 on page 308 of
Leung 2017
.

∎

It is possible, then, to show that the category of anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
is a full sub-tangent-category of functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}
; note that
W
acts as a cone for
(-)^{D}
, so this induces a map

\varrho:F(W)\to T.F(\mathbb{N}).

Proposition 2.10
.

Every anchored differential bundle in
\mathbb{C}
determines a functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}
; an anchored bundle morphism is exactly an enriched natural transformation.

Proof.

Start with an anchored bundle
(q:E\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
; then for hom-objects with domain
\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N})=1\mapsto id_{M},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}(\mathbb{N},T)=1\mapsto\xi.

For the hom-objects with domain
T
, the problem is slightly more difficult, as
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}(T,\mathbb{N})(T^{V})=\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(T,T^{V})
and
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}(T,T)(T)=\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(T,T.T^{V})
. This part of the proof amounts to constructing maps

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(T,T^{V})\to\mathbb{C}(E,T^{V}.M),\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(T,T^{V}.T)\to\mathbb{C}(E,T^{V}.E)/

The first mapping is straightforward: send
\theta
to
\theta.M\circ\varrho
. For the second map, observe that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+.M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}

The idea is to take an anchored bundle morphism
f
and rewrite it as a string of compositions that does not include
c
, switching out every occurrence of

T^{V}.\theta.M,\theta\in\{p,0,+,\ell\}

and replacing it with
T^{V}.(\theta^{\prime})
, where
\theta^{\prime}
is the corresponding map in
\{q,\xi,+_{q},\lambda\}
. This induces a map

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}(W,W)\to\mathbb{C}(E,E)\in{\mathcal{W}}

and the
\varrho
is exactly the unique
\alpha:F.W\to T.F
induced by universality.

For morphisms, the inclusion
(\Lambda^{+})^{op}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
ensures that any tangent natural transformation will be a linear morphism on the underlying differential bundle, and the tangent natural transformations coherences ensure that a tangent natural transformation will preserve the anchor. Conversely, an anchored bundle morphism will preserve each of the constructed morphisms
E\to T^{V}.E,E\to T^{V}.M
(as it preserves each of
\{q,+_{q},\xi,\lambda\}
), giving a natural tangent transformation. Thus there is a faithful embedding
\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1},\mathbb{C}]
.
∎

The converse, identifying those functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}\to\mathbb{C}
that determine anchored bundles, is immediate.

Corollary 2.11
.

The category of anchored bundles comprises precisely the
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors and
{\mathcal{W}}
-natural transformations

A:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
(Definition
2.8
)
so that the precomposition
\Lambda^{+}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
determines a differential bundle.
That is to say, the category of anchored bundles in
\mathbb{C}
is the following pullback in
{\mathcal{W}}
Cat:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Anc}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1},\mathbb{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DBun}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Lambda^{+},\mathbb{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

3
Enriched nerve constructions

Nerve constructions present a powerful generalization of the Yoneda functor that sends an object
C\in\mathbb{C}
to the representable presheaf
\mathbb{C}(-,C)\in[\mathbb{C}^{op},\mathcal{V}]
(for a general reference on nerve constructions and their realizations, see Chapter 3 of
Loregian 2021
).
The Yoneda lemma states that this functor is an
embedding
(that is, it is fully faithful), so no information is lost when embedding a category into its category of presheaves.
Nerve constructions, then, move this towards an
approximation
of the original category
\mathbb{C}
by some subcategory
\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, or more generally by some functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
In Section
5
, the “infinitesimal” approximation of a Lie groupoid as a Lie algebroid will be exhibited as approximation by a
{\mathcal{W}}
-functor
\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
.

We work with a
\mathcal{V}
that is locally presentable as a monoidal category, such as
{\mathcal{W}},\mathsf{Set}
, or the category of commutative monoids.

Definition 3.1
.

The
nerve
of a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is the functor

N_{K}:\mathbb{C}\to[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}];\hskip 14.22636ptC\mapsto\mathbb{C}(K-,C)

Any presheaf
A:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{V}
that is in the image of
N_{K}
is a
K
-nerve
.

Remark 3.2
.

The “
K
-nerve’’ terminology seems to go back to Grothendieck/Segals’s original intuition for the nerve construction of a category
3
3

3

Segal published the result, but seems to have credited the theorem to Grothendieck.
(
Segal 1974
) and appears in, for example,
Berger et al. 2012
and
Bourke and Garner 2019
. However, the “approximation” of a category by a functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
originally used in topology seems to be a more intuitive description of the functor
N_{K}
.

Example 3.3
.

(i)

The nerve of the identity functor
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}
is the usual Yoneda embedding
\mathbb{C}\hookrightarrow[\mathbb{C}^{op},{\mathcal{W}}]
.

(ii)

The first example of a nerve construction in the mathematical literature is the simplicial approximation of a topological space by
Kan 1958
. Recall the original construction of the simplicial nerve of a topological space
X
, where
X_{n}=\mathsf{Top}(\Delta_{n},X)
. This is exactly the nerve of the functor
\Delta\to\mathsf{Top}
that sends
n
to the
n
-simplex

\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}|\sum x_{i}=1\right\}.

(iii)

The following example figures into Segal’s original nerve construction for a category, and is revisited in Section
4
. Define a reflexive graph to be a presheaf over the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Cat}
whose objects are the two preorders

[0]=0,\hskip 5.69046pt[1]=0<1.

This is equivalent to the free category with two parallel arrows and a common retract,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

so that a graph in a category has an object-of-vertices
V
and an object-of-edges
E
, along with source and target maps
s,t:E\to V
; morphisms of graphs are maps
(f_{E},f_{V})
that commute with the source and target maps.

By Corollary
4.3
, any full subcategory containing the representables will be dense.
Define the category of
paths
,
\mathsf{Pth}
, to be full subcategory of graphs generated by

so that
\mathsf{Gph}([n],G)
picks out the set of paths of length
n
in a graph
G
. We call this subcategory
P:\mathsf{Pth}\to\mathsf{Gph}
, and see that
N_{P}
sends a graph to its
\mathsf{Pth}
-presheaf of composable paths:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

The pushout
[n]
is precisely the graph

0\xrightarrow{}1\xrightarrow{}\dots\xrightarrow{}(n-1)\xrightarrow{}n

where the reflexive map
e
at each vertex is suppressed.

Recall the functor sending reflexive graphs to anchored bundles from Example
2.7
(iv).
This may be restated as a nerve construction.

Proposition 3.4
.

There is a functor

\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\to\mathsf{Gph}({\mathcal{W}})

so that
N_{\partial}:\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})\to[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*},{\mathcal{W}}]
is the linear approximation of a reflexive graph as described in Example
2.7
(iv).

Proof.

Let
s,t:C\to M,e:M\to C
be a reflexive graph, and recall that the anchored bundle
C^{\partial}\to C
is induced by the equalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\partial}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T.C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.T_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e^{s}}

The category
\mathsf{Gph}
is a representable tangent category, where
D
is the discrete graph
D=D
. By the Yoneda lemma, we have that

[\mathsf{Gph},{\mathcal{W}}](\mathcal{Y}1,G)=G_{1},[\mathsf{Gph},{\mathcal{W}}](\mathcal{Y}0,G)=G_{0},[\mathsf{Gph},{\mathcal{W}}](D\times\mathcal{Y}(i),G)=T.G_{i},

so the limit diagram defining the infinitesimal approximation of a graph becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\partial}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gph}(D\times I,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gph}(D\times I,C).}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(e\times I)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\times e^{-})^{*}}

Now observe that
\mathsf{Gph}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}
with the dual tangent structure from Proposition
3.8
has a reflexive graph object

s,t:1\to I,!:I\to 1.

Construct its linear approximation as in Example
2.7
(iv) (e.g. take the coequalizer of
{\mathcal{W}}
-graphs):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times e^{s}}

By Proposition
2.10
, this determines a functor

\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}\to\mathsf{Gph}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}.

By the continuity of the hom-functor, the nerve
N_{\partial}:\mathsf{Gph}({\mathcal{W}})\to[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*},{\mathcal{W}}]
lands in the category of anchored bundles. The continuity of the hom-functor ensures that this is indeed the linear approximation from Example
2.7
(iv).
∎

The simplicial localization in
Kan 1958
has a left adjoint, the
geometric realization
, that constructs a topological space using the data of a simplicial set. This realization may be constructed using a left Kan extension.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-functor for a cocomplete
\,\mathbb{C}
.
The
realization
of
K
is the left Kan extension

\hbox to101.22pt{\vbox to48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.89424pt\lower-23.5472pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-37.89424pt}{-19.88747pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
