A
nerve/realization context
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
from a small
A
to a cocomplete
\mathbb{C}
.

The adjunction between simplicial sets and topological spaces follows from general categorical machinery as a nerve/realization context:

Lemma 3.6
.

For every nerve/realization context
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
, the realization of
K
is left adjoint to the nerve of
K
.

4
Nervous monads and algebroids

Transposing the characterization of algebroids from Theorem
3.9
to the enriched perspective introduces the challenge of finding an appropriate framework to describe algebroids as enriched structures. Kelly’s theory of enriched sketches (see Chapter 6 of
Kelly 2005
), small
\mathcal{V}
-categories equipped with a chosen class of limits cones, seems to be a natural candidate. However, when regarding a tangent functor as a
{\mathcal{W}}
-functor, the natural part

\alpha:F.T\Rightarrow T.F

is the unique morphism induced by the universality of
T
as a weighted limit, so it becomes unclear how to translate the condition that
\alpha
is a cartesian natural transformation (Definition
4.1
).

A clue for how to proceed may be found in
Kapranov 2007
, which proved that Lie algebroids are monadic over anchored bundles (when allowing for infinite-dimensional vector bundles).
Recent work in
Bourke and Garner 2019
and
Berger et al. 2012
has developed the appropriate notion of (enriched) theories that correspond to monads over general locally presentable categories. A critical insight is that for a filtered-colimit-preserving monad
\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{Set}
, the opposite category of the Lawvere theory
\mathcal{T}
is precisely the full subcategory of the Kleisli category whose objects are
[n]=\coprod_{n}1
, and the
nerve
of the inclusion

\mathcal{T}^{op}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{T}}

is fully faithful; that is, when the functor
K
is dense.
Formally, a functor is
dense
whenever its nerve behaves like the Yoneda embedding. The theory of dense functors is developed in Chapter 5 of
Kelly 2005
. We continue to assume that an arbitrary site of enrichment
\mathcal{V}
is locally presentable as a
\mathcal{V}
-category.

Definition 4.1
.

A functor
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is
dense
whenever the nerve of
K
is fully faithful, and a subcategory inclusion that is dense will be called a
dense subcategory
.

Dense functors are poorly behaved under composition, but there is a useful cancellativity result from Section 5.2 of
Kelly 2005
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Consider a diagram of
\mathcal{V}
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where
\alpha
is a natural isomorphism and
K
is dense.
If this diagram exhibits
(\alpha,J)
as the left Kan extension of
K
along
F
, then
J
is also dense.

Corollary 4.3
.

If
K
is dense,
F
is fully faithful, and
J.F=K
, then
F
is a dense subcategory.

Generally speaking, we will often refer to dense
subcategories
rather than dense
functors
.
This is achieved by factoring the dense functor

K=\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{K^{\prime}}\mathsf{im}(K)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}

where
\mathsf{im}(K)
is the
\mathcal{V}
-category whose objects are those of
\mathcal{A}
and whose hom-objects are given by
\mathsf{im}(K)(A,B)=\mathbb{C}(KA,KB)
, so that the inclusion of
\mathsf{im}(K)
into
\mathbb{C}
is fully faithful and therefore dense by Corollary
4.3
.

Example 4.4
.

(i)

The category of finite cardinals
\Sigma
is the full subcategory of
\,\mathsf{Set}
whose objects are given by finite coproducts of the terminal object,
[n]=\coprod_{n}1
. This is a skeleton of the category of finite sets, and a dense subcategory of
\,\mathsf{Set}
(see e.g.
Bourke and Garner 2019
).

(ii)

Recall that the category of
\mathcal{V}
-presheaves on
\mathcal{A}
,
[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]
is the free colimit completion of
\mathcal{A}
(see e.g.
Kelly 2005
).
If
\mathbb{C}
is cocomplete, and
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
is dense, then the realization
|-|_{K}
exhibits
\mathbb{C}
as a reflective subcategory of
[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]
, and is this a locally presentable category
4
4

4

In fact, an equivalent definition of a locally presentable category is as a cocomplete category with a dense subcategory.
. Conversely, if
\,\mathbb{C}
is a reflective subcategory of
\,[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]
that contains the representable functors, then
\mathcal{A}
is a dense subcategory of
\,\mathbb{C}
.

(iii)

By Corollary
2.7
, the category of differential bundles in
{\mathcal{W}}
is a reflective subcategory of
[{\Lambda^{+}}{op},\mathcal{V}]
, so
\mathcal{Y}:\Lambda^{+}\hookrightarrow[{\Lambda^{+}}{op},\mathcal{V}]
is a dense subcategory of differential bundles in
{\mathcal{W}}
following the argument in the above example.

Nervous theories (
Bourke and Garner 2019
) are generalizations of Lawvere theories that extend to arbitrary locally finitely presentable
\mathcal{V}
-categories. Recall that a classical Lawvere theory is a bijective-on-objects, product-preserving functor

t:\Sigma\to\mathcal{T}

where
\mathcal{T}
is a cartesian category. Nervous theories replace
\Sigma
with a dense subcategory of some locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-category, and the product preservation condition with conditions on the nerve of the theory map.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
be a dense
\mathcal{V}
-subcategory of a locally finitely presentable
\,\mathbb{C}
. We call the replete image of
N_{K}
in
[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]
the category of
K
-nerves. An
\mathcal{A}
-theory
is a bijective-on-objects
\mathcal{V}
-functor
J:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}
, where each

\mathcal{T}(J-,a):\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{V}

is a
K
-nerve.
The category of models for an
\mathcal{A}
-theory is the pullback in
\mathcal{V}\mathsf{CAT}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{\mathcal{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(These are called
concrete
models in
Bourke and Garner 2019
.)

Remark 4.6
.

The category of models for a theory is monadic. The core of the argument is due to Weber’s
nerve theorem
, found in
Weber 2007
, but an exposition on that result is beyond the scope of this thesis.

Example 4.7
.

(i)

A functor
t:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}
is a
\Sigma
-theory if and only if
\mathcal{T}
is a Lawvere theory, where we use the fact that
\Sigma
(Example
4.4
) a skeletal subcategory of finite sets. The nerve conditions in this case identify the models of the Lawvere theory, as the nerve of
\,\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Set}
sends a set to the strict product-preserving functor
[n]\mapsto A^{n}
.

(ii)

As shown in
Berger et al. 2012
and
Bourke and Garner 2019
, the original nerve construction from
Segal 1974
may be restated as saying that small categories arise as models of a
\mathsf{Pth}
-theory (Example
3.3
). The set
\mathsf{Gph}([n],G)
is the set of paths of through the graph
G
that have
n
non-identity elements, as

G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!G\cong G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{id}\!M{{}_{id}}\!\times_{s}\!G\cong G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s\circ e\circ s}\!G{{}_{t\circ e\circ t}}\!\times_{s}\!G.

Next, recall that the category
\Delta
may be regarded as follows:

•

Objects: A strict order
[n]=0<1<\dots<n
, for
n\geq 0
, regarded as a category.

•

Maps: Functors.

There is a bijective-on-objects functor from
\mathsf{Pth}\to\Delta
that sends the graph
[n]
to the pre-order
[n]
, as every graph homomorphism between paths will be order-preserving. Now observe that a model is precisely a simplicial set, where

X([0])=M,X([1])=C,X([2])=C{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C,X([3])=C{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C.

Furthermore, the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}

in
\Delta
becomes a composition map:

C{{}_{t}}\!\times_{s}\!C\to CX([2])\to X([1])

Associativity and unitality (for the section
e:M\to C
) follow by functoriality. Thus, a model of
\mathsf{Pth}\to\Delta
is a small category, and a morphism is exactly a functor.

Corollary
2.11
, then, gives a monadicity result for
{\mathcal{W}}
-anchored bundles over
{\mathcal{W}}
-differential bundles.

Proposition 4.8
.

{\mathcal{W}}
-anchored bundles are models of the theory
(\Lambda^{+})^{op}\to(\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*})^{op}
(see Section
2
).

Proof.

Note that the inclusion
\Lambda^{+}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
is a differential bundle, so it is a nerve. Then the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*},{\mathcal{W}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{DBun}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Lambda^{+},{\mathcal{W}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

exhibits
{\mathcal{W}}
-anchored bundles as models of a
(\Lambda)^{op}
-theory.
∎

As a corollary, we see that the category of anchored bundles is locally presentable.

Corollary 4.9
.

The category of anchored bundles is locally presentable, and
(\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*})^{op}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})
is dense.

In
Bourke and Garner 2019
, the authors identify exactly those monads on a locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-category that correspond to the models of a theory.
Recall the notation that the category of algebras for a monad is
\mathbb{C}^{T}
and the category of free coalgebras is
\mathbb{C}_{T}
. For a dense subcategory
\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, use
\mathcal{A}_{T}
for the category of free algebras over objects in
\mathcal{A}
. Recall that the Lawvere theory

K:\mathsf{FinSet}\to\mathcal{T}

for a filtered-colimit-preserving monad
\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{Set}
may be re-derived as the full subcategory of free algebras over finite sets,
K_{T}:\mathsf{FinSet}_{\mathbb{T}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Set}_{\mathbb{T}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{T}}
. Nervous monads abstract this property.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-category, with
K:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
a dense sub-
\mathcal{V}
-category.
A
\mathcal{V}
-monad
\mathbb{T}
over
\mathbb{C}
is
K
-nervous
if

1.

the inclusion
K_{T}:\mathcal{A}_{T}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{T}
is dense;

2.

the following diagram is a pullback in
\mathcal{V}
CAT:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}^{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{A}_{T}^{op},\mathcal{V}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathcal{A}^{op},\mathcal{V}]}}

Theorem 4.11

(
Bourke and Garner 2019
)
.

Let
K:\mathcal{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a dense sub-
\mathcal{V}
-category of a cocomplete
\mathcal{V}
-category
\,\mathbb{C}
.
There is an equivalence of categories between
\mathcal{A}
-theories and
\mathcal{A}
-nervous monads on
\,\mathbb{C}
.

The first step in showing that algebroids are monadic over anchored bundles is to construct a dense subcategory of
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})
that plays the role of the
V
-prolongations from Definition
3.4
. The enriched framework makes this straightforward: prolongations are weighted limits, with which we may freely complete
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}
.

Definition 4.12
.

Consider the category
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})
as a representable tangent category, and take the dual tangent structure on
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}
.
The category
\mathcal{L}
is defined as the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}
whose objects are generated by the prolongations of the Yoneda embedding
\mathcal{Y}:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\to\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})
.

For any small
{\mathcal{W}}
-category
\mathbb{C}
, the free completion of
\mathbb{C}
is the opposite
{\mathcal{W}}
-category of the category of copresheaves on
\mathbb{C}
.
5
5

5

This is the dual statement of the classical theorem that the category of presheaves is the free cocompletion of a small category
\mathbb{C}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}
is the free completion of
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{1}
with prolongations. Therefore, a choice of prolongations on an anchored bundle
A
determines a unique functor
\mathcal{L}\to\mathbb{C}
given by right Kan extension (see e.g.
Kelly 1982
). The continuity of

\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})(-,A):\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}\to{\mathcal{W}}

ensures that
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})(L_{V},A)
is
A_{V}
.

By Theorem
3.9
, the category of involution algebroids in
\mathbb{C}
is a full sub-
{\mathcal{W}}
-category of
[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]
that has a forgetful functor down to the category of anchored bundles. A consequence of Theorem
4.8
is that a functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
is an involution algebroid if and only if the precomposition

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

restricts to an anchored bundle, with each
V
sent to the
V
-prolongation of this anchored bundle. In other words, algebroids are models of the following enriched theorem (as in Definition
4.5
).

Definition 4.13
.

Define the
\mathcal{L}^{op}
-theory of algebroids as the functor

a:\mathcal{L}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}.

This functor is bijective-on-objects by definition, and satisfies the nerve condition because the
V
-prolongation of the tangent bundle is
T^{V}
, so each presheaf

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(a-,V):\mathcal{L}\to{\mathcal{W}}

is an
\mathcal{L}
-nerve.

The Weil nerve, then, translates to the following characterization of the category of involution algebroids in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
.

Theorem 4.14
.

The category of involution algebroids with chosen prolongations in a tangent category
\mathbb{C}
is precisely the pullback in
{\mathcal{W}}\mathsf{CAT}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Inv^{*}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathsf{Weil}_{1},\mathbb{C}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Anc^{*}(\mathbb{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{\mathcal{L}},\mathbb{C}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(2)

Consequently, in
{\mathcal{W}}
involution algebroids are monadic over anchored bundles.

Proof.

Recall the correspondence

(\hat{A},\alpha):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}\in\mathsf{TangCat}_{lax}\bar{A}:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}\in{\mathcal{W}}\mathsf{Cat}

If

\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{*}\to\mathcal{L}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathbb{C}

determines an anchored bundle
(\pi:A\to M,\xi,\lambda,\varrho)
whose
V
-coprolongation is
\hat{A}.V
, so that
\hat{A}
is the nerve of an involution algebroid by Corollary
4.9
.
∎

Thus, we may characterize
{\mathcal{W}}
-involution algebroids as algebras for a
\mathcal{L}
-nervous monad on the category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-anchored bundles.

Corollary 4.15
.

The category of involution algebroids in
{\mathcal{W}}
is equivalent to the category of algebras for the
\mathcal{L}
-nervous monad on
\mathsf{Anc}({\mathcal{W}})
generated by the theory

a:\mathcal{L}\to\mathsf{Weil}_{1}

from Definition
4.13
, using Theorem 19 from
Bourke and Garner 2019
.

Remark 4.16
.

The construction of involution algebroids as the category of models for a
\mathcal{L}
-theory is remarkably similar to the original nerve construction for categories in
Segal 1974
, with the symmetric nerve construction for groupoids replacing
\Delta
with the category of finite sets
\Sigma
(see Example 44 (iv) of
Bourke and Garner 2019
). Each construction truncates the original category to two objects and builds a new category with a bijective set of objects by freely adding limits to the two-object truncation.

5
The infinitesimal approximation of a groupoid

This section contains the main theorem of the chapter, namely that there is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inv}({\mathcal{W}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

This adjunction follows by inducing a nerve/realization context on the category of groupoids in
{\mathcal{W}}
:

\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}}).

A simplified version of this functor appeared in Proposition
3.4
, the linear approximation of a reflexive graph.
In fact,
\partial
will be exactly the free groupoid over the graph
\partial
from Proposition
3.4
(if we had worked in the category of reflexive graphs equipped with an involution).
Recall that groupoids and Weil spaces are both models of a sketch (recall Section
1
).
The symmetry of the theories gives two equivalent presentations of groupoids in
{\mathcal{W}}
:

Definition 5.1
.

The tangent category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids is equivalently

(i)

the tangent category of internal groupoids in
{\mathcal{W}}
, where the tangent structure is computed pointwise;

(ii)

the tangent category of transverse-limit-preserving functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Gpd}(\mathsf{Set})
with the cofree tangent structure from Observation
2.5
.

It is important to note that the tangent structure on
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids is representable, as it is a cartesian closed category with an infinitesimal object given by
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{W}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
(a proof that
\mathsf{Gpd}(\mathbb{C})
is cartesian closed for a cartesian closed category
\mathbb{C}
with sufficient limits a may be found in Section B2.3 of
Johnstone 2002
).

There are two classes of “trivial”
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids that will be useful.

Example 5.2
.

(i)

Every small groupoid
G
may be regarded as a groupoid in
{\mathcal{W}}
as the constant functor
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{Gpd}(\mathsf{Set})
sending
V
to the groupoid
G
preserves transverse limits, we may then apply the symmetry of theories to find a groupoid in the category of Weil spaces.

(ii)

Every object
M
in a category has a cofree groupoid given by
s,t:M=M
, this is the
discrete
groupoid whose only morphisms are the identity maps. Thus, every Weil space
M\in{\mathcal{W}}
has a corresponding discrete groupoid (this will often be written
M=M
to denote the discrete groupoid over
M
).

Using the cartesian closure of
{\mathcal{W}}
and the full subcategories of trivial and discrete groupoids, we have the following:

Observation 5.3
.

The category of
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids is both

(i)

a
{\mathcal{W}}
-category, with powers by
{\mathcal{W}}
. The power of a
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid
\mathcal{G}=s,t:G\to M
by a Weil space
E
is given by the
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid

[E,\mathcal{G}]=\{[E,s],[E,t]:[E,G]\to[E,M],\hskip 4.26773pt[E,e]:[E,M]\to[E,G]\};

(ii)

a
\mathsf{Gpd}
-enriched category with powers by small groupoids. The power of a
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid
\mathcal{G}:\mathsf{Gpd}\to{\mathcal{W}}
by a groupoid
\mathcal{H}
is the
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid

[\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}](V)=[\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}(V)]_{\mathsf{Gpd}}.

The following two
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids form the basic building block for the main result.

Example 5.4
.

(i)

Set
D:=\mathcal{Y}W
in
{\mathcal{W}}
. The discrete groupoid
D=D
represents the tangent functor internally, and the copresheaf
\mathsf{Gpd}(D,-):\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})\to{\mathcal{W}}
sends a groupoid
s,t:G\to M
to
TM
.

(ii)

The arrow groupoid is the free groupoid generated by the graph:

\bullet\to\bullet

Write the trivial
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid on this groupoid as
I
. Note that the power by
I
will send a groupoid to its “arrow groupoid”
\mathcal{G}^{\to}
, the groupoid whose objects are arrows in
\mathcal{G}
, with a map
u\to v
being a commuting square.
It follows that
\mathsf{Gpd}(I,\mathcal{G})
is the space of arrows of the groupoid,
\mathsf{Gpd}(I\times I,\mathcal{G})
the space of commuting squares, and so on.

The next proposition gives the necessary properties of
I
to construct the Lie derivative.

Lemma 5.5
.

(i)

For every groupoid
\mathcal{G}=s,t:G\to M
, there is an isomorphism

G{{}_{t}}\!\times_{t}\!G{{}_{s}}\!\times_{t}\!G\cong\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})(I\times I,\mathcal{G});

this corresponds to the unique filler for the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!w^{-1}\circ v\circ u}

(ii)

The arrow groupoid has a semigroup with a zero structure (like an infinitesimal object
D
), and the multiplication is the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\times 1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

The dual result of part
(ii)
is somewhat more obvious to see, as the following fork is always an equalizer in
{\mathcal{W}}
for a groupoid
G
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\square}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\square}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{e[s]\times I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{I\times e[s]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{m}}

(where we write
G
as the object of arrows and
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})(I\times I,G)
as
G^{\square}
).
Note that
G^{e[s]\times I}
and
G^{I\times e[s]}
correspond to the idempotents on
G^{\square}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{I\times e[s]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{e[s]\times I}}

Also note that a commuting square is equalized by these two maps if and only if
u=q=id
, which forces
w=v
; these commuting squares are exactly the image of
G^{m}
.

The semigroup structure on
I
represents the map sending

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Now look at the
\partial
defined in Proposition
3.4
, and take the same colimit in
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
.
Groupoids are monadic over reflexive graphs with an involution, so this is essentially the free groupoid over that graph (as the free functor is a left adjoint and therefore preserves colimits).

Definition 5.6
.

Define the
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoid
\partial
to be the
{\mathcal{W}}
-coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\times e[s]}

where
e=0\circ!,e[s]=i\circ s
. Note that just as in Proposition
3.4
,
\partial
is an anchored bundle.

The properties of the arrow groupoid make it possible to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 5.7
.

The object
\partial
determines a cartesian
{\mathcal{W}}
-functor

\partial(-):\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})

that is both an infinitesimal object in
{\mathcal{W}}
-groupoids and an involution algebroid in
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}
.

Proof.

Write the pushout powers of
\xi:1\to\partial
as
\partial(n)
, and for any Weil algebra
V=W_{n(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{n(k)}
, write
\partial(V)=\partial(n_{1})\times\dots\times\partial(n_{k})
. Composition for internal categories will be written in the diagramattic order, writing composition as an infix semicolon “;” to keep it distinct from composition of morphisms in
{\mathcal{W}}
.

The proof has two main steps:

1.

For every Weil algebra
V
, there is an isomorphism

\partial_{V}\cong\partial(V)

where
\partial_{V}
is the
V
-prolongation of the anchored bundle in
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})^{op}
.

2.

The universal lift for the anchored bundle, given as a coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\times\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\times\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\partial}\times\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\partial}\times\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxdot}

is a semigroup.

From (1) and (2), we may infer that the object
\partial
is an infinitesimal object. The semigroup map
\boxdot
is commutative and has a zero by (2), and satisfies all of the couniversality axioms by (1).

1.

The proof of this step follows by induction on the width (Definition
2.8
) of the Weil algebra
V
, where the cases
0,1
both hold by definition.
In the case
n=2
, we need only check this holds for
\partial\times\partial\cong\partial_{WW}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial\times\partial}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\delta,id)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(id,0)}

For any
G
, a map
X:\partial\times\partial\to G
corresponds to a commuting square in
T^{2}G
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.e\circ u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.T\circ v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

so that
T.e\circ v=id
and
e.T\circ u=id
.
It follows that
u=(e.T\circ v)^{-1};(T.e\circ u);v
(Lemma
5.5
(i)), and that precomposition with the uniquely induced map determines
(\bar{u},v):X\to G^{L(2)}
, where

T.0\circ\bar{u}=u.

Observe that any pair
(\bar{u},v):X\to G^{L(2)}
determines a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T.0\circ\bar{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e.T\circ v}

where
T.e\circ v=id
and
T.e\circ\bar{u}=id
.
Note that
T.0\circ T.p\circ T.0\circ\bar{u}=T.0\circ\bar{u}
, and that the bottom horn is
u=(e.T\circ v)^{-1};(T.0\circ\bar{u});v
; now check

\displaystyle T.e\circ u

\displaystyle=(T.e\circ e.T\circ v)^{-1};(T.e\circ T.0\circ\bar{u});(T.e\circ v)

\displaystyle=id;T.0\circ\bar{u};T.e\circ v=T.0\circ\bar{u}

and

\displaystyle e.T\circ u

\displaystyle=e.T\circ(e.T\circ v)^{-1};(T.0\circ\bar{u});v

\displaystyle=(e.T\circ e.T\circ v)^{-1};(e.T\circ T.0\circ\bar{u});(e.T\circ v)

\displaystyle=(e.T\circ v)^{-1};id;(e.T\circ v)=id

thus determining a map
X\to G^{\partial\times\partial}
.
The two maps are inverse to each other, giving an isomorphism.

For the inductive case, look at the prolongations of anchored bundles and recall that

A_{UV}\boxtimes_{M}A_{Z}=A_{UV}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{L}(U,A)}A_{UZ}

Where
A_{UV}
and
A_{UZ}
are treated as spans over
A_{U}
, so that

\displaystyle A_{U}\xleftarrow{id\boxtimes\pi^{V}}A_{UV}\xrightarrow{anc^{U}\boxtimes A_{V}}T^{U}.A_{V}

\displaystyle A_{U}\xleftarrow{id\boxtimes\pi^{Z}}A_{UZ}\xrightarrow{anc^{U}\boxtimes A_{Z}}T^{U}.A_{Z}

and observe that their span composition is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{UVZ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{UV}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}.A_{UZ}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{V}.A_{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{Z}.T^{V}.A_{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{U}\boxtimes A_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{U}.(\varrho^{U}\boxtimes A_{Z})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

So it now suffices to prove that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{U}\times D_{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{U}\times(D_{V}\times\partial_{Z})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{U}\times\partial_{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{U}\times(\partial_{V}\times\partial_{Z})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

is a pushout. But by the inductive hypothesis,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{V}\times\partial_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{V}\times\partial_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

so the result follows by the cocontinuity of
\partial_{U}\times(-)
, and
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
is a cartesian closed category (so
X\times(-)
is cocontinuous).

2.

Recall that the fork

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e[s]\times I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times e[s]}

is a coequalizer.
Also note that
e^{\partial}
is the coequalizer of
e,e[s]
. The commutativity of colimits then ensures that there is a multiplication map induced by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D\times I)^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D\times I)^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D\times I)^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(D\times I)^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\times I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Thus the multplication is associative, is commutative, and has a zero given by
1\xrightarrow{0\times s}D\times I
.

∎

Aan immediate corollary of Theorem
5.7
is that the
\partial
determines a nerve/ realization where the nerve factors through the category of involution algebroids.
This puts the Lie functor into a nerve/realization context (recall Definition
3.5
).

Definition 5.8
.

The
Lie realization
is the left Kan extension

|-|_{\partial}=Lan_{\partial}:\mathsf{Inv}({\mathcal{W}})\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}}).

This functor is well behaved. First, ote that it preserved products:

Lemma 5.9
.

The realization functor preserves products.

Proof.

Product preservation is a consequence of
|-|_{\partial}
being the left Kan extension of a cartesian functor along a cartesian functor (
Day and Street 1995
).
Note that this implies
\int^{v}\partial(v)=1
.
∎

Next, we see that the realization of an involution algebroid has the same base space.

Lemma 5.10
.

The base space of the groupoid
\partial
is
D^{v}
.

Proof.

When constructing the colimit of groupoids, the colimit’s base space is the ordinary colimit for the diagram of the base spaces. The reflector from simplicial objects to groupoids preserves products, so it suffices to check that the base space of
\partial(n)
is
D(n)
.

The base space of
I
is
1+1
, and the map
e^{s}_{0}
is given by
\delta^{-}\circ!
.
Since
D
is a discrete cubical object, its base space is
D\times 1
.
Thus the coequalizer defining
\partial
is

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

A map
\gamma:D+D\to M
is a pair of maps
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}:D\to M
.
We can see that
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
agree at
0
(they are both
\gamma_{0}(0)
), and
\gamma(0)
is a constant tangent vector. It follows that
\gamma_{1}\circ(id|id)=\gamma
.
∎

Now recall the co-Yoneda lemma: for any
\mathcal{V}
-presheaf
A:\mathbb{C}^{op}\to\mathcal{V}
,

F(C)=\int^{C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}(C,C^{\prime})\otimes F(C^{\prime}).

In particular, for an involution algebroid in
{\mathcal{W}}
(or any
{\mathcal{W}}
-presheaf on
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}
),

A(U)=\int^{V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}(U,V)\times A(V)=\int^{V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,U)\times A(V).

Lemma 5.11
.

For any involution algebroid
A
,

A(R)=\int^{V\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}A(V)\times D^{V}.

Proof.

Use the tangent structure on
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}
to regard it as a
{\mathcal{W}}
-enriched category:

\displaystyle D^{V}=\mathcal{Y}(V)=\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,-)=(U\mapsto\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,U))

\displaystyle=(U\mapsto\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,U\otimes R))=\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,R).

The following computation gives the result:

A(R)=\int^{V}\mathsf{Weil}_{1}(V,R)\times A(V)=\int^{V}D^{V}\times A(V).

∎

We can now see that the base space of an involution algebroid is isomorphic to the base space of its realization. That is to say, the realization sends an involution algebroid over a Weil space
M
to a groupoid over the Weil space
M
(up to isomorphism).

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
A
be an involution algebroid in
{\mathcal{W}}
. Then
|A|([0])=A(R)
.

Proof.

Use the Yoneda lemma, and the fact that
\mathcal{Y}[0]=1
is a small projective so that
\mathsf{Gpd}(1,-)
is
{\mathcal{W}}
-cocontinuous. Now apply Lemma
5.10
and Lemma
5.11
:

\displaystyle|A|([0])

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Gpd}(1,|A|)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Gpd}\left(1,\int^{v\in\mathsf{Weil}_{1}}A(v)\bullet\partial v\right)

\displaystyle=\int^{v}A(v)\times\mathsf{Gpd}(1,\partial v)

\displaystyle=\int^{v}A(v)\times D^{v}=A(R).

∎

Thus, as a final result, we have achieved an adjunction between the category of involution algebroids and groupoids in
{\mathcal{W}}
that is product-preserving and stable over the base spaces.

\lie

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Inv}({\mathcal{W}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Remark 5.13
.

Just as the introduction to this thesis begins with the work of Charles Ehresmann, we should take a moment to see how the Lie realization relates to his original research into sketch theory. Rather than sketches, we use their closely related cousins,
essentially algebraic theories
, which in this case are small, finitely complete
{\mathcal{W}}
-categories. We may regard
\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
and
\mathsf{Inv}({\mathcal{W}})
as
{\mathcal{W}}
-functor categories

\mathsf{Lex}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Gpd}},{\mathcal{W}}),\hskip 14.22636pt\mathsf{Inv}(\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Gpd}},{\mathcal{W}}),

respectively (where
\mathsf{Lex}
means finite-limit-preserving
{\mathcal{W}}
-functors). The functor
\partial:\mathsf{Weil}_{1}^{op}\to\mathsf{Gpd}({\mathcal{W}})
induces a left-exact
{\mathcal{W}}
-functor

\hat{\partial}:\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Inv}}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathsf{Gpd}}.

This means the functor from Lie groupoids to Lie algebroids is induced by a morphisms of essentially algebraic theories, and may thus be presented as a morphism of sketches.

Chapter 9
Conclusions and future work

1
Conclusions

We now take stock of what this thesis has accomplished.

Enriched essentially algebraic presentations of geometric structures

This thesis has been concerned with categories of vector bundles and Lie algebroids in smooth manifolds.
While these are not models of a limit sketch in the category of smooth manifolds, classical results such as the Serre–Swann theorem or Vaintrob’s presentation of Lie algebroids indicate that these categories do have some algebraic description.
By combining the results in Chapters
5
and
6
, we can see that vector bundles and Lie algebroids are characterized by tangent categorical gadgets, differential bundles (Theorem
5.1
) and involution algebroids (Theorem
5.1
).
By combining these observations with the enriched perspective on tangent categories, the results in Section
2
exhibit vector bundles and Lie algebroids as models of
enriched sketches
(Proposition
2.6
and Theorem
4.14
), as can also be found in Chapter 6 of
Kelly 2005
.
Note that from this perspective, the construction of the Weil nerve of an involution algebroid is mostly important as an intermediate step.

New tangent structures for Lie algebroids and groupoids

It is well known that the categories of Lie algebroids and groupoids have tangent structures induced by post-composition with the tangent functor, yielding the tangent algebroid and tangent groupoid, respectively.
The key result in Chapter
7
exhibiting the category of Lie algebroids as transverse-limit-preserving cartesian-tangent functors
\mathsf{Weil}_{1}\to\mathsf{SMan}
, then, gives the category of Lie algebroids a second tangent structure that corresponds to Martinez’s
prolongation
Lie algebroid.
Similarly, the construction of the infinitesimal nerve of a local groupoid restricts to a new tangent structure on the category of Lie groupoids; in this case, the base space is the Lie algebroid of the groupoid
G
, and the total space is the Lie algebroid of the arrow groupoid
G^{2}
.

While this may seem like a piece of formal category theory, it is closely related to symmetry reduction in classical mechanics using Lie theory. This goes back to Poincaré’s celebrated note
Poincaré 1901
, which introduced the
Euler-Poincare
formulation of Lagrangian mechanics on a manifold equipped with a Lie group action, where the tangent bundle is replaced with a Lie algebra action (see
Marle 2013
for a modern exposition). Poincaré’s formalism has been extended to Lie groupoids and Lie algebroids in
Weinstein 1996
and
Martínez 2001
, and the thesis
Fusca 2018
investigates fluid mechanics through this lens. This work may be interpreted as using the novel tangent structures on Lie groupoids and Lie algebroids given in Chapters
7
and
8
, and warrants further investigation.

Functorial semantics of Lie theory

The work in Section
5
puts the Lie derivative on a new formal grounding:

(i)

In the enriching category
{\mathcal{W}}
, the Lie derivative functor now arises as a nerve/realization context. This guarantees the existence of a left adjoint—the
realization
—that constructs a Lie groupoid from an algebroid. The realization preserves products and is stable over the base space.

(ii)

It is only a small extension of the work in Section
5
to show that the Lie derivative of a groupoid in a tangent category may generally be regarded as a weighted limit, where
A_{V}=\{\partial(V),G\}
for each Weil prolongation of the involution algebroid of the groupoid.

These appear to be new results, although in a nerve/realization approach they had previously appeared in Lie theory in the form of Sullivan’s construction (
Sullivan 1977
), which takes the differential graded algebra of a Lie algebroid and constructs a simplicial set:

\mathsf{DGA}^{op}(\Omega(\Delta_{n}),A)

where
\Omega(\Delta_{n})
is the de Rham cohomology of the
n
-simplex in cartesian space.

2
Future work

This section outlines various lines of research that were either cut from the thesis while writing due to time and/or space constraints, or new lines of research that the thesis-writing process has motivated but which have not yet been pursued.

Enriched sketches and Mackenzie theory

Following
Voronov 2012
, Mackenzie theory refers to the body of research developed by Kirill Mackenzie and his collaborators into Lie theory, particularly structures like double Lie algebroids (
Mackenzie 1992
), VB-Lie algebroids (
Bursztyn et al. 2016
), double Lie groupoids and so on.
Intuitively, a double Lie algebroid is a Lie algebroid in the category of Lie algebroids, while a VB-Lie algebroid is a vector bundle in the category of Lie algebroids.
These structures have an intuitive relationship with tensor products of sketches; that is, for limit sketches
A,B
there is a chain of isomorphisms of categories:

\mathsf{Mod}(A,\mathsf{Mod}(B,\mathsf{Set}))\cong\mathsf{Mod}(A\otimes B,\mathsf{Set})\cong\mathsf{Mod}(B,\mathsf{Mod}(A,\mathsf{Set})).

We have already demonstrated that the Lie algebroids and vector bundles are
{\mathcal{W}}
-sketchable, so the natural next step is to revisit Mackenzie theory via this lens. Certain results such as the symmetry of partial Lie derivatives from
Mackenzie 1992
(given a double Lie groupoid, the order in which the Lie functor is applied doesn’t matter) should be immediate.

A tangent categorical formalization of mechanics

Several papers in synthetic differential geometry have translated aspects of Lagrangian and Hamiltonian mechanics into the synthetic setting (
Bunge and Heggie 1984
;
Nishimura 1997
), and to a degree this has made it difficult to build enthusiasm for a tangent categorical presentation of mechanics. These approaches generally emphasize the ability to construct function spaces, or the use of a topos-theoretic internal language. The novel tangent structures for Lie algebroids and groupoids presented here, however, provide a new line of inquiry: to develop a unified framework for Lagrangian or Hamiltonian mechanics in a tangent category that agrees with the algebroid and groupoid approaches to mechanics.

Appendix: Background on locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-categories

\chaptermark

Appendix

Synthetic differential geometry uses a topos-theoretic framework, such as can be found in the final chapters of
Lavendhomme 1996
.
The internal language of a topos is a key tool, and models of synthetic differential geometry are constructed using sheaf constructions.
Tangent categories have an analagous toolbox, drawing from
enriched locally presentable categories
. We begin with locally presentable categories; basic material on these may be found in
Adámek and Rosický 1994
, and on enriched categories in
Kelly 2005
.
The following class of colimits is foundational in the theory of locally presentable categories.

Definition 9..1
.

A category is
filtered
whenever any finite diagram in
\mathbb{C}
has a cocone. A colimit whose diagram category is filtered is called a
filtered colimit
. Note that a category
\mathcal{D}
is filtered if and only if every colimit diagram
\mathcal{D}\to\mathsf{Set}
commutes with every finite limit in
\mathsf{Set}
.

Example 9..2
.

(i)

Any finite category
\mathcal{C}
with a terminal object is filtered: for any diagram
D:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
, the natural transformation
!:id\Rightarrow K_{1}
is a cocone via whiskering.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}

(ii)

Any category with finite colimits is filtered, because each diagram has a colimit (and therefore a cocone).

Definition 9..3
.

An object in a cocomplete
\,\mathbb{C}
is
finitely presentable
whenever
\mathbb{C}(C,-)
preserves filtered colimits. A cocomplete category
\,\mathbb{C}
is
locally finitely presentable
whenever it has a small subcategory
\,\mathbb{C}_{fp}
of finitely presentable objects so that every object in
\mathbb{C}
is given by the coend

C\cong\int^{X\in\mathbb{C}_{fp}}\mathbb{C}(X,C)\cdot X.

Example 9..4
.

In the category
\mathsf{Set}
, the subcategory of finitely presentable objects is precisely the category of finite sets.
Every set is a filtered colimit of finite sets, so that
\mathsf{Set}
is locally finitely presentable.

Intuitively, this means that every object in
\mathbb{C}
is generated by gluing together finitely presentable objects in a canonical way; hence the name locally finitely presentable categories.

The subcategory of finitely presentable objects in a cocomplete category is itself finitely cocomplete.
This follows from the fact that
\mathbb{C}(-,X):\mathbb{C}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a continuous functor (it sends colimits in
\mathbb{C}
to limits in
\mathsf{Set}
) and also that filtered colimits commute with finite limits in
\mathsf{Set}
.
Thus, for any filtered colimit of representables,

\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{col}D,\mathsf{lim}F)\cong\mathsf{lim}_{X}\mathsf{lim}_{Y}\mathbb{C}(D(X),F(Y))\cong\mathsf{lim}_{Y}\mathsf{lim}_{X}\mathbb{C}(D(X),F(Y)).

There are several ways to characterize locally finitely presentable categories; these may be found in
Adámek and Rosický 1994
.

Proposition 9..5
.

For a category
\,\mathbb{C}
the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbb{C}
is a locally finitely presentable category.

(ii)

For a small cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathbb{C}=\mathsf{Lex}(\mathcal{C},\mathsf{Set})
(where
\mathsf{Lex}
means finite-limit-preserving) and
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{C}^{op}_{fp}
.

(iii)

\mathbb{C}
is the category of models for some
limit sketch
(e.g. a small category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a class of cones
\mathcal{L}
, where a model is a functor into
\mathsf{Set}
sending cones to limit diagrams).

(iv)

\mathbb{C}
is a reflective subcategory of a presheaf topos for some small category
\mathcal{C}
(this holds tautologically for presheaf topoi).

(v)

\mathbb{C}
is a full subcategory of a locally presentable category that is closed under limits (this relies on a large cardinal axiom known as
Vopenka’s principle
).

One of the remarkable aspects of the theory of locally finitely presentable categories is that its results translate over to enrichment in a monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
without any significant changes.

Definition 9..6
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a monoidal category.
A
\mathcal{V}
-graph
\mathbb{C}
is given by a (large) set of objects
\mathbb{C}_{0}
, and a map

\mathbb{C}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}_{0}\to\mathcal{V}.

Given a collection of maps

m_{ABC}:\mathbb{C}(B,C)\times\mathbb{C}(A,B)\to\mathbb{C}(A,C),\hskip 7.11317ptj_{A}:I\to\mathbb{C}(A,A)

we say
\,\mathbb{C}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-category whenever the following associativity and unitality diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,D)\otimes\mathbb{C}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{C}(C,D)\otimes\mathbb{C}(B,C))\otimes\mathbb{C}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(C,D)\otimes(\mathbb{C}(B,C)\otimes\mathbb{C}(A,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(C,D)\otimes\mathbb{C}(A,C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes m}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\otimes\mathbb{C}(B,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,B)\otimes\mathbb{C}(B,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)\otimes\mathbb{C}(C,C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Examples of enrichment abound throughout category theory.

Example 9..7
.

(i)

Any category with biproducts is enriched in the category of commutative monoids.

(ii)

A symmetric monoidal closed category is self-enriched using the internal hom
[-,-]
.

(iii)

A 2-category is a category enriched in
\mathsf{Cat}
.

(iv)

The unit
\mathcal{V}
category is the 1-object
\mathcal{V}
category whose hom-object is
I
.

(v)

When
\mathcal{V}
is symmetric monoidal, we can construct the opposite
\mathcal{V}
-category of
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{op}(A,B)=\mathbb{C}(B,A)
.
The new composition is defined by

m^{op}:\mathbb{C}^{op}(B,C)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{op}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbb{C}(B,A)\otimes\mathbb{C}(C,B)\xrightarrow{m}\mathbb{C}(C,A)=\mathbb{C}^{op}(A,C)

There is a 2-category of
\mathcal{V}
-categories for any
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 9..8
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-functor
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is an assignment on objects
F_{0}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
and a collection of maps
F_{A,B}:\mathbb{C}(A,B)\to\mathbb{D}(F_{0}A,F_{0}B)
so that the following diagrams are satisfied:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)\otimes\mathbb{C}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}B,F_{0}C)\otimes\mathbb{D}(F_{0}A,F_{0}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(A,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}A,F_{0}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}A,F_{0}A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\otimes F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{C}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{D}_{F_{0}A}}

A
\mathcal{V}
-natural transformation
F\Rightarrow G
is a collection of maps
\gamma_{A}:I\to\mathbb{D}(F_{0}A,G_{0}A)
so that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}B,F_{0}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}B,F_{0}C)\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(F_{0}B,F_{0}C)\otimes\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,F_{0}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{C}(B,C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,F_{0}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,G_{0}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\otimes\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,G_{0}C)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,F_{0}B)\otimes\mathbb{D}(G_{0}B,G_{0}C)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\gamma.B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma.C\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

\mathcal{V}\mathsf{Cat}
is the 2-category of
\mathcal{V}
-categories,
\mathcal{V}
-functors and
\mathcal{V}
-natural transformations.

Enriched categories have a richer notion of (co)limit than ordinary categories.
A limit for a functor is defined as a universal cone; that is, a right Kan extension along
!
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

so that the universal property

\forall C:\hskip 4.26773pt\mathbb{C}(C,\lim F)\cong[\mathbb{C},\mathsf{Set}](K_{C},F-)

is satisfied.
Dually, a colimit is a universal cocone, and so a left Kan extension:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

where the couniversal property

\forall C:\hskip 4.26773pt[\mathbb{C}^{op},\mathsf{Set}](F-,K_{C})\cong\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{colim}F,C)

is satisfied.
This does not translate over to
\mathcal{V}
-categories, for a few reasons.
An obvious one is that
\mathcal{V}
need not be cartesian monoidal, so there is no reason a
\mathcal{V}
-category should have a functor
\mathbb{C}\to 1
.
The notion of a
weighted
colimit is more appropriate:

Definition 9..9
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
\mathcal{V}
-category, and call
W:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{V}
the weight and
D:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{C}
the diagram.
The limit of
D
weighted by
W
, or the weighted limit
\{W,\mathcal{D}\}
, is an object satisfying the following universal property:

\forall C:\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{V}(W,[\mathbb{C},\mathcal{V}](F-,K_{C}))\cong\mathbb{C}({F,W},C).

Dually, given a diagram
P:\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathbb{C}
, the colimit of
P
weighted by
W
,
W*P
, is an object in
\mathbb{C}
satisfying the universal property

\forall C:\hskip 4.26773pt\mathcal{V}(W,[\mathbb{C},\mathcal{V}](K_{C},F-))\cong\mathbb{C}(C,F*W).

Example 9..10
.

(i)

A conical (co)limit is weighted by the constant functor into the unit
I
,
K_{I}:\mathcal{D}\to w
.
This coincides with the definition of ordinary (co)limits.

(ii)

The power of
C
by an object
V\in\mathcal{V}
is an object
C^{V}
satisfying the universal property that

\mathbb{C}(B,C^{V})\cong\mathcal{V}(V,\mathbb{C}(B,C)).

Dually, the copower by
V
is given by

\mathbb{C}(B\bullet V,C)\cong\mathcal{V}(V,\mathbb{C}(B,C)).

(iii)

In ordinary categories, the power of an object
C
by
V
is given by the (possibly infinitary)
V
-indexed coproduct:

C^{V}\cong\coprod_{v\in V}C

whereas the copower is the possibly infinitary
V
-index product of
C
:

V\bullet C\cong\prod_{v\in V}C.

(iv)

In a symmetric monoidal closed category
\mathcal{V}
, the power by
\mathcal{V}
is given by
[V,-]
and the copower by
V\otimes\_
.

Following Kelly’s work, we observe that every finite weighted limit is composed of powers and conical limits.

Proposition 9..11
.

Every weighted (co)limit is the composition of conical (co)limits and (co)powers.

Corollary 9..12
.

Weighted limits are exactly conical/ordinary limits in ordinary categories.

The previous definitions relating to locally presentable categories transfer over mostly unchanged.

Definition 9..13
.

(i)

A monoidal closed
\mathcal{V}
is
presentable as a closed category
if
\mathcal{V}_{fp}
is a monoidal subcategory.

(ii)

Given
\mathcal{V}
as in (i), a weight
W:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{V}
is
filtered
whenever
W\star(-):\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\to\mathcal{V}
preserves finite limits.

(iii)

An object in a cocomplete
\mathcal{V}
category is
finitely presentable
when
\mathbb{C}(C,-)
preserves filtered weighted colimits.

(iv)

A cocomplete
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{C}
is
locally finitely presentable
whenever every object
C
is the coend

C\cong\int^{X\in\mathbb{C}_{fp}}\mathbb{C}(X,C)\cdot X.

Proposition 9..14

(
Freyd and Kelly 1972
;
Kelly 2005
)
.

All of the previous results about locally presentable categories lift up to locally presentable
\mathcal{V}
-categories, provided that
\mathcal{V}
is presentable as a closed category.
